<<<PDFPAGE 11>>>
Preface 
I 
n writing this short history of the British occupation of Sicily during the 
war against Napoleon, I lay no claim to originality. My debt to the two 
perceptive books by Dr. John Rosselli on Lord William Bentinck and to Dr. 
Piers Mackesy’s masterly history of the war in the Mediterranean from 1803 
to 1810 are, I need hardly add, enormous. I am also much indebted to the 
writings of Antonio Capograssi, Denis Mack Smith, Jacques Rambaud, 
Charles Auriol, R. M. Johnston, Angela Valente, and Harold Acton, though 
I have naturally had recourse to those primary sources available in this 
country. 
But my aim has been to try to synthesize the findings and conclusions of 
others, in works in English, French, and Italian, and to attempt to answer 
these questions—what were the British doing in Sicily, and did they on the 
whole succeed in achieving what they set out to do there?

Acknowledgments 
M’ thanks are due to the Department of Manuscripts at Nottingham 
University and to the trustees of the Duke of Portland for permission 
to include material from the journals of Lord William Bentinck and of Joseph 
Smith, and from the correspondence of Robert Fagan. I must also thank the 
Department of Manuscripts at the British Library for permission to quote 
from the Collingwood, Annesley, Hudson Lowe, Bunbury, Wellesley, Wind- 
ham, Liverpool, Heytesbury, Fagan, and Pasley manuscripts and from the 
papers of Sir Alexander Ball. My thanks are also due to the National Library 
of Scotland for allowing me to see a Lynedoch manuscript and to the 
National Maritime Museum at Greenwich for permission to inspect letters 
from Captain Ball to Nelson. Any transcripts of Crown copyright records 
appear by permission of the Controller of Her Majesty’s Stationery Office. 
I also wish to thank Mrs. Joan Streatfield for permission to include quota- 
tions from the unpublished letters of Lady Dalrymple, Dr. John Rosselli for 
his guidance on several points, and Professor Francesco Brancato for some 
helpful suggestions on reading matter. 
Finally I must thank Mr. Anthony Seymour for his help in recommending 
sources and for having made useful recommendations after kindly reading the 
first draft of this book.

<<<PDFPAGE 15>>>
Introduction 
J esate the whole of the period from 1793 to 1815 when Britain was 
at war almost continuously with the French republic and Napoleon’s 
empire, she contrived to maintain one faithful ally—though one faithful 
sometimes only in name, and usually more from need than sentiment— 
Ferdinand, king of Naples and Sicily. 
Those two kingdoms, with their long coastlines and their harbors both on 
the Adriatic and on the Tyrrhenian seas, required the help of a maritime 
power to preserve their independence from France. But they also needed a 
military power of the stature of Russia or Austria. Britain could provide them 
with naval protection, but never with troops in sufficient numbers to with- 
stand the might of the armies of France, and if Russia or Austria or both 
failed to come to their assistance or made a separate peace with France, all 
that Britain was able to do was to carry the court to the safety of Sicily and 
thereafter safeguard that island by covering it with a naval squadron and 
stationing a garrison there. 
The value of the Neapolitan-Sicilian alliance to the British fleet was consid- 
erable—the kingdoms’ ports supplied the fleet with winter bases, fresh water 
and food, and during the war of the first coalition, Naples contributed 
battleships and small craft. Economically, the alliance was important in 
weakening Napoleon’s stranglehold on British trade with the continent of 
Europe, and militarily, it provided a base for the launching of offensive 
operations in both eastern and western Mediterranean. 
What King Ferdinand wanted from his ally Britain was a guarantee that his 
mainland kingdom (Naples), lost to France in 1806, would be recovered and 
returned to his rule at the earliest possible opportunity, and at the latest in a 
general peace settlement. This Britain was unable to give, although she was 
willing to guarantee him Sicily. This failure on the part of Great Britain, 
suspicions (fully justified) that Britain intended to ignore the claims of King 
Ferdinand to the sovereignty of Malta, and suspicions (that were in fact not 
justified, though the reason for them was understandable) that Britain in- 
tended to take over Sicily and turn it into a permanent protectorate, all put a 
strain on the alliance. The utter dependence of the court at Palermo on 
British support from 1806 onward was humiliating for the king and queen, 
who came to detest, if not Great Britain, at least most of its soldiers and 
diplomats. Britain, for her part, as the war continued, became increasingly 
11 

<<<PDFPAGE 16>>>
2 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
uneasy about the loyalty of the court at Palermo, and finally came to exercise a 
virtual dictatorship over Sicily. In the end it was an Austrian army that 
brought the king back to his capital after the final defeat of Napoleon and 
enabled him to free himself from his ten-year-old yoke of dependence on 
Britain. Throughout that period Britain alone prevented the French from 
taking Sicily, as she had already for a brief time done in the early months of 
1799. Britain in her turn gained enormously from being able to use the 
island in her battle to keep the Mediterranean from turning into a Gallic sea, 
but she was in the end to find that Sicily became almost more of a liability 
than an asset, as it involved her in political obligations she found it difficult to 
discharge.

<<<PDFPAGE 19>>>
1 
Getting a Foothold 
bw the greater part of the war she fought with revolutionary and 
Napoleonic France, Britain contrived to maintain a fleet in being in the 
Mediterranean, supplies for which were largely drawn from the ports of the 
Neapolitan kingdom, with which was included the kingdom of Sicily. In 
1793 and 1798 Britain concluded treaties with Naples, entered into a secret 
agreement with the government of Naples in 1803, and signed further 
treaties with that government in Sicily in 1808, 1809, and 1812, all of which 
conceded to Britain the use of the harbors of the two kingdoms and from 
1806, by necessity, those which were situated in Sicily only. The British 
preoccupation with Sicily, as indeed with nearby Malta, arose not only from 
her concern to keep her naval squadrons provisioned, but also from her 
realization of Napoleon’s concern to extend his rule beyond Italy to the 
Levant. It requires no very deep grasp of strategy to see, from the briefest 
glance at a map, Sicily’s importance to any naval power that sought to 
dominate the Mediterranean. When in the spring of 1793 Pitt decided, once 
France had declared war, to send a strong fleet to the Mediterranean, he 
hoped that the Italian states would join in opposing the general threat the 
French revolutionary government posed. Naples, which had the most to 
offer so far as naval help was concerned, was hesitant to commit herself until 
a British fleet had appeared, having been earlier forced by a French one into 
declaring an unwilling neutrality. However, by the middle of July, a treaty of 
alliance with Britain was signed, and ships, troops, and sailors were promised 
in return for British naval protection. Neapolitan troops and ships cooper- 
ated in the siege of Toulon and only a serious revolt in Naples, led by 
sympathizers with the Jacobin cause, prevented serious help being given to 
the British in their, assault on Corsica. However, by 1795 four Neapolitan 
ships-of-the-line were successfully serving with the British fleet and, a year 
later, a squadron of gunboats; and when Napoleon seized Leghorn, the main 
supply base for Admiral Jervis, the ports of Naples gave vital assistance in 
providing all that the admiral required. 
When the decision was'made by Pitt at the end of 1796 to withdraw the 
fleet from the Mediterranean and the Austrian forces in northern Italy were 
driven out by Napoleon’s army, Naples hastened to make peace with the

<<<PDFPAGE 21>>>
Getting a Foothold 
NW 
French and agreed to revert to her neutral status, though she was still allowed 
to admit to her ports, on the mainland and Sicily, British ships. 
By 1798, however, the court of Naples grew so alarmed at the renewed 
threat of French aggression that it made appeals to Austria and Britain to 
come to its help if it were attacked and begged the British government to 
allow some warships to protect its coast. General Berthier in Rome, where 
the French army was now established, was making demands on the king of 
Naples that seemed a prelude to occupation, while naval preparations by the 
French in Toulon, Genoa, and Civita Vecchia were seen to presage an attack 
on Sicily.* The British government at this time was trying to rebuild the 
allied front that had broken down in the past three years, and, as Grenville the 
secretary of state wrote on 16 January 1798, Europe could be saved from 
French domination only by the union of the four Great Powers. 
Austria expressed her willingness to assist in defending Naples from attack, 
but she pressed Britain to dispatch, at once, a fleet to the Mediterranean. 
Grenville, who had persuaded Pitt that this was the right course to adopt, 
informed Naples that a squadron under Nelson was shortly to arrive in the 
Mediterranean. But it was essential that all the king’s ports be free to admit 
this naval force and allow it to buy what supplies it needed; and indeed if this 
request was not met, the fleet would be ordered to leave at once.# 
When Nelson’s squadron arrived off Naples on 24 June 1798 the attitude 
of the Naples government to granting these terms remained equivocal. It was 
divided into two factions, that of the foreign minister de Gallo, which did not 
wish to antagonize France, and that of Acton, who favored Britain. But in 
any case it was most unwilling to commit itself to a course of action that 
might give rise to a French attack until it was sure of Austrian support. The 
treaty that Austria had signed with Naples on 20 May 1798 was purely 
defensive in its terms, and not until 16 July was there added to it a clause that 
provided for Austrian military help, should Naples find herself under attack 
for opening her ports to the British fleet.* 
Nelson’s plea for the help of frigates was understandably turned down, and 
his request for the opening of ports to the whole of his fleet was also refused, 
albeit only officially—the terms of neutrality only allowing three to four 
warships at any one time to be given entrance to a neutral port. Unofficially, 
however, Nelson did obtain the agreement he sought, and Acton secretly 
signed, for the king‘ a letter instructing the governors of ports to permit free 
access to Nelson’s fleet, see that it was supplied with provisions, and allow its 
sick and wounded ashore. Even so, this private letter was not dispatched to 
the governors at once, and Nelson later complained that the governor of 
Syracuse was ignorant of these terms. However, his fleet was fully provi- 
sioned when he called at Syracuse on 20 July in pursuit of the French fleet 
bound for Eygpt, and had it not been, he would have been forced to return to 
Gibraltar for food and water. The government at Naples had of course been 

<<<PDFPAGE 22>>>
18 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
The Straits of Messina with HMS Foudroyant, painted by Pocock, engraved by 
Hall. (Courtesy of the National Maritime Museum, London.) 
waiting for the ratification of the Austrian treaty, with its vital clause affecting 
the ports, and Hamilton had to explain this to Nelson when he wrote to 
assure him that all was now well.© 
In any event, Nelson’s crushing victory at Aboukir Bay a few weeks later 
swung Naples firmly behind the British—her government already alarmed at 
the French occupation of the island of Malta. The welcome Nelson received 
from the court on returning to Naples was quite ecstatic and convinced, as he 
wrote, he had saved Sicily “in spite of Neapolitan councils,” he urged the 
king and queen to attack and drive the French out of Italy. Sir William 
Hamilton, the British minister, also favored this rash course of action, while 
the queen hoped to force Austria’s hand into making a full-scale offensive 
alliance, afraid that the emperor might otherwise come to terms with Napo- 
leon and abandon the cause of the smaller powers.” The decision that was 
made proved disastrous for Naples. Her army, which had advanced to 
capture Rome, was totally routed by the French, its incompetent Austrian 
commander Mack escaping south as best he could, its officers deserting, and 
its troops dispersing. Grenville, too late, had counselled caution, convinced 
that Naples should not risk going to war until sure of Austrian military 
support. Austria was extremely angry, having made clear five days before the 
advance on Rome was actually begun that she very strongly disapproved of 
Naples taking aggressive action. She also blamed the British government for 
trying to involve her in a war for which she was insufficiently prepared.8 
Utterly dismayed at what had happened, the king, the queen, and the royal 
family took refuge on board Nelson’s flagship Vanguard and, during the 
fourth week in December, sailed into temporary exile in Sicily. The people of 
Naples had begged the king to stay and fight it out with the French, but he 

<<<PDFPAGE 23>>>
Getting a Foothold 
19 
was unwilling to take the risk. Nelson also took away to Palermo two 
Neapolitan ships-of-the-line, but the rest of that navy having no crews were 
burned in the bay, on Nelson’s orders, so that they should not fall into 
French hands—three ships-of-the-line, a frigate, a corvette, together with 
some smaller vessels.? The kingdom of Naples, though not of Sicily, thus 
passed under the control of the French, and it was not long before they had 
set up a so-called Parthenopean republic, constructed on the best revolution- 
ary model and with the active cooperation of members of the Neapolitan 
nobility. 
The welcome the king received in Palermo from his subjects in Sicily was 
warm enough, though the latter detested the Neapolitans. “Down with the 
cowardly and treacherous Neapolitans” was a cry that echoed in the streets, 
and one that much offended the queen. But clearly concessions had to be 
made to the real strength of popular feeling (one with which Nelson was 
much in sympathy, writing that he thought a proud people had every right to 
be consulted in matters concerning their own defense). In consequence the 
king agreed to two Sicilian noblemen (Prince Trabia and Prince Cassaro) 
being given ministerial posts, though neither proved to be capable. But if the 
Sicilians loathed the Neapolitans, they detested the French as much, if not 
more. French émigrés who had fled from Naples on board British ships were 
refused admission and had to be sent away to Trieste; while a shipload of sick 
and wounded French soldiers, brought away from the army of Egypt and put 
ashore at the port of Augusta, were mostly murdered in cold blood by the 
fanatical local inhabitants.!° 
Meanwhile the second coalition, financed by Britain, was taking shape, and 
a month before the king left Naples his government signed a treaty with 
Russia. The latter was much concerned to prevent any further expansion of 
the power of France in the eastern Mediterranean—an expansion that threat- 
ened the Turkish Empire—and also concerned to prevent the British from 
dominating the Mediterranean. The Russians looked at the fate of Malta and 
of the Ionian islands (both of which Napoleon had seized) and for this reason 
they could not endure a French occupation of the kingdom of Naples or a 
French threat to the kingdom of Sicily. Seen from the point of view of Naples 
(or rather Palermo, for the time being), a Russian offer of military support, 
which neither Austria nor Britain could make, seemed to have many advan- 
tages; in fact the 155000 troops that the queen had fondly imagined landing 
from Russian transports to liberate Naples were a figment of her imagination. 
The only force the Russians provided was a small force of 600 marines from a 
warship anchored off Manfredonia."? 
Nelson was personally very hostile to the presence of Russian ships in the 
area, though the British government welcomed it and reproved him later for 
taking this stance. Nelson thought the Russians had their eyes on Malta, and 
when Admiral Ushakov, who had arrived with his fleet off Palermo on 1 

<<<PDFPAGE 24>>>
20 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
September 1799, agreed to assist in the bay of Naples, Nelson at once 
dispatched Troubridge’s squadron to seize Ponza, Ischia, and Procida, en- 
force a blockade of the port of Naples, and finally land a force of marines and 
contingents of sailors from British warships to carry the war to the Roman 
states. But by that time Admiral Ushakov’s fleet had been recalled to the 
Black Sea ports. 
!2 
Whatever the reasons (and the major one was his notorious attachment to 
Lady Hamilton), Nelson retained his ships off Sicily through 1799 to 1800, 
telling Ball, besieging Valletta, that he considered Sicily to be under Britain’s 
special protection. His attention had already been drawn to the vulnerability 
of Messina, which he rightly saw as the key to Sicily. If the French were to 
march into Lower Calabria, they had only to cross a strip of water measuring 
at its narrowest point only a mile and a half across. Much depended on the 
strength of the fortress and the loyalty of the Messina garrison, and the queen 
had learnt that Danero, its commander, was neither competent nor even 
loyal, though this information was certainly false. What was true, and what 
worried Danero, was the feebleness of the garrison, made up as it was of 
deserters, criminals, vagabonds, and conscripted Calabresi. Nelson told 
Troubridge to inspect the defenses and, if need be, land 300 marines, but he 
changed his mind on the queen’s intervention. Instead he transported 500 
picked troops from the royal army at Palermo and put them into Messina 
citadel, but this he considered a temporary measure.!3 He had in the middle 
of February written to General Sir Charles Stuart, commanding the British 
troops in Minorca (a place whose surrender he had recently taken), making it 
clear that a British force was badly needed in Sicily if the island was not to fall 
to the French. “What a state we are in here,” Nelson had written, “with the 
enemy at the door and no troops” [to depend on]. The 4,000 Neapolitan 
troops were totally untrustworthy, while a force of 13,000 Sicilians was still 
in the process of being raised. Although Messina was a strong fortress, able to 
withstand a long siege, treachery would probably yield it without ever a shot 
being fired. Only 1,000 British troops would suffice to make the island secure 
and Stuart must surely see the need for them.14 Hamilton wrote in the same 
vein to his old friend Colonel Graham, then commanding a regiment in 
Minorca, and to Grenville, the foreign secretary in London. The Sicilians, he 
thought, might hate the French, but there were enough causes for discontent 
in towns like Catania, Milazzo, and Trapani (exploited no doubt by Jacobin 
agents) to have already provoked disturbances; while the garrison at Messina 
was so distrusted, it was being replaced by troops from Palermo. If action 
was not soon forthcoming to draw the French off in some other direction, 
the royal family would again be forced to take refuge on board HMS 
Vanguard, Nelson’s flagship that had earlier borne them to safety in Sicily.15 
Graham responded sympathetically. Were Sicily to fall, he told Hamilton, not 
only Malta but also Egypt would remain firmly in enemy hands. Though

Stuart had no large force to dispose of, Graham was sure that it needed only a 
small detachment to “do the trick.”!© 
Stuart required no further urging. By the end of March he had crossed to 
Sicily and installed two weak regiments in Messina citadel, to the obvious 
delight of Nelson and Hamilton and of Ferdinand and his queen, who 
ordered the governor and troops at Messina henceforth to take their orders 
from Stuart. Stuart was able to dismiss the garrison of hated Neapolitan 
troops and to supplement his British force by enlisting 2,000 Sicilian re- 
cruits. In May, Colonel Graham was sent from Minorca to take over com- 
mand of this force. He was even offered a seat on the council of the 
governing body of Messina, but he had to decline because the duties pre- 
scribed to him were purely military. Graham and the two British regiments 
remained in Messina until December, by which time the danger had long 
receded, and they were then sent to Malta to join Captain Ball and assist him 
in the siege of Valletta.17 
There had been an attempt by the queen and Nelson, backed by Sir 
William Hamilton, when Graham first arrived at Palermo to assume com- 
mand of Messina garrison, to persuade him to take the British troops there to 
help in the reconquest of Naples, Calabria being by that time free of any 
likely threat from the French. Graham replied he would have to see what 

<<<PDFPAGE 27>>>
Getting a Foothold 
23 
troops he could spare when he got to Messina, and on arriving there he made 
it clear that the British must all remain in the citadel. In this he received the 
full support of the officer commanding the troops in Minorca, General Sir 
James St. Clair Erskine. Later attempts were made that year, this time by 
Nelson, not the court, to get Graham to send his troops to help in the long 
drawn-out siege of Valletta. Nelson visited Erskine personally to try to get 
him to let the troops go, but again he met with no success. It was only when 
Erskine was replaced by Fox that orders arrived to let Graham depart, 
together with his Messina garrison. 
18 
Stuart, when writing to Henry Dundas, the secretary of state for war, 
insisted that it had not simply been the urgent requests from Nelson and 
others that had prompted him to take the action he had, but a realization that 
possession of Sicily could help the cause of the allies in Italy by keeping 
French forces pinned down in Naples, and help also in the capture of Malta. 
It could also, he added, be a useful base for future offensive operations, either 
in Egypt or Italy.!? Stuart was right, and once Britain took Malta, Sicily 
became of great importance as a major source of supply for that island, which 
needed its cattle, grain, timber and wine, although the cattle and grain were 
not always forthcoming. And as the war with Napoleon continued into the 
nineteenth century and Britain and France became involved in a struggle of 
economic attrition, Sicily became like Malta an entrepdt for smuggling goods 
into Napoleonic Europe. Because Neapolitan customs officials were corrupt, 
almost without exception, communications between Sicily and the mainland 
were never broken throughout the war. The ports of Sicily, moreover, 
remained vital to the British fleet in its task of watching the French in 
Toulon, as well as for keeping open the trade routes from west to east in the 
Mediterranean.?° 
Apart from the naval and military protection afforded King Ferdinand by 
the treaty with Britain, which was signed in December 1798, an ex gratia 
payment of £100,000 was made by the British government in March to help 
him recover Calabria and Naples.?! Thus financed, his patriot army under the 
command of Cardinal Ruffo succeeded in overrunning Calabria and, by mid- 
June, had retaken Naples. And when news of Russian victories against their 
armies in the north arrived, the French had already withdrawn from the city. 
On the overthrow of the Parthenopian Republic, which had owed its 
existence to French mhilitary power, Ferdinand, with the assistance of Nelson 
whose services were rewarded with the duchy of Bronte and the very active 
support of the queen, took a terrible revenge on his disloyal subjects—one 
that was not forgotten in Naples and was to cast a lingering shadow over all 
later attempts the court was to make, when it was once more in exile in Sicily, 
to enlist the active support of those who had been its subjects on the 
mainland of Italy. 
The British government was most anxious that the king himself should 

<<<PDFPAGE 28>>>
24 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
return to Naples at the earliest possible opportunity, since it looked on the 
state of disorder there with a sense of continuing and growing disquiet. But 
the king was not eager to return, at any rate immediately, afraid of possible 
threats to his life and the lives of the royal family. While he had been exacting 
vengeance on his Neapolitan subjects, he had never set foot on the soil of 
Naples, preferring to address his orders from the safety of the cabin of 
Nelson’s flagship. 
Troubridge had written in a letter to Nelson of the appalling state of 
corruption, oppression, and economic stagnation he had encountered in the 
kingdom of Naples, and of the need for an act of oblivion in regard to the 
thousands who were being held for trial. And Sir Arthur Paget, the young 
British minister who succeeded Hamilton at Palermo but who had called in 
at Naples en route, made the same kind of report to Grenville. In pressing the 
court for the king’s swift return and pressing the need for reform of the 
government, Paget made himself very unpopular. The queen called him “the 
detested Paget” and complained in a letter she wrote to de Gallo of Paget’s 
hard and abrupt manner—“1t 1s clear that Paget wants us to feel all the weight 
of his country’s power.” Acton greeted Paget’s suggestion that the amnesty 
granted by the king on 31 May 1800 would have much more effect if the 
king in person were to supervise its enactment with what Paget described as a 
“degree of ill-humour.” Acton told Paget the king had a right to be free of all 
outside interference in running his kingdoms? internal affairs, and that he 
must be free to choose how long he spent in either Naples or Sicily. Paget 
thought that the queen’s opposition to the speedy return of the king arose 
more from pique than from principle, stemming, he believed, from the 
“effect of their own humiliating and dependent position.” “The queen has 
been taught to believe that I was sent here to dictate, to use haughty language 
upon the subject, at which idea . 
. 
. she was violently irritated.”22 
Paget was convinced, as he told Grenville, that nothing good or useful 
could be done in Sicily without some sort of direct intervention, and “the 
more I reflect upon it the more despondent I feel.” As Harold Acton 
remarked, in his book on the subject of the Bourbons of Naples, this sounds 
like the voice of Lord William Bentinck speaking fourteen years before his 
cime:+3 
Napoleon’s victory at Marengo and Russia’s defection from the allied cause 
meant that Naples was once again threatened by invasion from a French 
army. The king begged Britain to send him troops, but Paget had to tell 
Ferdinand that only Austria could help him. The British government was in 
no position to send more than 15,000 men, and such numbers would be of 
little avail against the armies France could command. Any assistance Britain 
could give would have to be purely maritime.2# When four months later at 
Lunéville Austria made a separate peace without any reference to her allies, 
the British government advised Ferdinand to make the best terms he could 

<<<PDFPAGE 29>>>

25 
Sir ‘Arthior Paget by Count LOrsays “Gbvedey of the National Portrait Gallery, 
London.) 
with France, disregarding any engagement he might previously have made 
with Britain. If by such terms he was forced to exclude British ships from his 
kingdoms’ harbors, this would be regarded by Britain as an act of necessity, 
not hostility. 25 With this advice, and in view of the fact that the French were 
preparing to land in Sicily where there was no longer a British garrison, the 
king, who was totally unprepared to put up resistance in the island,?¢ 
hastened to make a separate peace. 
In March 1801 King Ferdinand signed the Treaty of Florence with Napo- 
leon by which he was permitted to recover his kingdom, shorn only of its 
Elban and Tuscan adjuncts; provided he maintained a strict neutrality and 
agreed to accept, feed, and pay for a French army of occupation, until a 
general peace should be signed. Not until then did he return to Naples. Sicily 
however remained exposed, and only the news that Addington’s ministry had 

<<<PDFPAGE 30>>>
26 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
finally signed the Treaty of Amiens prevented the French from seizing Mes- 
sina.?7 
By the Treaty of Amiens Britain agreed to leave Malta within three months 
and to hand it back to the Knights of St. John. But before leaving she was 
naturally anxious to ensure that the island remain friendly and capable of 
defending itself. To this end she persuaded Ferdinand to dispatch 2,000 of 
his troops to Malta, secretly agreeing to pay their cost to the tune of 
£50,000. Naples claimed sovereignty over Malta—a claim dating back to the 
sixteenth century—and King Ferdinand had been bitterly offended at the way 
by which in August 1800, after the French in Valletta had surrendered to the 
British, General Pigot had not allowed the hoisting of the Neapolitan flag 
beside that of the Knights of St. John. Ferdinand had also taken it ill when in 
January 1801 Malta had been declared a British protectorate without his 
even being consulted.?8 Now that it was to be handed back under the Treaty 
of Amiens, it was important to both Britain and Naples that it should not 
again fall prey to the French. Paget tried to impress on Ferdinand that the 
independence of Sicily could not be preserved if Malta was lost, and that as 
long as Malta was friendly or neutral, the island of Sicily could be de- 
fended.?? When it rapidly became apparent that, in view of Napoleons’ 
provocative actions, Britain intended to keep hold of Malta, whatever might 
have been said in the treaty, Napoleon made approaches to Ferdinand for a 
military alliance and the joint seizure of Malta. These approaches were 
tactfully rejected on the grounds that his kingdoms were too exhausted to 
take part in fresh hostilities, and that war with Britain (repugnant in any case, 
since that country had been a faithful ally) would only result in Naples being 
blockaded and her population reduced to starvation.2° 
When in May 1803 Britain renewed the war with Napoleon, the latter sent 
an army once again to occupy the coasts of Apulia and Abruzzi within the 
confines of the kingdom of Naples. Not unnaturally this resulted in renewed 
tension between Naples and Napoleon, the former tacitly supporting Britain, 
bitterly resenting the French occupation, yet desperately anxious to maintain 
its neutrality. Sir John Acton, the British-born minister who still carried the 
most weight with the king and who really directed royal policy, made secret 
approaches to the British, very soon after war broke out. He urged them to 
occupy Messina and other ports in Sicily, if the French army of occupation 
showed signs of moving out of its bases. “We shall protest against the 
measure to save appearances,” he told A’Court, the British chargé d’affaires in 
Naples, and if the French then attacked Sicily, Neapolitan forces would help 
to defend it.+! A°’Court thought the French were still so dispersed that no 
immediate danger threatened, but he said he would inform Admiral Bicker- 
ton and the British governor of Malta (Ball) if the French started to concen- 
trate, so that appropriate action could be taken.32 
Nelson, who was bringing out the new minister, Hugh Elliot, to the court 

<<<PDFPAGE 31>>>
Getting a Foothold 
27 
of Naples, had orders from Addington to act vigorously if the French tried to 
attack Sicily and to take good care of the royal family whom George II 
considered to be “the most faithful allies he ever had.” Colonel Villettes, the 
officer who commanded the British garrison in Malta, had also received 
orders from London to provide as many as 2,000 troops (if he could spare 
them) to seize Messina, should Nelson decide it was necessary. Nelson, while 
fully appreciating the reservations of the king and queen, who had no wish to 
risk having to flee through premature action by the British in Sicily, urged 
Acton to take no risks. “Though Naples be lost, Sicily is secure; but if Sicily is 
lost so is Naples.”33 Nelson also impressed on Elliot the necessity for speedy 
action, if it looked as if Sicily would be attacked: “In a small degree we risk it 
every day, in hopes of preserving the kingdom of Naples.” The danger, as he 
saw it, was that the enemy could ferry over some thousands of men in a very 
short time, if they had the boats. And no navy in Europe could stop them, 
with the current running at seven knots.34 
The trouble was, as Nelson realized, the very real fears of the king and 
queen that they might provoke Napoleon, who, deeply suspicious of the 
court of Naples, was shortly to demand and procure the dismissal of Acton 
on account of that statesman’s notorious Anglophilism, the dismissal of the 
émigré French Count Damas (in command of the Neapolitan army) and even 
to demand, though unsuccessfully, the removal of Elliot from his post as 
minister. The queen was genuinely frightened that Nelson and Elliot be- 
tween them might involve Naples again in a war. She wrote in September 
1803, “It will be a real miracle of St. Januarius if, caught between Nelson and 
Elliot, we are not dragged into some adventure which will reduce us to the 
situation of the king of Sardinia,” [now a puppet of the French republic]. 
And again in May of the following year she complained to her correspondent 
de Gallo, “The English accuse us of being pro-French and the French pro- 
English—all we want is to be neutral and get rid of French troops.”#° 
Yet Elliot had already become a spokesman for the queen’s more cautious 
approach and he impressed the need for it on Nelson and Ball: “We are 
steering between Scylla and Charybdis, it is as dangerous to do too much as 
too little.”3° Ball, in Malta, when writing to Elliot, acknowledged the force 
of the latter’s argument, that it was unwise to risk losing Naples by taking 
overhasty action in Sicily. Yet he remained extremely suspicious of the pro- 
French elements in the Naples cabinet, and he was worried that the Sicilians 
themselves, discontented with rule from Naples, would if not assisted by 
Britain turn to the French out of desperation.37 For this reason he had 
ordered Villettes to have a force of 1,200 ready to move on Messina in an 
emergency.?8 
: 
Certainly Ball had extreme views about the future of Sicily and its rela- 
tionship to Great Britain—views he shared with his correspondents Wiliam 
Miles, an agent of Pitt, Francis Leckie, the English consul at Syracuse and a 

<<<PDFPAGE 32>>>
28 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
gentleman farmer in that area, and Granville Penn, the under secretary at the 
Colonial Office in London. He had, he wrote to Penn, told Nelson and Elliot 
that if Sicily should fall to the French, they would draw more wealth and 
resources from it than from the whole of Italy. Whereas, he went on, “a good 
proclamation and fifteen hundred troops would lay all Sicily at our feet—we 
must declare Messina an English port. We could then protect Sicilian trade 
from the Barbary states and turn all the inhabitants in our favour.” Miles had 
already told Ball that the Sicilian people as a whole would favor an annexa- 
tion by Britain, that the men of property said so privately, even if they dared 
not say so publicly, while “the lower and industrious class to a man would 
fight for our cause.”3? 
In August 1804 Ball was suggesting that since the king derived few 
advantages from the possession of Sicily, he might, rather than run the risk of 
having it taken over by France, be ready to accept compensation in the form 
of an annual pension from Britain, while the latter took over the island 
herself.4° There is no reason to believe that these views were ever shared by 
any British cabinet, but when four years later they were first aired in the 
columns of the British press (although then Leckie, and not Ball, was 
responsible for their publication), they caused not unnaturally some alarm 
and also suspicion at the court of Palermo—suspicion that was to be deep- 
ened further by subsequent British publications and finally by the disclosure 
of the private views of Lord William Bentinck. 
The difference of opinion between Elliot and the heads of the British 
armed services about taking action over Sicily was resolved in the end by a 
compromise, which took the form of a secret agreement between Britain and 
the court at Naples. Messina and Syracuse were to be garrisoned by the 2,000 
Neapolitan troops sent to Malta in 1802 and returned to Sicily in July 1803; 
Messina’s citadel was to be repaired; gunboats were to patrol the straits; the 
people of Calabria were to be armed; and the king’s forces in Sicily were to be 
put on a footing for war. To meet the expenses incurred by all this, the British 
government promised Naples £170,000 and thereafter an annual subsidy of 
£150,000, which if Naples was involved in hostilities could be increased to 
£300,000. And to make sure it got value for its money, it sent an experienced 
British officer, nominated by Ball and Villettes, to report on the progress of 
the fortifications. This officer, Colonel Layard, stayed in Messina for nearly 
two years, keeping in touch with Elliot and Ball, and was ready to give them 
early warning of any hostile movements from Italy.4! 
Meanwhile it was agreed that Nelson should keep a warship in the Bay of 
Naples ready to take off the royal family, if the French started to advance on 
the capital; and a year later Villettes was instructed to send 2,000 of his 
troops to Messina if Elliot should deem it necessary. 
The eventual enforced resignation of Acton meant in effect that the queen 
was once more in control of foreign policy. It was she who, against the king’s 

<<<PDFPAGE 33>>>
Getting a Foothold 
29 
wish, was to turn to Russia and also to Britain for help in getting the French 
expelled, and to get the czar to dispatch to Corfu 10,000 troops for use in 
Italy. The court of Naples in 1805 was playing a dangerous double game. On 
the one hand, through its envoy in Paris, it had signed a treaty of neutrality 
with France, which the king in Naples then ratified, and that got the French 
army withdrawn from its territory. Yet at the moment of that ratification it 
signed a military alliance with Russia and invited a Russian army to land, 
supported by a very much smaller British one. This was to be an act of bad 
faith that Napoleon was never to forgive, and it was to cause him to proclaim 
the end of Bourbon rule in Naples. 
The ever-present threat by Napoleon, once he crowned himself king of 
Italy, to take over the kingdom of Naples had been a source of continuing 
anxiety not only to Britain but also to Russia, now firmly based in the Ionian 
islands with 11,000 troops and a large naval force and ready to resist any 
French threat to Turkey. Russia had throughout 1804 been urging Britain to 
send troops to Calabria, while Britain in turn had been pressing Russia for a 
force of 15,000 men to join with her in a joint expedition to free all of Italy 
from the French.4? But the signing of any sort of agreement had been 
inhibited by several factors: the British inability to provide a force of more 
than 5,000 at a time when their own shores were faced with invasion, and 
Pitt’s recent policy decision to commit no large force to the Mediterranean; 
the Russians’ dislike of committing themselves until Austria had shown her 
hand; and above all the Russians’ suspicions that the British were only 
concerned with Sicily, where British troops would be little use in helping the 
allied cause on the mainland. Back in the summer of 1803, the Russian 
chargé d’affaires in Naples had warned Acton against British ambitions,*? 
and eighteen months later the queen had shown Elliot letters received from 
her envoy in Petersburg urging her to distrust the British. It seemed to the 
czar they were interested only in embroiling Europe in a general war, from 
which they would shortly seek to escape, retaining Sicily as a British posses- 
sion. The czar, it was said, was most unwilling for the keys of Sicily to be 
given to Britain.44 These Russian suspicions were not justified, but it does 
seem from what Harrowby, the then-foreign secretary, wrote to Elliot on 5 
November 1804 that the cabinet was very interested in developing Sicily’s 
military potential as a source of manpower for future operations conducted in 
the Mediterranean.! Harrowby envisaged that if the king was forced to take 
refuge in Sicily, a considerable proportion of his army could then be enlisted 
in British pay. This force, commanded by British officers, could become a 
very efficient army, and though initially at the service of the king, it could 
later be made available to serve elsewhere in the Mediterranean.*9 
By the spring of 1805, however, the difficulties with Russia had been 
overcome, even if Russia’s suspicions remained in the minds of her diplomats 
if not of her soldiers. On 11 April 1805 an Anglo-Russian treaty was signed 

<<<PDFPAGE 34>>>
30 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
providing for the protection of Naples and for the expulsion of the French 
from Italy. Meanwhile a force of 5,000 men under General Craig was sent 
out from England, destined initially for Malta, but ultimately for Italy. Craig 
had orders to place himself at the disposal of the Russian commander, and 
British transports were to convey Russian troops from Corfu to Naples. But 
the principal object of Craig’s expedition, as was made clear in his secret 
instructions, was the safeguarding of the island of Sicily. On no account, his 
orders ran, were the French to be allowed on the island, and if they did 
succeed in landing, they must be expelled without delay. If French action 
should compel the king once more to seek asylum in Sicily, or if under 
extreme French pressure, he were to close his ports to the British, then Craig 
must at once take hold of Sicily. Over this decision he was accorded complete 
freedom to act on his own, although he was told that if circumstances 
permitted, he should work with Nelson, Elliot, and Ball.4° 
At the same time a British officer, Lt. Colonel Smith, was dispatched to 
Sicily to report to Malta and then to London on the current state of Sicilian 
defenses; and to help him prepare his eventual report, he was sent copies of 
the recommendations made by Charles Stuart six years earlier. The con- 
clusions to which Smith finally arrived were not very different from those of 
Stuart. The Sicilians, he found, were discontented and heartily disliked the 
French, but even so they might not resist if a force of 10,000 Frenchmen 
landed. The regular Neapolitan troops, dispersed throughout the island, 
were unreliable, especially the officers, while the principal military comman- 
ders were either useless or objects of ridicule. General Bourcard, a Swiss 
officer, had been two years in command in Sicily, yet he had never once 
stirred out of Palermo and knew nothing of the island’s resources. 
Guillichini, in command at Messina, was an ex-naval captain from Tuscany, 
ignorant of military matters and obviously a person quite unsuited to com- 
mand the island’s principal fortress. 
All the fortresses, apart from Messina, were wholly lacking in artillery, 
were generally of inferior construction, and were in a very poor state of 
repair. Palermo itself was indefensible. Syracuse suffered from a lack of guns 
and also lacked an adequate water supply. Messina alone was in good 
condition and with a garrison of 3,000 British soldiers could hold out, Smith 
thought, indefinitely. As for the island generally, he agreed with the verdict of 
General Stuart that it could only be defended by the local inhabitants. All this 
Smith reported to the court before returning home to England, and he 
thought the king and queen welcomed the idea of Sicily having a British 
garrison.*7 
The landing of an Anglo-Russian force consisting of 13,000 Russian and 
7,000 British troops under the command of General Maurice de Lacy, an 
aged but Anglophile Russian officer, in the bay of Naples in September 1805 
and its hasty withdrawal a few months later, without ever having fired a shot,
