do not need recounting once again. It would never have landed in the first 
place had Napoleon not ordered General St. Cyr’s withdrawal to meet the 
imminent Austrian threat, and he fully intended that St. Cyr should return 
once Austria had been defeated. Napoleon’s victories at Ulm and Austerlitz 
made the presence of the Anglo-Russian force in the kingdom of Naples 
anachronistic. Even with the help of Neapolitan forces, most of whom were 
completely untrained, it was quite inadequate to defend Naples against the 
forces the French could muster. General de Lacy received an order to with- 
draw his army at once to Corfu, while Craig had already made the decision 
that to carry out his original instructions he must reembark and retire to 
Sicily. Yet while there remained the slightest hope in the minds of the queen 
and her advisers of negotiating with Napoleon, the court remained extremely 
anxious to prevent the British from landing in Sicily. Serious differences 
arose between Elliot and Craig over this question, Elliot supporting the court 
in its efforts over forty days to treat with Napoleon. Offers were made on 
behalf of the queen involving Ferdinand abdicating in favor of his eldest son 
and (though this was not known to Elliot) closing the ports to British ships. 
It was believed that Spain and Russia would lend the negotiations support, 
and Tatischeff, the Russian envoy in Naples, who was certainly no friend of 
the British, hinted to Neapolitan ministers that Britain intended to take over 
Sicily.48 Initially the queen had desired the British force to remain near 
Naples to back her diplomacy by a show of force, but when Craig made clear 
he was leaving anyway, she had wanted the British to go to Malta. Writing to 
Damas, she fulminated at what she regarded as British cowardice: “The smell 
of gunpowder sickens the feeble organs of General Craig. . 
. 
. I am entirely 
disanglomanised.”4? 
Craig in fact went to Messina not Malta, although he took care not to land 
his force until invited by the king to do so. Elliot, who remained ingenuously 
satisfied that the queen’s negotiations had some chance of success, told Craig 
that an uninvited landing would be treated by the court as a hostile act. Craig 
understandably showed irritation, sure that Elliot was being deceived. As 
Nelson had noted two years earlier, the court at Naples had not found it 
difficult to hoodwink this British minister.°° 
Writing to Castelreagh on 1] February, Craig told him his original inten- 
tion had been to make for the port of Augusta, much more capacious than 
that of Messina, but had decided the risk of leaving the latter open to the 
French was one it was too great to take. They might slip across 5,000 troops 
before the British were aware they had done so, and since Collingwood and 
the British fleet had had to leave the Mediterranean, there was no squadron at 
hand to deter them. Now that the French were established in Venice and in 
Venice’s ports in the Adriatic, Sicily appeared to be all the more vital to 
British interests in the Mediterranean. The court and Elliot had seemed to 

<<<PDFPAGE 38>>>
34 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
think he intended to land without permission, but in the existing circum- 
stances he saw no necessity to do so.°! 
In early February the disagreement between Craig and Elliot was brought 
to an end by the steady advance of the French army, and the “existing 
circumstances” mentioned by Craig were a reference to the king’s decision to 
leave Naples and take refuge in Sicily. Napoleon had never had the least 
intention of yielding to the requests of the queen. “The dynasty of Naples has 
ceased to reign,” he had publicly pronounced at Pressburg, and his brother 
Joseph was to seize the kingdom. On 9 February the king sailed for Palermo, 
followed shortly by the queen and her entourage, and once in Sicily, on 
Acton’s prompting, he visited Messina and allowed Craig to land.®? In the 
third week in February British troops took over Messina. They were not to 
leave it for another nine years. 
Julian Corbett describes these events as “the last stroke of the Trafalgar 
campaign,” by which the British were able to avert the full consequences of 
Ulm and Austerlitz.°? And there is no doubt that if the British had not been 
able to command the straits from their firm base in Messina citadel, having 
first removed all boats from the mainland as well as the coastal artillery, the 
French, pursuing the routed elements of the Neapolitan army of Damas, 
could have crossed with ease into Sicily and so gone on to threaten Malta and 
the whole command of the Mediterranean. 

<<<PDFPAGE 39>>>
2 
Sicily as the British Found It in 1806 
S 
icily was different from mainland Italy. This difference arose partly be- 
cause the island lay so close to the coast of north Africa, and after its 
conquest by the Arabs retained a very strong African connection that lasted 
into the sixteenth century; and partly because it became and remained, for a 
period of over four hundred years, a kingdom annexed to the throne of Spain 
and was administered separately from other Spanish possessions in Italy. 
When Ferdinand IV of Naples became King Ferdinand III of Sicily, succeed- 
ing his father Charles III, there were few ties of affinity between the Neapoli- 
tans and the Sicilians. The African and the Spanish connections had bred in 
the people of Sicily a complacent sense of isolation from what went on in the 
rest of Europe. 
The period of the African connection had been one of great prosperity and 
vigor, intellectual as well as material, and the Arab influence on Sicily’s 
culture persisted throughout the reign of the Normans and of the Suabian, 
Frederick II. The exotic flavor of the court at Palermo, where Arab and 
Byzantine influence intermingled, made it entirely different from any other 
court to be found in Europe.? 
The Aragonese and then the Spanish connections (once the thrones of 
Aragon and Castile were united), which began at the end of the thirteenth 
century (when Peter of Aragon was called in to replace the detested An- 
gevin), resulted in Sicily becoming still more distinct from neighboring Italy. 
Sicily welcomed Spanish rule, and in spite of periodic revolts, was for long 
considered a loyal province; most Sicilians looked on themselves as closer to 
Spaniards than to Italians.* But this meant that Sicily was also cut off from 
that flowering of Italian culture that began in the age of Giotto and Dante 
and blossomed into the Italian Renaissance. Its isolation was further in- 
creased by the introduction there of the Spanish Inquisition.® This last was 
responsible, needless to say, for effectively strangling free enquiry, so that all 
the rebirth of learning and science that illuminated so much of Europe in the 
sixteenth and seventeenth centuries had little effect on the island of Sicily. 
There were other respects in which Sicily’s distinctness from Italian life was 
made manifest. The island boasted of having a parliament that dated back to 
the early Middle Ages and that at the end of the eighteenth century was still 
possessed of its liberties. That this fossilized relic of past history, surrounded 
35 

<<<PDFPAGE 40>>>
36 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
as it was by both myth and romance, was now merely a bastion of privilege 
and had been largely responsible for keeping Sicily poor and backward does 
not alter the fact that it was extraordinary and indeed very nearly unique. In 
the whole of Europe—outside of Sicily, only England and Corsica could 
boast of parliaments that had remained intact.© Also in contrast to other 
countries, at the end of the eighteenth century when the European aristocra- 
cies had lost much of their power (and were trying desperately to regain it) 
and most if not all of their feudal privileges, the nobles in Sicily had hung on 
to theirs. Henry Swinburne—an English traveller to the island—remarked in 
1785 how the Sicilian baronage were more independent of royal authority 
than were the barons in the kingdom of Naples, and exercised more control 
of their vassals. In Sicily, the lord of the manor seemed to operate without 
restraint: 
He appoints to every place of jurisdiction and trust; civil and criminal law 
are both distributed by his creatures ... in general the barons have 
disposal of everything within their manor.” 
While a Frenchman visiting Sicily in 1778 could write: 
The Sicilians, remote from their king, whom they know only by his 
representative, are accustomed to consider him as a pensioner whom they 
defraud of his due; while they pride themselves in granting free gifts, and 
from time to time make experiments of disobedience, with the sole view of 
performing an act that may assist their freedom. 
The Sicilians have had so many sovereigns that they are accustomed to 
like none of them, and to give the preference only to him, with whom they 
can make the best conditions.® 
He also commented on what he described as their “insular and republican 
character.” Even the Catholic Church in Sicily was remote and cut off from 
the continent. Subjection to Rome was minimal in matters ecclesiastical and 
all holders of senior church appointments were nominated to their posts by 
the crown. The king of Sicily in his own person was apostolic delegate, and 
the court of monarchia, which enjoyed supreme jurisdiction in church affairs, 
was presided over by a judge who was a royal appointee.? 
Sicily in 1806 was, as it had remained for centuries, “a traditionalist and 
static society” (to use the words of Professor Mack Smith), and all attempts 
by its foreign rulers during the eighteenth century to change it to conform 
with modern ideas in keeping with the Enlightenment had in every case 
encountered failure. What the British found, when they came to the island, 
was a country that for historical reasons was so backward it was a century 
behind the most advanced of the western states. Nelson in the summer of 
1803 described Sicily’s condition as “almost as bad as a civilised country’s can 

<<<PDFPAGE 41>>>
Sicily as the British Found It in 1806 
Buh 
be,” with a population so discontented it would welcome even the hated 
French, if that meant any change for the better.!° He was merely echoing Sir 
Arthur Paget, who had told Grenville in 1800 that the state of the island was 
so wretched and corrupt that “nothing useful can be effective but by the 
introduction and direct influence of foreigners.” There were several others 
who shared this opinion. Colonel Bunbury, on General Craig’s staff, re- 
marked on the welcome the British received when they first landed in 1806; 
he noted that if a few of the merchants had appeared at first to favor the 
French, this was out of no love for the latter but because “they longed for 
better laws” and some “relief from capricious tyranny.” And the French they 
reckoned were more likely to stay, while the British would only be “birds of 
passage.”!1 
The Abbate Balsamo, who was Nelson’s contemporary and an expert 
agronomist among other things, saw the difference between the Sicilian 
laborer and his counterparts in Western Europe as similar to that between the 
Sicilians and the savage tribes of Africa or America.!? The plight of the 
peasant was hard indeed. He lived in a state of extreme poverty, seeing the 
profits from agriculture passing into the hands of a tiny minority—the 
income of the richest prince (Butera) equalled one-tenth of the national 
revenue, or so it was reckoned in 1810. He was often forced to live in the 
open, no matter how inclement the weather, or to return to his village late at 
night, since his place of work was so far from his home. The peasant was ill- 
clothed, ill-housed, very close to starvation, burdened by all sorts of dues and 
taxes, ignorant of modern methods of farming, short of capital and clinging 
desperately and obstinately to outdated customs. The Scots landowner Gould 
Francis Leckie, who at the end of the eighteenth century had farmed land 
round Syracuse, had endeavored to introduce new farming methods, but 
according to the traveller John Galt, who visited Sicily in 1810, few had 
followed his example.'% 
Though once the granary of the Roman Empire, Sicily by the start of the 
nineteenth century could scarcely produce enough to feed itself. Only a third 
of the land was cultivated and this so badly that it produced no more than a 
third of what it might have. The main reasons for the shortage of corn lay in 
the increased population, which by 1800 was half as much again what it had 
been in 1700, and in a shift in the population from the country areas to the 
towns.!4 But the inefficiency of the system by which the corn was marketed, 
as well as the unwillingness of cultivators and landlords to undertake agri- 
cultural improvements that would have ensured increased production and 
have maintained an export trade, was also a major contributory factor. The 
growing of greater quantities of cereal was not encouraged by government 
taxes on the export of corn and the consumption of flour. And the strict 
control of export licences benefited only the merchants of corn who “played 
the market” and manipulated prices. Peter Brydone, in his tour through 

<<<PDFPAGE 42>>>
38 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
Sicily made in 1770, remarked how the duties on the export of wheat that he 
described as “exorbitant” had served to make corn a drug on the market and 
had been responsible for the ruin of farmers.1° Of the sum total of Sicily’s 
harvest, a third was bought by the government at a price it fixed for its own 
convenience, and the rest was sold to monopolist corporations. The latter, 
not the market, controlled the price, and price controls led inevitably to a 
black market and to the bribery of officials. 1© 
There were other reasons for the decline of the grain trade and for a falling 
off in production. The kind of hard grain that was grown in Sicily could not 
compete with the cheaper-priced soft grain that was able to be grown in 
milder climates. The discontinuance of granting long leases led to the re- 
moval of any incentive to improve the land or to use modern methods. Land 
was overcropped and exhausted, while the profits accruing to the landlords 
were not invested in agriculture but frittered away in conspicuous consump- 
tion by absentee landlords who lived in Palermo. Lack of roads and the high 
cost of transport also discouraged would-be improvers of the backward state 
of Sicilian farming, while the onset of the French revolutionary wars had 
dislocated all maritime trade.!7 
The plight of Sicilian agriculture had been made worse in recent times by a 
drought that had lasted for the past six years, and at the time that the British 
arrived, the appalling prospect of famine was looming. It seemed to the 
English ironical that in neighboring and barren Malta the markets were well 
supplied with corn, due to good management by the Governor Ball, while in 
Sicily there was starvation: “a glaring proof (Hugh Elliot wrote) of the . 
. 
. 
incapacity of this government,”!8 though the reasons were more complex 
than Elliot realized. 
There was very little manufacturing, although linen was made at Cal- 
tanisetta and silk was woven in Messina and Catania; and there was very little 
internal trade, not only because of the mountainous country, but also because 
roads and bridges were lacking and because there were so many local tolls. 
Even in the middle of the nineteenth century it cost more to carry goods to 
Palermo from the interior of the island than it did to transport them from 
Sicily to places as far off as Paris or London. The external trade of Sicily was 
closely linked to the mainland of Italy and to central and northern Europe. 
The war therefore was initially to have a harmful effect on this trade, and the 
loss of markets in France and Switzerland had serious results for silk produc- 
tion in the provinces of Messina and Catania.19 
Another feature of Sicily’s backwardness was its wholly corrupt judicial 
system, with a complexity of different laws seemingly designed to produce 
the maximum confusion. Because of delays in the dispensing of justice, 
people no longer looked to the courts but preferred the protection of a 
powerful noble. Judges were ignorant or subject to court pressure, torture 

<<<PDFPAGE 43>>>
Sicily as the British Found It in 1806 
39 
was used to extract confessions, and orders could be obtained from the king 
for proscriptions, confiscations, and arbitrary arrests (much in the manner of 
lettres de cachet during the ancien régime in France) to satisfy some private 
vendetta or enrich the magistrate involved.?° 
The vast majority of Sicilians in a population of a million and a half were 
either peasants or poor townspeople; Palermo’s population numbered 
200,000 and absorbed most of the wealth of the country. There was much 
jealousy of the capital on the part of towns like Catania and Messina, and 
Palermo’s claims to jurisdictions were challenged by its principal rival, Mes- 
sina. There was practically no middle class, but numerous clergy and a few 
thousand nobles. In what was still in many ways a feudal society, the Sicilian 
nobility controlled the island, some of its members being extremely rich. In 
fact, as a class, the nobility were far more wealthy than was the crown, 
holding in towns and lands in the island three times as much as the king. 
Even so, the greater part of the noble families was heavily in debt. It was said 
that it was almost as difficult to find a nobleman out of debt as it was to find 
one who was well-informed, and though the revenues of a few families were 
nominally very great, these were the families that were most in debt. This 
explains why the nobles as a class were eager, when a new constitution was 
drafted, as it was in 1812, to be rid of their feudal obligations to the crown, 
while insisting on being paid compensation for the extinction of their feudal 
rights over the peasants. They possessed the means to oppress the poor 
through the exaction of these feudal rights and enjoyed all sorts of privileges 
and jurisdictions in both town and country. Under the reforming viceroy 
Caracciolo, toward the end of the eighteenth century, enlightened reforms 
had been set in train to restrict the nobles’ privileges, put an end to monopo- 
lies, and help the peasants to get credit more easily. But the peasants were 
unwilling to change their ways, and the abolition of villeinage merely created 
landless laborers.2! In any case, the French Revolution brought all en- 
lightened reforms to a halt. Drummond, the British minister at Palermo, 
writing in the spring of 1807, compared the state of Sicily to that of England 
under the Plantagenets, even if the nobles did not keep private armies.?? In 
the view of the Scotsman Francis Leckie, the nobles as a class were “ignorant 
beyond belief” and were very largely responsible for the “abject wretchedness 
of the people.””4 
The Sicilian parliament, which was summoned to meet only once in four 
years, consisted of three bracci or estates of the realm: militare or noble, 
ecclesiastico or clerical, and demaniale or communes of the royal domain. Its 
powers were limited to granting donativi (the name implying gifts not taxes) 
to supplement the ordinary income of the crown, present petitions for redress 
of grievances, and elect a body of royal nominees whose task was to collect 
the taxes and administer the laws between sessions of parliament. In the 

<<<PDFPAGE 44>>>
40 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
braccio of peers, a nobleman had as many votes as he had feudal estates, and 
Prince Butera with his nine towns, over which he exercised jurisdiction, was 
therefore entitled to no less than nine votes in the Sicilian parliament. 
The fiscal system was quite inequitable, with its burdens falling not on 
luxuries but on articles of daily consumption. There was no register of land 
and taxes on property were based on self-assessments, while the nobles 
resisted strenuously any attempts to survey their lands.24 The king had been 
forced by his need for money to sell annuities secured on the customs to 
monopolistic corporations, yet his court, while in Sicily, made no attempt to 
live in any way economically. Neapolitan and French émigrés either occupied 
the posts at court or drew state pensions at Sicilian expense—they were a host 
of parasites, some far from reputable, and a cause of much bitterness among 
native Sicilians.*° The king foolishly, though perhaps understandably, in 
order to uphold his independence tried to maintain an army and navy as well 
as diplomatic representation at all the principal courts of Europe, something 
that the slender royal revenues, even when bolstered by British subsidies, 
found it extremely hard to finance. 
King Ferdinand, in whose person was united sovereignty over the two 
kingdoms of Naples and of Sicily, spent very little of his time at court, 
preferring the retired life of a country gentleman with sporting tastes. He had 
a passion for shooting and fishing, and showed little interest in the business 
of government, which he was glad to leave to the queen. Although lacking in 
formal education, he was neither a dolt nor a simpleton, possessing a certain 
native shrewdness that he used in his dealings with the British. He was 
moody, extremely obstinate, and had fixed ideas about his royal prerogatives 
that were similar to those of the queen. His common touch and earthy 
bonhomie commended him to the Naples lazzaroni who had nicknamed him 
“Nasone” for obvious reasons, but underneath he remained a despot, and a 
vindictive one at that.2© One Frenchman has described him as being popu- 
lacier plutot que populaire (common rather than popular), though when he 
first arrived in Palermo the people welcomed him very warmly. Only when 
they came to realize that he looked on Sicily merely as a place for furnishing 
him with the means by which he could pay his court and recover Naples did 
their attitude toward him change. For on one subject he was singleminded— 
the recovery of his Neapolitan throne. As he was to write in 1814: “I will 
never renounce what God has given me and my father left me and which in 
justice belongs to me and also to my successors, nor ever agree to accept 
compensation.”*7 Even then it was the queen and ministers rather than the 
king himself who attracted unpopularity. 
The queen of Naples, Maria-Carolina, has been portrayed so often in print 
that her character must be familiar to most people. A strange, contradictory, 
and pathetic person, she was a compound of restless energy, passion, charm, 
and utter ruthlessness, swayed by every momentary occurrence or the most 

<<<PDFPAGE 45>>>
Sicily as the British Found It in 1806 
4] 
casual and idle rumor. Robert Fagan, British consul general in Palermo and a 
man to whom she unburdened herself, described her as “either exhilarated to 
excess or engulfed in a chaos of imaginary and gigantic evils.”28 
She had all the pride of her Hapsburg mother, the formidable Empress 
Maria-Theresa, and the political amorality of her wretched sister, the French 
Queen Marie-Antoinette. General Moore, first deputy and then commander 
of the British force in Sicily in 1807, decided in the end that she was not 
really wicked, but only badly guided by those about her. It was her misfor- 
tune, in his opinion, to feel that she must meddle in public affairs for which 
she had no capacity.2? Admiral Collingwood, who commanded the fleet in 
the Mediterranean from 1805 to 1810 and had some dealings with the 
queen, shared Moore’s views to a large extent. “She would be thought a deep 
politician, yet all her schemes miscarry. She broods over what is impracticable 
with her little means, and frets herself continually that others are not as dim- 
sighted as herself.”3° She chose to be surrounded by a network of spies and 
employed agents all over Italy, as well as maintaining in her pay chiefs of the 
partisans or masse (often no more than brigand bands) in the mountains of 
Calabria and the Abruzzi. It was estimated that she spent a million and a half 
ducats in maintaining this system of spies and informers, many of whom 
proved double agents in the pay of Saliceti, the chief of police in Naples. But 
all her intrigues in Naples, Rome, Tuscany, and Genoa, London, St. Pe- 
tersburg, and Vienna in the end had very little effect. She did exercise some 
influence, though not as much as was once thought, in Sicily, over the king 
and her sons. By 1806 she had really become in many ways a tragic figure, 
worn out by personal misfortune, childbearing, and miscarriages, which had 
driven her to take opium daily. She loathed Sicily and its inhabitants, talked 
sometimes of selling the island, and longed only to return to Naples, the 
recovery of which was her main preoccupation. In the winter of 1798 she had 
described Palermo as being “poverty-striken, and peopled by rogues, where 
the price of everything is exorbitant and the climate depressing,” and she 
never altered this initial opinion. Of the Sicilians themselves she wrote, “the 
priests are completely corrupted, the people savage, the nobles of doubtful 
loyalty. 
. 
. 
. I ought not to govern Sicilians I hate them and, if they were as 
honest as I am, the King and the Hereditary Prince would say the same, for 
they think the same.” She in turn was detested in Sicily and was so unpopular 
that, when her son the hereditary prince was seriously ill (possibly from food 
poisoning), it was widely rumored that the queen had poisoned him in order 
to recover her influence.3! She, who had been devoted to the British in the 
days of Nelson and Emma Hamilton, was now convinced that they were only 
concerned to keep Sicily for themselves and had no real interest in recovering 
Naples. The only Englishmen she liked were those who were ready to flatter 
her and forward her schemes for campaigns on the mainland for the recovery 
of the kingdom of Naples—Admirals Nelson and Sidney Smith and, for a 

<<<PDFPAGE 46>>>
42 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
time, General John Stuart; she liked the British ministers at Palermo, Sir 
William Hamilton, William Drummond and, for a few years, Hugh Elliot, 
and the consul general, Robert Fagan. She hated Hamilton’s successor Paget, 
the two generals, Fox and Moore, and particularly Lord William Bentinck, 
who was to be responsible for her enforced exile from Sicily, so that she was 
later led to declare that when she saw an Englishman she felt the guillotine on 
her neck. Even though she intensely disliked the French, seeing them always 
as the murderers of her sister and oppressors of all monarchies, she was quite 
prepared to intrigue with them, if only to get rid of the British. She loathed 
the ideas of the French Revolution and had no love for the Bonapartes, 
though the personality of Napoleon had an awful fascination for her. She 
told her confidant the Marquis de Gallo that she considered Napoleon “the 
greatest man of the century” and that for him, as a person, she had “a really 
deep respect.” She said she hoped his schemes would fail, but wished him 
personal glory and good fortune, so long as they were not “at our expense.”3 
She was quite unscrupulous in her methods, and Joseph Bonaparte believed 
not only that had she placed a price on his head, but that she had tried to have 
a plague-ridden ship driven onto the coast of Naples. In the end she became 
the scapegoat for everything that went wrong in Sicily, although it is not 
difficult to understand the desire of the British to see the last of her. 
Her remaining children (only six remained of the seventeen to whom she 
had given birth) were married into Europe’s royal families—her daughters to 
the Hapsburg Emperor Francis, the Spanish Prince of Asturias, the brother 
of the king of Sardinia (in 1807), and the duke of Orleans (in 1809), while 
her eldest son, the hereditary prince, found a second wife in the Spanish 
Infanta. The hereditary prince and her youngest son Leopold (who was 
completely dominated by his mother) and her two then-unmarried daughters 
(Maria-Amelia and Maria-Christine) all accompanied the court to Sicily. 
(Both the empress of Austria and the princess of Asturias were dead before 
the end of 1807.) 
Francis, the hereditary prince, was at the age of thirty-five still largely 
under parental influence. Interested like his father almost solely in country 
pursuits, he found himself after 1810 compelled to play a political role. 
Though he seemed to go along with the British when appointed by his father 
as vicar general, he had little real sympathy with their views on Sicily and 
remained a Neapolitan at heart. 
As to ministers and court officials, the man who wielded the greatest 
influence when the British first became concerned with Sicily was the Anglo- 
phile Sir John Acton, a former favorite of the queen, although he had lost her 
favor completely. His eventual retirement was due, however, as much to old 
age and growing incapacity as to the jealousy of the queen. For many years he 
had been the real directing hand in Naples. He was not, in the opinion of 
William Drummond who briefly succeeded Paget as envoy, a very profound 

<<<PDFPAGE 47>>>
Sicily as the British Found It in 1806 
43 
politician, and one who was happy to wait on events, but nevertheless the 
only man who in Drummond’s words could hold the helm “of a crazy vessel, 
in very stormy times.”33 Forced in May 1804 to withdraw to Palermo by 
pressure from Napoleon, he continued to advise the king from Sicily and be 
sent diplomatic correspondence. When the royal family were once again 
forced to leave Naples and flee to Palermo, Acton regained for a short time 
his former dominant position, since his rival at court, the Count Damas, was 
blamed for the rout of the army of Naples. His sudden return to power, 
however, lasted a matter of less than four months, and he finally retired in 
June 1806, having become a sort of Polonius.## 
The man chosen to take his place, the Marquis Circello, was not a strong 
character. The British first thought that he was on their side, but they soon 
realized he was no match for the queen and was incapable of influencing royal 
policy. The court adopted measures without his knowledge, and his sense of 
loyalty toward the crown forced him to accept them without any protest. 
William Drummond thought little of his abilities—“no minister of foreign 
affairs was more foreign to those affairs than he”; while Lord William 
Bentinck, a few years later, contrived to have him removed from office for 
having “entirely despotic” principles.° 
The queen surrounded herself at Palermo with a group of advisers who 
were wholly non-Sicilian: Tatischeff, the Russian minister until 1808, Ka- 
unitz, the Austrian minister until 1810, Roger Count de Damas, the French 
émigré (though he left Sicily in 1806), Corsicans who were anti- Bonapartist, 
and Neapolitans like Circello, and Medici, the minister of finance, perhaps 
her most able and loyal servant (despite his dubious prior career as a sus- 
pected Jacobin), Prince Moliterno (of whom more later), and the young and 
romantic Prince Canosa, the so-called “Don Quixote of reaction,” whom she 
dispatched to the island of Ponza to try and raise a rebellion in Naples. 
She was also surrounded by humbler men, some of them near-criminals, 
the most notorious among whom were Colonel Carbone and Marquis Cas- 
trone.3 The only others who counted for something in a court dominated by 
Neapolitans and French were the Duke of Ascoli, an agent of the king, and 
the Marquis Saint Clair, the queen’s secretary and (so gossip had it) her secret 
lover. 
Ascoli was the king’s private secretary and had considerable influence with 
him, partly because he was a good sportsman and shared the king’s mania for 
shooting and fishing. Ascoli did not like Sicilians any more than he liked the 
British. He was in turn disliked by the queen, who was jealous of anyone 
who challenged her influence. Ascoli had been head of the police in Naples, 
where he gained an unsavory reputation for brutality and the abuse of power. 
In Sicily he supported the king in his role as unchallenged sovereign and 
urged him in 1811-12 to resist the demands being made on him by the 
British for constitutional and administrative reform, to the extent even of 

<<<PDFPAGE 48>>>
44 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
abdicating and seeking refuge at some foreign court. He favored taking 
extreme measures, even if this meant civil war or playing the game Napoleon 
wanted, rather than yielding to British pressure, though the king did not 
accept his advice. The British eventually forced him to leave and go into exile 
in Sardinia, but for a time he was virtual viceroy.37 
Castrone was another undesirable character, the protégé and confidant of 
the queen. He was the chief of police in Palermo and controlled the network 
of spies and agents with whom the queen surrounded herself, maintaining 
her contacts with mainland Italy and particularly with the kingdom of 
Naples. Together with the queen he ran a lucrative business in privateering 
and piracy, the profits from which went to maintain this whole system of 
informers and agents.3® While Castrone operated from Palermo, Carbone 
based himself on Messina, was instrumental in organizing flying columns of 
the Calabrian masse from 1806 to 1809, and collaborated with the English 
commanders. But he acted also as a spy on the English and reported all he 
heard back to the court, even though his truthfulness was profoundly sus- 
pect. He was appointed by the British to be head of their military secret 
service and was so well regarded by senior officers that in 1815 he was 
awarded a pension for life by the British government, on the recommenda- 
tion of Lord William Bentinck.3? 
The Marquis Saint Clair, an ex-French guards officer, possessed good looks 
but no real talent. Alquier, the French minister in Naples, had described him 
as less than a mediocrity. Though he was hated by the British as the 
representative of the pro-French faction, he hardly deserved the execration. 
Appointed tutor to Prince Leopold, he was merely the lap dog of the queen, 
and the senior posts he filled in the army gave him status but no effective 
power. He was honest and loyal to his mistress, and accompanied her when 
she went into exile.4° 
Another favorite of the queen was the colorful condottiere Prince Mo- 
literno, a dashing type of professional soldier, adventurous, physically brave, 
but lacking any political sense. The queen, who was herself a romantic, fell in 
easily with his wild schemes for the reconquest of the kingdom of Naples, 
and at one time of the whole of Italy. Even when the king was forced to 
concede the overall command of all forces in Sicily to a British officer, Lord 
William Bentinck, the court still attempted, unsuccessfully, to appoint Mo- 
literno to a similar post. His military activities, as well as those he carried out 
as a double agent in correspondence with the French, made him yet another 
of those at court who aroused in the British the deepest suspicions of 
treachery or at least bad faith.4! 
Relations between the British in Sicily and the court at Palermo grew 
increasingly strained during the first five years of occupation, and eventually 
ended in the British government agreeing to active interference—something 
that most British cabinet ministers had studied studiously to avoid. 

<<<PDFPAGE 49>>>
3 
Problems of Defense in the Early Years of 
Occupation 
Glaser 1806 and 1807 the prospect of the invasion of Sicily by a 
French army was a very real one. Napoleon did not cease to impress on 
his brother, now seated on the throne of Naples, the importance of taking 
Sicily. He could not tolerate that on Italy’s doorstep there should remain 
(and in English hands) a smugglers’ base, a refuge for deserters, a standing 
threat to the stability of his empire in Naples and elsewhere in Italy, and what 
he described as “an impassable barrier” to communications with the Levant. 
Only after the Treaty of Tilsit, signed in July 1807, which secured him Corfu 
and the Adriatic, did Napoleon lose interest in capturing the island. Sicily in 
theory remained desirable, if only to safeguard his base in Corfu, but from 
this point on he regarded it as of only secondary importance. Once he became 
committed to his plan for absorbing Spain and Portugal, Sicily was seen by 
him merely as a trap where British forces could be kept immobile that might 
otherwise oppose him in the peninsula. 
But in 1806 he could not wait to have news of the capture of Sicily. “I 
would rather,” he told Joseph, “a ten years war than leave your kingdom 
incomplete and Sicily an unsettled question.”! Both he and Joseph had 
counted on making a quick dash across the straits of Messina in the wake of 
the routed army of Damas, and they had only been frustrated in this by 
Craig, who had taken away all the boats available as well as the guns from the 
coastal batteries. Joseph then insisted that he needed an army of no less than 
25,000 men if ever a crossing was to succeed, a figure Napoleon considered 
ridiculous. According to the latter, a force of 10,000 or at the most 15,000 
was all that could reasonably be required. Joseph had, so Napoleon told him, 
15,000 more troops than he needed, and if he still could not take Sicily, not 
to mention the fortress of Gaeta whose garrison under the Prince of Hesse 
still held out against the French, then he would never be able to do so, even if 
given 100,000 men.? 
Bonapartist plans for Sicily suffered a setback in July, when General 
Stuart’s army landed in Calabria and won an impressive victory at Maida. But 
with Stuart’s withdrawal shortly after and the fall of Gaeta in the autumn, 
preparations for the capture of the island were resumed. Nothing could be 
45 

<<<PDFPAGE 50>>>
46 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
done immediately, for the masse insurrection had to be crushed and the 
country cleared of brigand bands, but this was achieved by the spring of 
1807. Even then, there still remained the British-held fortresses of Scilla and 
Reggio. These places the British had garrisoned at the time of their with- 
drawal following Maida, and their capture remained a precondition for any 
successful invasion of Sicily. Napoleon continued to pester Joseph 
throughout the summer and autumn of 1807 for their immediate reduction, 
finally issuing him a personal order to take them. By March 1808 they had 
both fallen and Sicily was once again faced with the prospect of invasion.? 
When General Craig had arrived in Sicily in February 1806 he had found 
the island largely defenseless. On its long coastline, with its multiplicity of 
roadsteads, small bays, and open beaches, an enemy force could land very 
easily; and its main harbors, save for Messina, were lacking in the necessary 
means to defend them—artillery, stores, and also men. Colonel Smith had 
made this apparent in his report of the previous October when he stated that 
the island as a whole could only be defended by its inhabitants. The regular 
forces at the king’s disposal were supposed to number 9,000 or thereabouts, 
but their effective strength, it transpired, was not much more than a third of 
this figure, and they were for the most part in very poor shape. As to the 
militia, it proved nonexistent for all practical purposes, a force without either 
clothes or pay or any sort of organization. Meanwhile Sicily remained depen- 
dent on Malta for guns and ammunition.* Apart from the northeastern strip 
of the island occupied by the forces of Craig, its safety, as Elliot impressed on 
Collingwood, was wholly in the hands of the British navy, and the extent of 
the latter’s protection would sway the loyalty of the Sicilians. Collingwood 
did in fact provide a substantial squadron to guard Sicily and keep watch on 
the enemy-held ports in the Tyrrenhian and Adriatic—four ships-of-the-line, 
eight frigates, and three sloops—while a sizeable flotilla of gunboats and 
galleys, including Neapolitan gunboats, was based on Messina to cover the 
straits. The formation of the Sicilian flotilla was a most important contribu- 
tion to the effective defense of Sicily, since it was small craft that were needed 
in the narrow waters that separated Sicily from Calabria. It was manned by 
Sicilian volunteers who were only too willing to serve in vessels that flew the 
British flag, not the Neapolitan one.© What remained of the Neapolitan navy 
was of very little immediate assistance, apart from the gunboats already 
mentioned—one ship-of-the-line and some frigates and sloops, but so ne- 
glected through lack of money they were little better than useless hulks. 
Later, though not until 1809, these ships were repaired at British expense in 
the naval dockyards at Gibraltar and Malta. 
Lack of money in the next few years, despite a large subsidy paid by the 
British, continued to keep the king’s regular army in a wretched state of 
inefficiency. It was badly trained and, in General Moore’s view, did not 
possess a serviceable musket. Recruitment was a difficult problem because of 

<<<PDFPAGE 51>>>
Problems of Defense in the Early Years of Occupation 
47 
the inadequate rewards that were offered. The queen tried to enlist the masse 
and form them into regular units, but these people resented discipline. 
Criminals and Italian deserters from the French army were accepted as 
recruits, and the king ordered all means short of force, though he dared not 
use conscription, to encourage enlistment. General Fox found the Neapolitan 
troops totally undependable and despised by the masse and the people of 
Sicily. He regretted that the more reliable Sicilians were not kept apart from 
the Neapolitans, but the court “by the fatality that attends almost all their 
measures” made no attempt to separate them, and they lost all pride and 
esprit de corps.” 
After the disaster at Mileto at the end of May 1807, when 6,000 men 
under the Prince of Hesse met with humiliating defeat, a rather more serious 
attempt was made to organize the royal army. The king and queen, with 
some reluctance, allowed its inspection by British generals, who were not 
impressed by what they found. Not only was the discipline poor—the officers 
discontented, the soldiers unpaid, pawning or selling their arms or desert- 
ing—but the higher command was unimpressive. Even Count Damas, favor- 
ite of the queen and certainly no lover of the British, was more damning in 
his verdict on the latter than any British general: “Hesse is a loyal and brave 
man, but very far from intelligent. Bourcard does not want to leave his 
beloved Sicily; baron Acton [brother of Sir John] is merely another edition of 
Hesse, while Cattolica is a fool. That is all I can say. In fact we have 
nobody.”8 
In 1806 attempts were made to raise a genuine force of militia, the court 
issuing an edict in May to enroll a force of 24,000. Yet a year later Colonel 
Bunbury, quartermaster general and chief of staff, reckoned that there was 
not a single militiaman in the whole of the district the British occupied. In 
1808 Cattolica brought forward a plan to enlist 40,000 Sicilians, raised and 
commanded by local landowners, but this was opposed by the queen and the 
Neapolitans. The British command had no faith in the scheme and disap- 
proved of it anyway as discouraging regular enlistment. There was already 
jealousy between the regulars and the volunteers over the question of rank 
and command, and there was no provision for unified control, let alone 
overall command by the British. In June 1809 there were reckoned to be a 
maximum of 1,500 militiamen in the whole of the island, outside Palermo, 
who were trained dnd equipped for general service. And the corps at Palermo 
the British considered to be fit for little save ceremonial occasions. The court 
did agree to the British embodying two battalions of Sicilian militia, but 
nothing ever came of this. At Augusta not a man turned up, and at Milazzo 
the 380 who did appear were of such poor quality (having been crimped or 
dragooned into joining) that they had to be instantly discharged.? 
All this meant that Sicily’s defenses continued to rest on the British alone, 
apart from any help from the peasants whom the court out of fear refused to

48 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
arm. Initially Craig had had to strip Malta in order to raise the strength of his 
force, but on his return soon after to England (giving up his command on 
grounds of ill-health) he persuaded the British ministers to reinforce the 
Sicily garrison. Guns and gun-carriages arrived, as well as two battalions of 
infantry, and General John Stuart, who succeeded Craig, was able to recruit 
500 Sicilians, paid and clothed by the British government and officered 
largely not by Sicilians (to Colonel Bunbury’s relief) but by members of the 
Corsican corps or by foreign regiments. By the early summer of 1806 Stuart 
had a force of 7,500 holding a defense line in northeast Sicily, which 
stretched from Milazzo in the north through Faro and Messina as far south as 
Taormina. The king then allowed him to extend his command further south 
to Cape Passaro to include the ports of Augusta and Syracuse, which, on the 
insistence of Collingwood, were to be garrisoned by British troops.!° Even 
so, the island enjoyed a security that was only relative. Moore, writing in 
1807, took a typically pessimistic view of the prospects of defending it 
successfully. He thought it required 12,000 more troops, to include cavalry 
and artillery, in addition to the existing garrison, which then amounted to 
14,000. More brigs, cutters, and small craft were needed, while the fortresses 
that were far too numerous but because they existed had to be manned were 
(save for ammunition) completely lacking in the means to resist an attack for 
long.14 
The glaring defect in these Sicilian defenses did not however prevent the 
island from being used for offensive purposes during the summer of 1806. 
The driving force behind the offensive was the British Admiral Sidney Smith, 
recently appointed commander of the fleet in the central Mediterranean. 
Pushing, vain, and irresponsible, the admiral arrived with a great reputation. 
Everyone had heard of his exploits, for he had a great gift for self-publiciza- 
tion: his daring raids on the French coast, his dramatic escape from the 
Temple prison, his triumphant relief of St. Jean d’Acre. A spoilt child of 
fortune, brave, dashing, seductive, he was (in the words of Jacques Ram- 
baud) “only too ready to lend an ear to the flattery of an Italian court and 
become the champion of a distraught queen.”!? The latter, delighted to 
discover what she fondly imagined was another Nelson, played on Smith’s 
obvious vanity, and decided, as she said, “to work on his feelings.”!3 On 
pleas from her, he took stores and ammunition to the garrison of besieged 
Gaeta, though without consulting anyone else. This infuriated Elliot, who 
prided himself that he alone could approach Acton and so the court.14 
Smith raided the Calabrian coast, capturing guns and inciting revolt. He 
took Capri, got Stuart to garrison it, and then persuaded him to cooperate in 
a scheme to conquer the whole of Calabria. In fact the object of the expedi- 
tion, which Stuart led with 5,000 troops who were landed in the Gulf of St. 
Euphemia, was to force the French from the toe of Italy, destroy their supply 
dumps, guns, and magazines, and so pre-empt an invasion of Sicily. In this

Stuart was entirely successful, though his victory at Maida in July owed more 
to the action of his subordinates than to brilliance of leadership on his part. 
The French lost nearly 4,000 men and at Crotone, to which the British sent 
two regiments round by sea, they lost all their guns and warlike stores, 
accumulated for use against Sicily, as well as their army’s sick and wounded 
that General Régnier had left behind. 
Wisely refusing to pursue the French army with the limited force at his 
command and wisely refusing to commit a force of 1,000 men to reinforce 
Gaeta (a fortress that was on the point of falling), Stuart contented himself 
with capturing the strongpoints of Reggio and Scilla, the two places the 
French must hold before embarking on an invasion of Sicily. It should be 
remembered that in doing all this he had acted entirely without instructions, 
his only orders from London being to safeguard Sicily against attack.!° 
During the course of these operations, Sidney Smith had obtained from 
Ferdinand a commission to act as the viceroy of Calabria, with command of 
all the king’s forces there, and this not unnaturally had annoyed Stuart. The 
latter through Elliot and Acton made strong representations to the court and 
as a result Smith had to step down and accept Stuart as commander-in- 
chief.!° But he did succeed in involving Stuart in schemes, which were really 
those of the queen, for raising the Calabrian masse. Stuart was unwisely 
persuaded to issue an inflammatory proclamation inciting the Calabrians to 
rise, an action that the ministry in London found a source of some embarrass- 
ment. Stuart, though acclaimed for his victory and rewarded by being made a 
Knight Commander of the Bath, was all the same rebuked (although mildly) 
for the indiscretion of his proclamation. The government, he was told, could 
not guarantee indefinite protection for the people of Calabria, and in taking 
his action he should first have obtained the approval of the British Minister 
Elliot.!7 Stuart shortly afterward resigned out of pique at being passed over 
for command and was replaced by General Henry Fox, the younger brother 
of the statesman, while General John Moore was sent out to act as Fox’s 
deputy and second-in-command. Henry Fox was a sick man and known by 
the ministers to be such, so his reliance on John Moore, where decisions or 
actions were required, was something that was accepted in London. 
Meanwhile Sidney Smith, in the words of Bunbury, “always panting for 
distinction 
. 
. 
. restlessly active”!8 and bursting with enthusiasm, spread his 
ships and small craft along the coast as far north as the bay of Naples, 
scattering his proclamations to revolt. Moore blamed him, perhaps unfairly, 
for concentrating on these activities rather than trying to relieve Gaeta, and 
for taking credit for capturing towns that had either already been abandoned 
or must have fallen to the-British army as it fell back on Scilla and Reggio. 
Smith kept pressing Elliot and Fox for more money and more men, urging 
that these were all that were needed to drive the French out from the 
kingdom of Naples. Moore, who was the real driving force behind the less

decisive and ailing Fox, had to point out that it was not the intention of the 
British command to tie up its forces in Calabria, more so since the French 
had been reinforced. The Calabrians anyway were hopelessly divided, with 
property owners siding with the French and the masse intent on rapine and 
murder. The main object of the British forces must remain the defense of 
Sicily, and the navy’s small craft ought to be used not in conducting futile 
raids but in intercepting the coastal traffic. 
Moore was sent by Fox to investigate what Sidney Smith was really doing, 
and, having landed at various points, he came to the conclusion Smith’s ideas 
were absurd. Moore found the people frightened and disheartened, ready to 
hoist, as danger dictated, either the Bourbon or republican flag and occupied 
only in plundering or satisfying some private vengeance. He thought it not 
only an act of cruelty but one unbecoming to the British name to encourage 
this kind of hopeless insurrection unless the British intended to stay. On 
behalf of Fox he therefore told Smith to abstain from any further promises of 
British assistance to chiefs of the masse; Fox alone must now be responsible 
for help afforded to the Calabrians.!? Smith however remained firm in his 
conviction of the rightness of his strategy and that Sicily “could not be 
defended militarily or maintained politically without Calabria as a téte du pont 
and as a magazine and mart.”?° 
Elliot, who was sensitive on questions affecting protocol and his position 
as minister, wrote home privately to complain of Sidney Smith’s irregular 
behavior: his direct communications with the queen, his importunate re- 
quests for funds, and his misapplication of those funds, granted for the 
purpose of obtaining intelligence.?1 Smith eventually received from the 
Admiralty a mild reproof (though he described it as “most mortifying”) 
,?7 
but he nevertheless remained the queen’s ally in her plans for the reconquest 
of Naples until his posting away the following January. There is no doubt 
that Sidney Smith had found in Stuart a ready collaborator, and the latter’s 
departure meant that the army was no longer prepared to indulge Smith’s 
schemes. Henry Fox wrote home to his brother that Calabria had become for 
Stuart “in a degree his hobby horse” and that he had “in the vagabond massi 
[sic]” a confidence he thought no one else had. Stuart was, in Fox’s opinion, 
not without his share of vanity and he had more confidence in the court than 
Fox had, because Stuart had been misled by its flattery and failed to realize 
how much it was despised.?% 
After defeating the Prussians at Jena in October 1806, Napoleon had 
advanced into Poland but had then been checked and had retired onto 
Warsaw. Russian ministers in foreign courts gave out that the French had 
been defeated, and the court at Palermo, and the queen especially, were only 
too ready to believe this story. In consequence the queen, through Circello, 
pressed hard for another expedition to be dispatched to mainland Italy, 
suggesting that this was desirable, if only for political reasons. If a general

peace were concluded, she argued, it might well be on the basis of uti

always withdraw to Sicily.?4 But quite apart from the fact that Britain had 
never guaranteed the recovery of Naples, Circello’s proposal was unaccepta- 
ble. As General Fdx explained to his brother, such an expedition was bound 
to call down a superior army of the French, who had ample reserves in the 
north of Italy. Moreover there were few on the mainland who genuinely 
supported the Bourbon cause, and most Calabrians gave their support only 
to the party that appeared the strongest and that promised them the max- 
imum personal gain. To send a force to Italy now would divide the army in 
Sicily, weaken the defenses of that island, and prevent a decisive blow being 
struck when the opportunity eventually offered. He regarded the plan to send 

<<<PDFPAGE 60>>>

SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
Hesse to Naples with an army of 8,000 royal troops to cooperate with a 
British army (even supposing a force of that size could be raised at that time 
in Sicily) as both “chimerical and absurd.” Masséna, he reckoned, was far too 
experienced to make the mistake of Régnier at Maida, and anyway he was so 
strongly positioned, he could choose freely which force to attack.?° 
Two months earlier, Admiral Collingwood had already voiced very similar 
views. Writing to Elliot in July, he had expressed considerable skepticism 
about the wisdom of capturing Capri, an island without either a port or a 
roadstead, which could hardly be reckoned to be of much use in preventing 
the passage of coastal traffic. As for an expedition to Naples, he regarded it as 
“the vainest dream that ever entered the imagination of a woman”: 
When they [the Bourbons] 
possessed it, with all the resources of the 
country at their command, with 
the (professed at least) loyalty of an armed 
people, and the army of the allies at their head, it was abandoned as 
untenable; and now that the country is disarmed ... and the enemy 
possessing every place of strength, on what foundation can the hope of 
success be built?? 
Even the fall of Gaeta fortress Collingwood considered no bad thing, all 
attempts to support its resistance having taken ships from more useful tasks, 
exhausted Sicily of war stores, and reduced British strength in guns and men. 
Drummond, who had returned to Sicily in February 1807 to take up once 
again the post he had relinquished in 1803—Fox, since Elliot’s departure, 
having filled in both as minister at Palermo and military commander—was 
also convinced, at least initially, that the time was not ripe for invading Italy, 
and took offense at Circello’s attitude.?” But the queen was not to be put off 
so easily, even if it meant an expedition consisting only of Neapolitans, and 
Fox discovered that without any notice the royal troops in the British area 
had been withdrawn from his command.?8

The Court, William Drummond, and the British 
Generals 
deep distrust obtaining between the court at Palermo and the British 
A government, further exacerbated as it was to be by the strained relations 
that soon developed between Drummond and the British generals, became 
apparent in 1806. Negotiations for peace with Napoleon had been con- 
ducted by Charles James Fox, foreign secretary in the Ministry of All the 
Talents, during the months of June and July, and fears were expressed by 
Acton and Circello that the British saw Sicily as expendable; they wanted to 
force the king to exchange it for territory in, perhaps, the Balearics.! It was 
true that the Russian plenipotentiary had agreed to accept such terms from 
the French, but the czar had refused to ratify them, and Britain never agreed 
to them. Drummond was instructed to refute vigorously allegations made in 
Palermo that Britain had neglected Ferdinand’s interests, and to emphasize 
instead that it was her perseverance in standing up for the king’s right to 
choose that had been one of the principal reasons for the failure of peace 
negotiations. Whitbread, while speaking in the House of Commons in 
January 1807, made clear that Fox could only have proceeded on the basis of 
uti posstdetis, and had made the retention of Sicily the sine qua non of any 
peace settlement. Howick, speaking in the same debate, referred to the 
impossibility of Britain ever surrendering Sicily without first obtaining Ferdi- 
nand’s consent, adding with a patriotic flourish that “it would have broken 
the hearts of those brave men who fought for it in the plains of Maida, if it 
could have been given up on any other conditions.” In any case the death of 
Charles Fox, which occurred in September 1806, had ended the peace 
negotiations; but as even he had allowed the garrison to be increased to 
13,000 by the end of July 1806, there was really no question of Britain 
deciding to leave Sicily in any hurry. Sicily (in the words of Professor 
Holland Rose) “was the chief cause of the prolongation of the war, as Malta 
had been of its inception.” 
Instead, the ministry decided to bind the court more closely to her British 
ally by offering it a treaty of alliance. This treaty provided for Sicily’s ports to 
be kept open to British ships, and for Sicily to supply provisions for British 
crews and British troops as well as for the island of Malta. In return, Britain 
agreed to protect the ports of Messina and Augusta, to maintain at least 
58 

<<<PDFPAGE 63>>>
The Court, Drummond, and the Generals 
59 
10,000 troops stationed on the island at her own expense, and to pay a 
subsidy of £300,000 annually, backdated to September 1805. Further provi- 
sions were suggested to the court, but these were most unfavorably received. 
They involved the British guaranteeing to keep 5,000 troops in Sicily in 
peacetime, giving Syracuse, Milazzo, and Augusta the same free-port status 
Messina enjoyed, and granting to British merchants in Sicily the position of 
most-favored nationals.* The court was understandably afraid that the British 
had ideas about annexing Sicily, while Messina was bitterly opposed to 
sharing her privileges with other ports. The hostile manner in which the draft 
treaty embodying these last clauses was rejected by the court caused a 
temporary rift with the British government. Drummond decided to suspend 
the subsidy and wrote back to London for instructions. These instructions, 
drafted in March 1807, were never in fact dispatched to him because of the 
fall of the ministry and its replacement by one led by Portland. They would 
have entailed Britain playing a significant role in governing Sicily, the sort of 
role that she was eventually compelled to adopt in 1811. They would have 
entailed the continued suspension of the subsidy Britain paid to the court and 
ultimately the use of force, were the court not to accept measures designed to 
render it less unpopular, and were aimed at removing what seemed a real 
danger of a discontented people or a disloyal court betraying Sicily to the 
enemy. Strangers obnoxious to Sicilians were to be removed from the royal 
council, Sicilian noblemen were henceforth to be admitted to positions of 
trust, there was to be a unified command under the senior British officer, and 
no post of military importance was to be given to a non-Sicilian, or indeed to 
anybody not approved by the British commander. It was also proposed that 
the British minister should have the power to remove from their posts any 
foreigners suspected of treachery.* 
When Canning took over the Foreign Office, he decided these instructions 
should not be sent and that all that could reasonably be required of the king 
was his confidence and cooperation. But by this time, Drummond, having 
been persuaded that the court’s attitude had radically changed, had already 
renewed the payment of the subsidy. The treaty, without the offending 
clauses, was signed on 23 March 1807, though because of changes in the 
international scene brought about by the Treaty of Tilsit in July, its ratifica- 
tion by the British sovereign was postponed for another fourteen months. 
Drummond had éonvinced himself that because the queen had agreed to 
withdraw from the royal council and Saint Clair had been removed from his 
post, the policy of the court had changed, and that he (Drummond), Cir- 
cello, and the king would henceforth control the conduct of affairs. In 
believing this he was certainly naive, and the generals made it clear that they 
thought so. 
Meanwhile the British government had decided during the winter of 1806 
to send a naval force to Constantinople to support Russia and to frighten the 

<<<PDFPAGE 64>>>
60 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
Porte, who, it was thought, was about to yield to French pressure and side 
with Napoleon. Though Britain now possessed in Sicily an army of 20,000 
men, it was felt that because of the court’s dubious loyalty, no substantial 
force could be spared from the island to lend support to the naval squadron— 
a deprivation that had fatal consequences since it resulted in the total failure 
of Duckworth’s expedition to the Dardanelles. Instead it was decided to send 
from Messina a force to seize Alexandria, in order to forestall a possible move 
by Napoleon to recover Egypt.® 
Accordingly 6,000 men, commanded by Brigadier General Fraser, were 
dispatched to capture Alexandria from the Mamelukes, which they did on 20 
March 1807. Fraser was not an experienced commander, and Fox would 
have preferred to send Moore, but decided he could not spare him from 
Sicily. Fraser, in trying to extend his position to include control of the Nile 
delta, suffered two serious military reverses involving the loss of a third of his 
force, and Fox had to send from. Sicily 2,000 reinforcements to make good 
this loss. When the ministry in London was told by Fox that it would require 
16,000 troops, including cavalry, to defend Egypt, it decided Sicily took 
priority. The possession of Alexandria, wrote Windham, had always been 
subordinate to the security of Sicily, and to “the more important object” of 
acting against the French in Italy.” The British troops in Egypt were with- 
drawn, though not until September 1807. 
Meanwhile General Fox had been under strong pressure both from Drum- 
mond, now a spokesman for the court, and from the Russian ambassador 
Tatischeff, to send another army to Italy to relieve the pressure on Britain’s 
allies. Such an expedition would, it was argued, cause a diversion in favor of 
the Russians, who felt threatened in Dalmatia by French forces in Friuli, and 
would meet with little opposition in Naples, which kingdom the French had 
denuded of troops. Now that the army of Napoleon had received a serious 
setback in Poland, the time was ripe, so it was argued, for an assault on Italy.8 
General Fox rejected these arguments. He had already made clear to Drum- 
mond his objections to such an enterprise, drawing attention to the large 
proportion of his army that was now committed to Egypt, to the possibility 
of having to send another force to the Dardanelles, and to the lack of 
dependability of the Neapolitan troops of the king. He doubted the accuracy 
of the information about the strength of French troops in Naples, and the 
French in any case could always draw on a large strategic reserve in the north. 
Anyway of what use was a plan whose secret was bound to be betrayed by the 
queen and her entourage, he asked?? Rebuffed, the court acted on its own, 
and dispatched Hesse’s army to defeat at Mileto. 
The reactions of Fox and Moore to the latter were, predictably, “I told you 
so.” Moore wrote privately to Fox: “Thus has ended Circello’s and the 
Queen’s expedition in the only manner it could reasonably be expected to 
end.” He was sorry that a brave man like Hesse had been employed on what 

<<<PDFPAGE 65>>>
The Court, Drummond, and the Generals 
61 
he called “so bad a corvée,” but “Fools, I believe, never learn—so that we 
cannot hope that those who direct the Sicilian government will profit by this 
check.” And Fox could not resist telling Drummond that the Neapolitan 
cavalry had behaved even worse than their infantry. As for the masse, perhaps 
even the queen would now acknowledge their uselessness—they had not fired 
a shot in the whole campaign, and their vengeance had as usual been directed 
against their defenseless countrymen. 
!° 
Two months later, Fox received from Castlereagh instructions drafted on 
21 May ordering him to prepare to land in Calabria or some other part of 
Italy. This might, in Castlereagh’s opinion, lead on to the conquest of Naples 
and even its permanent occupation, but at worst it would furnish a useful 
diversion and perhaps destroy a French corps as well.!! It was clear that 
Castlereagh, who had been prompted by Drummond, did not realize the size 
of Ferdinand’s army, fondly imagining that it and half the British force (the 
proportion he instructed Fox to hold in readiness) amounted to 20,000 men. 
Fox had to point out his error to him, at the same time emphasizing the poor 
fighting quality of the Neapolitans, as revealed so recently at Mileto. If he 
did, he wrote, take an army to Italy, he would have to leave behind 2,000 
men to secure the fortresses of Messina and Syracuse, and that would give 
him a strength of 8,000 (half the army being still in Egypt) to confront a 
French army of 20,000. He remained without any cavalry and, even if joined 
by the king’s forces, he would still be outnumbered by the French. 1? 
Over plans for yet another expedition (Mileto took place at the end of 
May) and over attitudes toward the court, the disagreement between Drum- 
mond and the generals came to a head in July 1807 when Fox went home and 
the outspoken Moore took over the post of British commander. Ever since he 
had changed his mind over the attitude of the court, Drummond had tried to 
persuade the generals that the French party no longer controlled it, and that 
since the king’s power was limited by noble and corporate privileges, many 
abuses existing in Sicily were outside the power of the king to remedy. He 
was sure the queen’s influence was now minimal, and protested that he 
(Drummond) had been unfairly stigmatized for “weakness, imbecility and 
lack of steadiness.”!3 Fox in his turn had represented to Drummond that the 
recent appointment of the king’s eldest son to be inspector general of the 
royal army and of Saint Clair to be one of his aides certainly did not 
correspond with any loss of power by the queen. As to the barons, Fox was 
unsure whether giving them more power would not be preferable to what he 
characteristically described as “the miserable weak government of the reign- 
ing family.”!4 
Drummond continued to believe that the way to better Sicily’s lot was by a 
reform of the constitution, initiated by the king, and that he (Drummond) 
could use his influence to bring such a reform about. He certainly did not 
want such a change effected by a military coup, a course the generals were

known to favor. “I would desire for many reasons that the seat of legislature 
should rather be a Sicilian parliament than a British camp.”!§ The generals 
favored stopping the subsidies until British demands were met for radical 
reform of Sicilian institutions, but such a recourse, in Drummond’s view, 
would never produce the results desired. He thought the king and queen far 
too proud to yield to menaces of that sort, and meanwhile the royal army 
would fall to pieces through lack of pay.!° 
In March Drummond had visited Messina to try to present his case in 
person, but the generals had given him a chilly reception. Moore had talked a 
little wildly about dispatching the queen to Trieste, and Drummond had 
come to the conclusion that he understood little of Sicilian affairs.!7 Moore 
for his part despised Drummond, whom he thought “the weakest and silliest 
of men,” a “false, unprincipled, mean fellow ... who at first bred all the 
mischief with the queen and who still, as far as he is able, will endeavour to 
foment it.” Moore was certainly not renowned for his tolerance of civilians 
and politicians, and his strictures concerning Drummond recall curious 
echoes of his condemnation twelve years earlier of Sir Gilbert Elliot’s conduct 
in Corsica. Sir Arthur Paget, the diplomat and former minister at Palermo, 
who passed through Sicily in July and who listened to both sides in the 
dispute, concluded that they were irreconcilable. 18 
The navy also was drawn in to the quarrel. Captain Thornborough com- 
manding the squadron, entrusted with the defense of Sicily, was approached 
by the court to convoy to Italy a royal expeditionary force. In private talks he 
had had with Drummond, the latter had appeared to agree with him that 
such an action would only divide his squadron and subject it to unacceptable 
risk. Yet when Thornborough met the queen in the presence of Drummond, 
the latter totally unexpectedly turned round and spoke in support of her case. 
“This sudden change 
. 
. 
. has astonished me,” wrote Thornborough, “at 
present I know not how to act with such a man. 
. 
. 
. [I] am convinced he 
would wish to throw all the blame on the Army and Navy.”!? Drummond 
was certainly devious, and his lack of frankness in dealing with Canning over 
the question of the Spanish regency and Ferdinand’s claim to it for one of his 
sons was a year later to lead to his recall. 
The person, attitude, and influence of the queen remained at the center of 
the dispute. She herself put her views to Paget during his stay in Sicily. She 
complained that it;almost seemed that the British looked on Sicily as a 
conquered country. The generals complained that the court did not recruit, 
yet how, she asked, could it hope to do so, when the British offered higher 
rates of pay. The court had 6,000 regular troops ready to embark at an 
instant, yet the generals treated her plans with contempt and refused to 
expend British blood in removing a tyrant from the throne of Naples. Those 
who accused the court of betrayal were, she said, nothing but a crowd of 
malcontents who had taken refuge at Messina and got the generals to listen to
