<<<PDFPAGE 69>>>
The Court, Drummond, and the Generals 
65 
them.?° It was difficult to argue rationally with her. She had in the winter of 
1807/8 been involved in one of her many intrigues, this time with the 
Russian ambassador, with the idea of a Russian garrison being sent to Sicily 
to replace the British. Tatischeff, anxious to discredit Britain, had according 
to Drummond been spreading rumors that he was able to mediate between 
the court and Napoleon, to the end that the king should recover Naples, 
provided the British got out of Sicily. At Drummond’s peremptory request, 
which earned him a rebuke from Canning, Tatischeff was ordered to leave 
Palermo, but the incident did little to better relations between the Sicilian 
court and the British.?! 
Russia, of course, since the Treaty of Tilsit, when Czar Alexander joined 
hands with Napoleon, had become an enemy of Britain. It was therefore, in 
British eyes, disgraceful that the queen should be seeking the help of the 
Russians. There had already been an incident in January, when Circello had 
been under pressure from Drummond to order the seizure of a Russian 
frigate that had earlier arrived in Palermo harbor, before war was declared 
between Britain and Russia. Thornborough, acting on Admiralty instruc- 
tions, had wanted to attack the frigate, but the Sicilian government had at 
first demurred. It was finally induced by Drummond to declare a state of war 
with Russia, and the frigate’s captain agreed to surrender to the Sicilians, 
though not to the British, thus defusing an awkward situation that had 
touched the quick of Sicilian pride.?2 
The Sicilian court was particularly sensitive about attacks on its loyalty to 
Britain and on its general competence, including those made in the British 
press. Castelcicala, the minister in London, was so incensed at an article 
published in February 1808 in the pages of the Whig Morning Chronicle that 
he persuaded the British government to start proceedings against Perry, the 
proprietor. The matter was eventually settled, though not until after more 
than a year, by Perry making an abject apology. The paper, he said, was 
merely anxious to draw the British public’s attention to the danger to which 
British troops were exposed in the current Sicilian situation, and he had no 
wish to interfere in the way an independent state was governed, much less to 
try to sow dissension between the Sicilian king and his subjects.*° But six 
months later there was more trouble, when Leckie’s “Historical Survey” was 
published and brought to Castelcicala’s attention. In it Leckie did not mince 
his words. He described the Sicilian government as exhibiting “all the 
weakness and degeneracy” associated with the Byzantine Empire “in its last 
stages of degradation,” and he urged the ministers to undertake the much- 
needed reforms in Sicily, since by propping up an unpopular regime the 
British themselves incurred popular hatred. He attacked the attitude of the 
queen who clearly had no love for the British, the duplicity of the Palermo 
court, and the pro-French sympathies of at least one minister, and demanded 
that Britain interfere in Sicily’s internal affairs for “pressing motives of self- 

<<<PDFPAGE 70>>>
66 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
defence.” Castelcicala wrote to Canning, describing the survey as grossly 
insulting—an appalling libel on the king, the queen, and the whole Sicilian 
government, and again asked for the author to be prosecuted and punished 
with the full vigor of the law.?4 And matters had been made no easier, in 
trying to establish relations of trust between the British and Sicilian govern- 
ments, by an episode concerning Spain, which involved the Sicilian royal 
family. 
pee as the news had reached Palermo that Ferdinand VII of Spain, 
nephew of Ferdinand II of Sicily, had been forced by Napoleon to abdicate, 
that the Spanish royal family were now prisoners in France, and that a revolt 
had broken out by the Spanish people against French occupation, great 
excitement prevailed at Palermo. By the will of Charles III, the father of the 
Sicilian king and of the unpopular Charles IV of Spain (who had recently 
yielded his throne to his son), if the male line of Charles IV should fail, 
Ferdinand the Sicilian king would become heir to the throne of Spain. 
Drummond took up the cause of the king (Ferdinand III of Sicily), 
sponsored his claim to the Spanish regency, and managed to persuade the 
queen that it had the support of the British government. (Needless to say, 
King Ferdinand of Sicily was not himself greatly interested, and Drummond 
felt he had cause to complain that the king did not go to Spain in person.)?° 
Yet Drummond was acting on his own initiative, not only without authority 
but directly against Canning’s stated wishes. This was to have unfortunate 
consequences, not just for Anglo-Sicilian relations but also for Drummond 
personally. 
Writing on 31 May 1808 and a week later on 7 June, Drummond told 
Canning he was sure that if the Spanish monarch, as well as his sons, were 
excluded from the throne of Spain, then Britain must wish to see on that 
throne someone who was her friend and ally. He thought the prospects for 
old Spain were gloomy, but that Prince Leopold could be sent to England 
and helped to a throne in Spanish America. At this stage, he assured Can- 
ning, he had breathed no word to the king or his court that he was thinking 
along these lines, but wanted to learn what the ministry thought.?° Yet, 
without waiting for a reply, Drummond committed himself at once (and by 
implication the British government) to sponsoring a Sicilian regency in 
Spain. Having toyed first with the idea of sending the hereditary prince to 
Spain, while Leopold went via England to America to claim the regency on 
behalf of his father, he got the court to agree that Leopold, in the company of 
various advisers, would be sent to Gibraltar to sound out the Spanish. To this 
end Drummond had already advanced £10,000 of British money toward the 
expenses of the voyage and the cost of Leopold’s stay in Gibraltar.27 
Drummond’s letter of 31 May and his second one of 7 June crossed with a 
dispatch to him from Canning. This warned him not to encourage the court 
to make any claim to the Spanish regency—a claim for which the backing of 

<<<PDFPAGE 71>>>
The Court, Drummond, and the Generals 
67 
Britain had already been canvassed by Castelcicala.28 And when Canning 
learnt of Drummond’s views and his assurance that he had not yet communi- 
cated them to the court, he instructed Drummond on no account to lend 
support to any such claim. The question of choosing a regent for Spain must 
be a matter for the Spanish Cortes, and any attempt by the court of Sicily to 
claim the regency without being invited was bound to arouse a hostile 
response. The last thing the British government wanted was to offend the 
Spanish patriots.?? 
But things had now gone too far for Drummond to see how he could 
retract his support, and Circello had been given his written assurances that 
what he was doing was backed by his government. The queen had agreed 
that Leopold should leave for Spain as soon as possible in the company of her 
favorite Saint Clair, the Duke of Orleans, Prince Cassaro, the Duke of Ascoli 
and Tommasi, a legal adviser of the government. The whole party was to be 
conveyed by the British warship Thunderer, which Ball in Malta had agreed 
to supply, without having been shown the passenger list. The T/underer duly 
sailed in September and arrived at Gibraltar, only to find that the party was 
not permitted to land. Prince Leopold was kept three days on board until he 
agreed to return to Palermo and abandon any attempt to make contact with 
the Spanish Junta and the patriot party.°° 
Canning had naturally been outraged when he discovered what Drum- 
mond had done, disregarding his clear instructions. The British government 
had been placed in a quite invidious position. It must either approve Drum- 
mond’s actions and so appear to be interfering in Spain’s strictly internal 
concerns, or disown the whole Sicilian démarche and thus risk causing 
offense at Palermo. It decided to take the latter course, and Drummond was 
told that he was recalled.? 
Drummond comes somewhat discreditably out of the whole wretched, 
botched affair. He tried to excuse himself to Canning by making out, quite 
untruthfully, that Circello had been the moving force in having Leopold sent 
to Spain, and that he, Drummond, had merely connived, and then only in his 
private capacity. His excuse for the failure to tell Captain Ball that the 
Thunderer was to carry a royal prince was a thoroughly unconvincing one; 
over the sending of the Marquis Saint Clair, he attempted to shift all the 
blame on the queen—“another proof of her dirty cunning which fools have 
mistaken for sense and acuteness”—though he certainly knew that Saint Clair 
was going. 
The situation was not improved by a protest, lodged soon after by Cas- 
telcicala at the action the British took at Gibraltar. When he tried to argue 
that Leopold’s party should have received the same hospitality that British 
forces had received in Sicily, Canning became extremely angry. “Tt can hardly 
be necessary to you to point out the inaptitude of this illustration.” British 
troops, he added, had been invited to Sicily, and then in order to safeguard 

<<<PDFPAGE 72>>>
68 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
the island, while Leopold’s presence at Gibraltar was scarcely required for 
defense of that fortress. Nor had his reason for coming there been to look for 
safety and British protection. It was all very well to take a “high tone” about 
the right of the king of Sicily to the regency of Spain being “clear and 
incontrovertible” and to assert that such a claim was the only way to avoid 
civil war, but the evidence for this was not forthcoming, nor was there any 
for the claim by the king to devolve the regency onto his son. If the united 
voice of Spain had called for Ferdinand II or Leopold, that indeed would 
alter the case, but the facts proclaimed that this was not so. The court at 
Palermo, Canning went on, must have been aware that Drummond had 
acted without any precise instructions, and had he not connived personally at 
the action taken by the Sicilian court, the British government would be 
entitled to take grave offense at what had been done. The arrival of a prince 
of Sicily aboard a British vessel before a British fortress was liable to the 
gravest misconstruction, and the king was advised to stand very clear of any 
suspicion that he had condoned it.?% 
What Canning was saying in so many words was that it had all been the 
fault of the queen. And this was underlined in a secret letter he sent the same 
day to Castelcicala in which he protested in the strongest terms about the 
inclusion of the Marquis Saint Clair in the party that had accompanied 
Leopold. Saint Clair he described, without actually naming him, as someone 
whom the British government had been trying over the past two or three 
years to have removed from positions of trust and had only refrained from 
pressing its case out of a sense of delicacy. Hence the government had been 
amazed to discover that this man had been sent to visit a British fortress in 
company with Prince Leopold—“a Frenchman and one whose near connec- 
tions are notoriously established at the throne of Bonaparte.” He must, wrote 
Canning, be sent home at once, and on no account would he be allowed to 
pass through Gibraltar into Spain.34 
The upshot of the whole affair had been to further injure Sicilian pride, or 
at least the pride of the royal family, and to make the British yet more 
suspicious of the loyalty of the court at Palermo. 
Moore, once he had assumed command, obtained two interviews with the 
queen, at one of which the king was present, in which Moore did his best to 
persuade her that he had the court’s best interests at heart and wanted 
sincerely to do all in his power to regain for it the kingdom of Naples. If it 
had not been possible to agree on the feasibility of plans, this was for very 
good military reasons. As he told Paget, the king and queen thought that 
because the French had recently sent some troops to Corfu from Naples, the 
whole of that kingdom now lay open, and that it only needed a force to land 
for the country to fall like a ripe fruit. It would, he commented, be time 
enough to land at Naples when the French had gone—“the kingdom will not 
run away.” The largest force that could be raised in Sicily, 8,000 British and 

<<<PDFPAGE 73>>>
The Court, Drummond, and the Generals 
69 
7,000 Neapolitans—the latter ill-paid, ill-led and disaffected—would not be 
under a unified command, could certainly never beat the French, and would 
not impress the local people. As for it acting as a diversion, it would do 
nothing to distract Napoleon, who “knows that the fate of Italy will be 
decided on the Vistula.”35 
Moore, with good reason, was very doubtful if he had succeeded in 
convincing the queen. He felt that she so detested the British, he seriously 
wondered if her real aim was not to get the British out of Sicily and to see 
their army destroyed on the mainland. The king, it is true, did eventually 
permit him to inspect the royal armed forces, whose condition he found 
indifferent—about equal to that of the London Volunteers but with “far less 
discipline and without their zeal and personal bravery”; but he was not 
granted a unified command, the king insisting that the time for this would be 
when a joint force had landed in Italy.3° 
Moore was convinced there was no remedy for an unsatisfactory situation 
until the role of British diplomat in Sicily was merged with that of military 
commander, as it had once been very briefly when General Fox had filled 
both parts; for, as things were, the British army had no influence whatever at 
court. The queen, he was sure, was guilty of treachery, and surrounded 
herself with foreign émigrés who were certainly no friends to Britain. The 
only way to maintain Sicily was to take over its government, for if it were left 
to the king and queen “they will lose themselves, the island and us.” If the 
French were to land, the island would be gone, the royal troops probably 
would desert and the local inhabitants, losing confidence, would offer the 
enemy no resistance. All the British army could hope to do would be to shut 
itself up in its fortresses and await relief from the British navy. It would 
clearly therefore be prudent to retain enough transports to take away the 
army.37 Moore had decided to make Castrogiovanni (today and in ancient 
times called Enna), set on a well-nigh impregnable hill in the center of the 
island, his pivot of resistance, leaving only enough men to garrison the 
principal ports. 
This very pessimistic analysis, and Moore’s call for 12,000 more troops, 
decided the cabinet to recall him. The Treaty of Tilsit had left Britain on her 
own again and had put paid to any hopes of using Sicily as a base for offense, 
so Moore and 8,000 of his best troops were ordered back to protect the home 
shores and to act as a mobile strategic reserve outside the Mediterranean. The 
cabinet had been put in a position in which it must either accept Moore’s 
views or recall him and replace him by someone else. It chose the latter, for 
reasons that Castlereagh made clear in a letter he wrote to Hawkesbury: 
Unless we are Berea to act up to his principles of setting 
the govern- 
ment aside, which I certainly am not ... I should not 
despair of his 
obedience, if of his conversion, to any system we might lay down for him, 

<<<PDFPAGE 74>>>
70 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
but unfortunately the Sicilian government know the extent of his opinions, 
which deprive him of all influence, and make him a less effective instru- 
ment for enforcing any measures of authority short of the extreme one of 
subversion.38 
Bunbury found it incredible that at the moment when Sicily seemed to be 
threatened with the greatest danger, Moore and so many excellent troops 
should be removed to another theater, leaving behind to guard the island a 
force of only 9,000 men. Russia was now an ally of France, and Marmont 
had in the Adriatic area a disposable force of 40,000 that could be trans- 
ported by French ships at Venice and by Russian warships at Corfu and 
Trieste. And at Toulon and at Genoa there were French troops as well as the 
ships to carry them, while their fleet could easily be joined by a Spanish one 
from Cartagena. Ministers in London had taken a risk, one that was nearly to 
lose them Sicily, and as soon as Castlereagh realized that the French had 
taken over Corfu and that the threat to Sicily was real, he decided to reinforce 
the latter.3? 
Moore’s replacement in Sicily was the same John Stuart who had been 
there before, prior to the arrival of Henry Fox, the victor of Maida and friend 
of the court. Perhaps to ingratiate himself with the ministers, he had told 
them that he considered that Sicily could be held with a garrison of only 
10,000, given sufficient naval support. Fox and Moore, on the other hand, 
insisted it needed 26,000. Castlereagh decided to compromise, increased the 
garrison to 16,000 by the early months of 1808 and, ignoring the possibility 
of it having to be evacuated, ordered home the available transports.*° 

<<<PDFPAGE 75>>>
5 
Threats of Invasion and Counterinvasion 
ties fear of a French invasion of Sicily continued to preoccupy Stuart 
throughout the course of 1808. During the winter of 1807/8 the threat 
of a French assault was a real one, for Napoleon, seeing Sicily denuded, albeit 
temporarily, by Moore’s departure together with 8,000 of the best British 
troops, ordered the capture of Reggio and Scilla and preparations for full- 
scale invasion. ! 
French success in capturing these two strongpoints was due to the British 
navy’s inability to keep station in the winter storms and intercept convoys of 
small craft carrying munitions from Naples to Pizzo. British reverses were 
also the result of the French success in capturing (perhaps through treachery) 
in the straits of Messina four gunboats, armed with twenty-four-pounders— 
guns that the French found extremely useful in reducing Scilla’s fortifications. 
Sherbrooke, the general in temporary command of the British forces at 
Messina, ordered the withdrawal of the Scilla garrison after a siege of seven 
weeks, not having sufficient forces to make a diversion to relieve the fortress. 
Scilla fell on 23 February 1808, but by then Napoleon had changed his plans. 
Originally, he had ordered Ganteaume, commanding the Rochefort and 
Toulon squadrons, to sail to Naples, embark 9,000 men there, and then land 
them around Messina at the same time that Régnier was to put across a 
further 9,000 from Reggio and Scilla. But he now felt that Russia’s threat to 
the Turkish Empire in the Mediterranean had become of far-more-pressing 
importance than the need to deprive the British of Sicily. He decided 
therefore to reinforce Corfu with the object of staking out a claim to the 
Turkish provinces in Albania and Greece. He instructed Joseph, who at that 
moment was busy preparing to enter Sicily, to regard Corfu as of greater 
importance: “Sicily’s fate is known and sealed, Corfu’s is quite unknown and 
hangs in the balance . 
. 
. in the present state of Europe the greatest misfor- 
tune that could befall me would be the loss of Corfu.”* And Ganteaume was 
not to call at Naples, but instead to make directly for Corfu. When part of his 
fleet under Admiral Cosmao became detached in the course of a storm and 
put into the port of Taranto, Cosmao sent to Joseph for instructions, but 
Joseph, though longing to invade Sicily, did not dare disobey his brother, 
and Cosmao was therefore dispatched at once to rejoin Ganteaume’s fleet at 
Corfu. Napoleon, now having learnt that the British had been reinforced in 
71 

<<<PDFPAGE 76>>>
HDD 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
Sicily and that a powerful British fleet had arrived in the Mediterranean, and 
having no wish for Ganteaume’s ships to become engaged with a superior 
force, ordered them to return to Toulon. It is true that Joseph still had a force 
ready amounting to nearly 8,000 men, though of these 3,000 made up the 
garrison holding down Lower Calabria; but without the munitions and 
transports promised that would have arrived with Ganteaume’s fleet, he felt 
that he could take on the British with any reasonable chance of success. When 
Napoleon’s interest soon after shifted to the theaters of Spain and Portugal, 
Joseph was recalled in July to take over the Spanish throne.* 
During the summer of 1808 Stuart continued to act offensively against the 
Neapolitan coast and kept up pressure on the enemy ports, although he 
decided to terminate the raids by Calabrian refugees that were organized by 
the Marquis Carbone. These raids, he had been assured by General Sher- 
brooke, were merely pillaging expeditions that could only result in bringing 
discredit on the British army in Sicily. 
In September he was able to report a most successful combined operation 
against the small harbor of Diamante. A detachment of British troops had 
been landed and with the help of two British sloops had captured thirty-eight 
enemy vessels, including two large Neapolitan gunboats.° During the re- 
mainder of that month he continued to make hostile demonstrations with 
troop transports, as if planning a landing, but eventually he had to call these 
off for lack of an adequate naval escort.”7 But in any case he was now 
confronted with a far more resourceful and daring opponent than Joseph 
Bonaparte had ever been, and he himself was forced onto the defensive. 
For Joseph’s successor in the kingdom of Naples, the daring, ambitious 
Joachim Murat, was quite determined to undertake the conquest of Sicily as 
soon as he could. As a preliminary, he captured Capri during October 1808, 
describing this success to Napoleon as a good augury for the seizure of 
Sicily. Although the British had not intended to surrender the island so 
easily—in fact the relief force arrived too late (having been driven back by a 
storm) and the many desertions from the Corsican regiment, once the deed of 
surrender had been signed, had proved, to say the least, somewhat discon- 
certing—they did not regard its loss as serious. The cost of defending it had 
been excessive and it had served very little purpose except as a base for the 
queen’s spies and agents.? The loss did however alarm the court, who, 
through Drummond, complained to Collingwood about the lack of naval 
protection. Collingwood was impatient with such complaints. He told 
Mulgrave that no important point on the coast of Sicily would be left 
undefended and his ships were constantly on station to watch the approaches 
to the island. The court, he thought, seemed to imagine that when those 
ships were out of sight, the defense of Sicily had been abandoned, and yet 
when they were seen in port, the enemy had been left to do as he pleased. “It 
would,” he commented feelingly, “require a squadron in every port to 

<<<PDFPAGE 77>>>
Threats of Invasion and Counterinvasion 
73 
remove all their apprehensions.” However, to calm the fears of the court, and 
particularly those of the queen, who still apprehended a British betrayal, he 
detailed Admiral Martin with three ships-of-the-line to base himself on 
Palermo harbor.!° 
Throughout October and November Murat continued to prepare for 
invasion, assuring Napoleon that all he required was 6,000 troops and naval 
support; and he argued that the time was now ripe, since the strength of the 
British army in Sicily had been reduced to a mere 4,000 [sec]. Clearly, Murat 
had swallowed whole the disinformation put out by Napoleon to alarm the 
government in London and deflect its attention from Portugal to Sicily. He 
had ordered false reports to be placed in various Dutch newspapers about the 
strength of the British in Sicily, a landing by 30,000 French, and the exodus 
of the king from the island. But by March it must have dawned even on 
Murat that Sicily’s invasion had been postponed indefinitely, orders having 
reached him to send troops northward to meet the threat presented by 
Austria.!1 
Meanwhile there had been various pressures on the British to use their 
forces in Sicily to support a national Italian uprising of the kind that had just 
been witnessed in Spain. A Neapolitan called Biancamano had recently got in 
touch with Drummond, making out that he was the spokesman for the chiefs 
of all the parties in Naples. He alleged that these chiefs were in league with 
others throughout the whole length of Italy, and that they looked to the 
British in Sicily to give them material support.'? The government in 
London, being apprised of this, instructed Stuart to use his discretion. But 
any help that he might give must be used not for some attempt at the 
temporary recovery of Naples but for a purpose “likely to lead to important 
and extensive combinations,” or in other words to a major effort to liberate 
Italy from the French.!? A month later he received instructions that were a 
little more positive. He was urged to explore the possibilities of giving 
assistance to the Italians, and for this purpose he was allowed to retain the 
transports he had been told to send home. He was warned however that any 
help given must be dependent on the success achieved by the patriot armies 
of Spain.!4 
Through Lowe, when commanding the Capri garrison, Stuart had made 
various inquiries as to how far the local inhabitants would support the arrival 
of an army of 10,600. He contemplated the possibility of a landing at some 
point along the coast between Terracina and the Gulf of Spezia, since any 
landing in the kindgom of Naples would have little appeal to other Italians. 
Colonel Bunbury, corresponding with Lowe, pressed for a landing where the 
French were weak, and that meant the Roman states or Tuscany. They had 
32,000 men, it was true, but scattered over so extensive an area, sickly, and 
locked up in garrisons, they would not be able to concentrate them to meet a 
threat posed far from Naples. But he did admit to serious doubts about the 

<<<PDFPAGE 78>>>
74 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
whole subject of Italian resistance. “I fear there are no leaders,” he wrote, 
“nor any one common or durable bond, which could ensure to us the united 
and continued aid of the Tuscans, Romans, Neapolitans and Piedmontese.”!5 
Collingwood was also very skeptical about there existing a spirit of pa- 
triotism on which to build a war of resistance. “There is no stuff to work on 
there—the people are licentious, the nobles are unprincipled. It is a superior 
army alone that can effect a change or maintain it.” The Italian people, in his 
view, did not possess “the Spanish spirit.” 
!¢ 
Stuart by now had become distrustful of Biancamano and his claims, 
especially since he seemed to be linked with slippery characters like Prince 
Moliterno, the condottiere who kept in with both sides. He told Castlereagh 
that he did not doubt that many in Italy were discontented, but he had yet to 
hear of a cohesive party, a concerted plan of operations, or any measures 
generally agreed upon. Much depended on the future actions of the Span- 
iards and the Austrians, for his force of 9,000 (all he could spare) would 
never free Italy on its own. In the kingdom of Naples the upper classes would 
be slow to desert their French masters by whom they had been courted and 
rewarded, and the people as a whole still had bitter memories of the royal 
vengeance in 1799. An amnesty guaranteed by Britain would be “an indis- 
pensable prelude” to any attempt to recover Naples. And he tried to convince 
the king and queen that he could not undertake any move to the continent 
until he received unequivocal proof that the Italian peoples were organized 
for war.17 
Murat, through his agents, was well aware of the British involvement with 
potential rebels and he kept Napoleon informed accordingly, as also did 
Eugene, the viceroy of Italy. He knew of the British correspondence with 
patriot leaders in Bologna and Venice in regard to placing Italy under one 
constitutional sovereign, and of 12,000 who had pledged themselves to take 
up arms in the Roman states, but he sensibly and realistically attached little 
importance to all this and assured Napoleon that a general uprising 
throughout Italy was an impossibility.18 
Castlereagh, as soon as he realized that Italy was no duplicate of Spain, 
decided that troops in Sicily could be better employed in Catalonia. In 
October, therefore, he instructed Stuart to prepare to dispatch a force to 
Spain, without realizing the damaging effect that this would have on morale 
at the court. The latter was already sufficiently alarmed by the recent loss of 
Capri to Murat, the movement of troops into Lower Calabria, and the 
seeming absence of naval support, to which reference has already been made. 
Stuart delayed acting on Castlereagh’s instructions, if only because it seemed 
useless to send a force of less than 7,000 to Spain, and that would leave in 
Sicily fewer than the minimum of 10,000 troops guaranteed by the treaty of 
alliance. The king made it clear he would never agree to such a reduction in 
British strength, since he thought it was bound to appear to Murat an open 

<<<PDFPAGE 79>>>
Threats of Invasion and Counterinvasion 
15 
invitation for him to invade.!9 Stuart’s arguments persuaded Castlereagh, 
who received his dispatches in May 1809. By that time Austria had reentered 
the war, and Castlereagh, in writing to Stuart to approve his not sending 
forces to Spain, expressed the hope he would now begin to make himself 
useful in Italy.2° 
The vague wording of Castlereagh’s dispatch exemplified the cabinet’s lack 
of a plan for cooperation with the Austrian armies. The Austrians themselves 
had since the spring been trying through the offices of Count Sallier de la 
Tour to coordinate plans with the British command and with the courts at 
Palermo and Cagliari for joint operations in northern Italy. La Tour, who 
was a Piedmontese subject serving as an officer in the Austrian army, had 
arrived in Palermo on 31 March. He had instructions from the Austrian 
government to obtain the support of the king and the British—the latter 
providing, it was hoped, if not troops, then transports and naval escort. The 
Archduke John, who was to command the Austrian army in the north of 
Italy, impressed on La Tour the need for speed—his own campaign against 
the French was due to start on 9 April—and what he wanted was for forces 
from Sicily, and if possible from Sardinia also, to join his own army in the 
Lombard plain or in the valley of the Po.?? 
The queen, as might have been expected, was a great enthusiast for the 
plan, and she even got the king to agree to Stuart being given the supreme 
command. Stuart agreed, in principle only, to taking some action in Italy, but 
would not commit himself to details until he knew more of the Austrian 
movements and until he had received instructions from London. The rele- 
vant instructions (if such they can be called) did not reach him until July. In 
any case he felt incapable of doing more than causing a diversion with the 
limited number of troops he had, even if the French had withdrawn their 
main army and the mass of Italians were “ripe for revolt.”?7 Collingwood, on 
the other hand, was a firm supporter of the Austrian case. He sent three 
frigates to reinforce Martin and urged Stuart to take advantage of the dearth 
of French forces along the coasts. If Austria were to fail now, and the fate of 
Italy depended on her, he would not wish Britain to be responsible, and he 
could not see how the allied cause could be helped by the temporary posses- 
sion of Naples or of some island in its bay.?° 
Stuart finally agreed with the court to make a double landing in the south 
of Italy, 15,000 troops going to Calabria, and another 2,000 to Naples itself, 
in the hope of achieving a coup de main there. But when he learned of the 
Austrian defeats that had taken place in Bavaria, he suspended all prepara- 
tions for sailing—this despite violent protests by the queen, who refused to 
believe that Vienna had fallen. The troops had been embarked on 26 May, 
but Stuart told La Tour that they could not leave until the situation in the 
north was clarified. The queen meanwhile bombarded La Tour with messages 
urging immediate action and with plans for 3,000 Sardinians to join with the 

<<<PDFPAGE 80>>>
76 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
British in the seizure of Genoa.?* Stuart’s hesitancy is understandable, given 
the memories of his government’s displeasure at his raising of the masse in 
1806, his instructions not to intervene in Italy unless there was a plan for a 
general liberation, and the fact that he had not yet received permission to 
withhold his troops from Spain. He was also in fear of a mutiny among the 
officers of his army, many of whom were highly critical of his competence to 
lead them in action and therefore thoroughly disapproved of the whole idea 
of the expedition.?5 
At last, feeling he must do something with an army of 16,000 lying idle, 
and convincing himself that the Austrian reverses were not as severe as 
French reports made out, he decided to capture the island of Ischia and to use 
it as a base for further operations. This was a course that the court approved, 
though Collingwood considered it a waste of effort.2© The expedition on 
board included a large contingent of the court entourage, including Prince 
Leopold, Circello, Tommasi, Saint Clair, Ascoli, and General Nunziante, and 
they insisted on making landfalls at Policastro and Sapri en route in anticipa- 
tion of picking up numerous recruits for the expedition. These landings 
proved a complete farce and showed only too clearly that the local people 
were not at all ready to welcome the Bourbons. By the time the convoy 
arrived off Ischia, Stuart had lost all confidence in the successful outcome of 
his enterprise and would probably have returned to Sicily at once had the 
Ischia garrison not surrendered so easily.27 
Landing at Ischia on 25 June, Stuart was soon in control of the island and 
of its neighbor Procida, but instead of assaulting Naples forthwith, he wasted 
time in prolonged discussions about the nature of the new administration, 
the court having sent along Prince Leopold, who suddenly proclaimed 
himself viceroy. Murat had taken immediate steps to strengthen the defenses 
of his capital, and Stuart realized too late that the moment for attacking it had 
passed.?8 
In the south, a force of 3,000 men under General Mackenzie had tried and 
failed to capture the little fortress of Scilla, having been incorrectly informed 
that the French had withdrawn from Lower Calabria. When they did with- 
draw, shortly after, they blew up the fort and all the brass cannon assembled 
there for the assault on Sicily, something that drew down on the general 
responsible the wrath of both Murat and Napoleon, the possession of Scilla 
being considered by the latter as indispensable for an invasion of Sicily.2? 
Stuart, under pressure from Bunbury and Collingwood to land troops in 
the Roman states, was on the point of dispatching 4,000 to capture the port 
of Civita Vecchia when two new factors changed his mind. One was the news 
in the Moniteur that the Austrians had been defeated at Wagram, the other a 
warning from Collingwood about the state of the French fleet in Toulon. 
France had now got in the Mediterranean the largest fleet she had ever 
mustered since the signing of the peace of Amiens, and this fleet was now 

<<<PDFPAGE 81>>>
Threats of Invasion and Counterinvasion 
77 
lying in Toulon’s outer harbor as if awaiting orders to sail. Collingwood 
warned that he was unable to guarantee the protection of Ischia, or indeed of 
Palermo itself, for if the French fleet got out from Toulon, it might well be in 
the Bay of Naples before he knew that it had sailed, were the weather to favor 
the enemy. In view of all this, Stuart decided he must, if ignominiously, retire 
forthwith to Sicily, abandoning the captured islands that Collingwood had 
never considered worth taking.3° 
Back in Sicily, the Count La Tour continued to press for a joint expedition 
to land on the northwest coast of Italy, and in this he received the support of 
the court (disgusted at Stuart’s withdrawal from Ischia) and the British 
ministers in Palermo and Cagliari. But, wisely, Stuart remained hesitant. It 
may be, as La Tour suggested, that Stuart possessed only physical courage 
and was frightened of making any decision for which he could be held 
personally responsible; but given the decisive defeat of the Austrians, a 
landing in the north would have courted disaster and exposed Sicily to 
immediate invasion.?! 
La Tour then propounded a new plan. It involved 6,000 Austrian troops 
being sent to Sicily to join the king, as a bait to tempt him back into Italy. 
The idea had great appeal to the queen, since it seemed a way of ridding the 
court of its utter dependence on the British, and with 4,000 troops the 
Sardinians were offering, there could soon be a force of 10,000 in Sicily 
completely under the control of the court. The whole thing was a fantasy, 
based on Vienna’s erroneous conception that the allied forces in Sicily were 
made up of 5,000 British auxiliaries under the direct command of the king, 
and a royal army of 15,000. In any case the La Tour mission and schemes for 
sending armies to Italy were brought to a very speedy conclusion when the 
Austrians signed the Peace of Vienna.?? 
What Stuart was persuaded to do, at the thrice-repeated request of Col- 
lingwood, was to send a small force of 1,700 to capture the southern Ionian 
islands. Now that the French looked on the point of moving into Illyria, 
Collingwood was persuaded it was important to prevent them from getting a 
hold on Greece and threatening the existence of the Turkish Empire. Posses- 
sion of the islands, he hoped, would act as an effective check to the French 
and would simultaneously afford Sicily correspondingly greater security. 
During the first week in October British forces from Sicily captured the 
islands of Zante, Ithaca, Cephalonia, and Cerigo, and shortly after, Santa 
Maura. Stuart was then pressed by his subordinates to authorize an assault on 
Corfu, but because of his instructions from London he had no choice but to 
resist this pressure.33 Wellesley, the secretary of state, positively forbade an 
attack on Corfu since far too many troops would be needed not only to 
capture the fortress but to hold it. The policy of the British government was 
to act defensively in the Mediterranean while the struggle being waged in 
Spain and Portugal was employing so much of the British army. The gar- 

<<<PDFPAGE 82>>>
78 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
risons in the Ionian islands were to be reduced to 1,300, the balance being 
brought back to Sicily. And if Sicily were ever threatened, still more of the 
garrisons were to be recalled. The security of Sicily and Malta, as Liverpool 
emphasized to Stuart, was the primary object in that theater, and the five 
southern Ionian islands were considered to be of only secondary impor- 
tance.3+ 
Thus at the start of 1810, a year during which Sicily was again under threat 
of French invasion, Stuart was left in command of a force of 16,000 with 
which to defend it, and was under increasing pressure from London to send 
to Spain as much as a third of it. 

<<<PDFPAGE 83>>>
6 
Growing Impatience with the Court at Palermo 
ie the root of the differences between the court and the British representa- 
tives in Sicily, either military or civil (for Amherst, who in March 1809 
had replaced Drummond as minister, shared the views of the generals about 
the court), lay three apparently unanswerable question: how to obtain 
stricter control of the way in which Britain’s subsidy was spent, how to get a 
unified command of all troops on the island, both royal and British, and how 
to procure the court’s support in the effort to mobilize the island’s defenses. 
On each of these three important matters, and of course the three were 
interlinked, all attempts to secure agreement or to establish any measure of 
confidence continued to meet with total failure, and in consequence bred 
increasing frustration. 
The growing financial difficulties of the court led the British government 
to offer it an increase of £100,000 in the amount of the annual subsidy, in 
return for which it was suggested that certain important concessions be 
made. The first of these was the introduction of a unified command on the 
island. If this were done the problem of accounting could be solvied simply 
and painlessly. British senior officers at Messina had long been pressing for 
the right of supervising the way in which the subsidy was spent—ostensibly 
on military objects, but in reality on pensions for émigrés and for financing 
the queen’s network of spies." 
What Canning wished to make clear to the court was that his government 
had no desire to interfere in Sicily’s internal affairs, though neither could it 
commit itself to the restoration of the kingdom of Naples.” The court, while 
welcoming the first assurance, remained completely adamant over the ques- 
tion of a unified command. This was awkward for the ministry in London. It 
had earlier secured from Castelcicala, Sicilian minister to the Court of St. 
James, a pledge that if Sicily were invaded, all forces there would come under 
British command. This pledge had been used by ministers to satisfy a 
querulous House of Commons who were doubtful about the value obtained 
from the annual subsidy paid to Sicily. Now the court made out that 
Castelcicala had acted without authority, that the king had become the 
commander-in-chief (an expedient to be rid of a difficult question, since his 
health would prevent him from taking the field) and that to surrender 
command of his forces would be a blow to his royal dignity. The queen saw it
