as an absolute necessity for the king and his sons to be seen to command if 
they were not to lose “face” in Sicily and indeed throughout the whole of 
Europe.* The queen got her way and the treaty was signed and duly ratified 
in 1810 without the government in London mentioning the court’s con- 
tinued intransigence. “We are still,’ Amherst wrote to Stuart, “in the same 
uncertainty . 
. 
. left to ourselves to remedy as best we can the want of unity of 
plan and employment of the military resources of Sicily.”4 
Stuart by now had become disillusioned with a court whose darling he had 
once been. The operations he had undertaken in the Bay of Naples in June 
and July had opened his eyes to the court’s duplicity. He complained to 
Castlereagh of the way he had been deceived over the role of Prince Leopold, 
who had accompanied him to Ischia in what was nominally a military role. 
Yet once Ischia had been taken, the prince had revealed himself unexpectedly 
as the king’s representative on the island, a step that Stuart considered to be 
both “premature and injudicious,” in view of the suspicions of many Neapoli- 
tans in regard to the king and his future intentions. Stuart also complained 
bitterly of the actions of the Marquis di Schiara, appointed by the king as 
civil governor in Reggio and the surrounding area. Despite the king’s solemn 
amnesty, backed by assurances from the British commanders, Schiara had 
behaved in such a way as to alienate all the local people, and although Circello 
tried to make out that Schiara had acted without authority, Stuart was 
convinced he had been the recipient of secret instructions from the queen. 
While the latter was considered the source of authority by ministers and all 
those in government, “Revenge, Passion and Asperity” would rule, and there 
seemed to Stuart to be little advantage in any military agreement with the 
court.® 
It was only the strongest opposition on his part and on that of Rear 
Admiral Martin, now in command of the naval force that was protecting 
Sicily, that prevented the court in February 1810 from once more trying to 
raise the masse by sending a force under Prince Moliterno into Calabria with 
3,000 muskets. The fact that this madcap scheme of the queen had been 
planned without consulting the British, though it entailed the employment 
and escort of British transports for 4,000 men, caused the British comman- 
ders no small irritation, and it also caused Amherst to lodge a protest against 
such “separate and secret undertakings.”© Stuart by now had become con- 
vinced that only intervention by Britain could save Sicily from disaster, 
ironically the same conclusion that Moore had reached three years before. He 
informed Bunbury there were no alternatives but to take over Sicily or to 
abandon it altogether, and he told Amherst that Britain must dictate to the 
court “its measures as well as its policy.”7 
Amherst had come to agree with him. Writing to Wellesley, the foreign 
secretary, he called on the ministry at home to intervene in Sicilian affairs. 
Outlining his reasons for doing so, he drew attention to the preparations 

<<<PDFPAGE 87>>>
Growing Impatience with the Court at Palermo 
83 
being made by the French to invade Sicily, the inadequacy of Sicilian de- 
fenses, the deplorable state of the royal army, and the fact there was no 
unified command. He also drew attention to the civil unrest, the widespread 
contempt for the court and its government, and the acceptance by most 
Sicilians of the absolute need for foreign intervention, no matter from what 
quarter it came. Britain, by supporting the court, was reproached for per- 
petuating current abuses, and Sicilians queried the value of an alliance with a 
country pledged to such nonintervention. Intervention now by Britain 
would be welcomed by everyone on the island, save only the queen and her 
entourage, a group whose hostile attitudes endangered the lives of the British 
garrison. 
In practical terms what he was suggesting was the introduction of Sicilians 
to the cabinet® (at present made up of four Neapolitans), the summoning of a 
parliament, the exclusion of the queen from public affairs, and the granting to 
Britain of a unified command; that, or at least the right to control all 
expenditures on the royal forces, whose condition was such they might well 
desert the moment the French set foot on the island. Force, he thought, 
should be used if necessary to ensure these measures were carried through— 
measures imperative in the face of invasion, but also to prevent the island 
from remaining merely a “backward and lukewarm ally.”? 
The discontent of the British commanders (greater even than that of 
Amherst) at the court’s lack of cooperation in making preparations against an 
invasion is apparent in letters written by Campbell, the adjutant general, to 
his fellow officers. “The court makes no effort, sends no succour, takes no 
notice. Ferdinando the fourth goes a’tunny fishing, and his wife continues 
her usual pranks . 
. 
. convicted in my mind of acting in concert with Murat 
for our expulsion. 
. 
. 
. We must soon hear of some decision by the govern- 
ment to abandon Sicily or strongly reinforce and effectively maintain [it].” 
For as things were, the court presented nothing but “a tissue of folly and 
inconsistency.”!° 
The invasion crisis of the summer came on the heels of a political one, 
resulting from the court’s financial difficulties and the parliament's refusal to 
grant it any money. The last time the king had asked for a donative had been 
back in 1802, and since that date he and his court had had to seek refuge on 
the island. The way in which they had used Sicily for their own, not Sicilian, 
purposes, the gross extravagances of the queen, the awarding of senior posts 
to foreigners, and the maintenance of a substantial army merely in order to 
recover Naples, caused steadily mounting dissatisfaction. A body of discon- 
tented nobles whose motivations were various and who were led by the vain 
and ambitious Prince Belmonte together with his uncle, the idealistic Prince 
of Castelnuovo, sponsored a plan for a more rational tax system, designed by 
Balsamo, an enlightened economist. This plan envisaged abolishing do- 
natives and substituting a tax on land and a moderate tax on general con- 

<<<PDFPAGE 88>>>
84 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
sumption. The plan was approved by parliament but rejected out of hand by 
the court, who, under the pretense of negotiating a scheme for amending the 
Sicilian constitution, succeeded in dividing the barons and winning over a 
part of them, headed by the loyal and upright Cassaro. The recalcitrant 
barons led by Belmonte then decided to turn to the British and made an 
appeal for help to Amherst, who, though sympathetic, had no instructions.!! 
Amherst, knowing he was quite shortly retiring from his post in Palermo, 
recommended that his successor be empowered to intervene in the govern- 
ment (in ways he had already sketched out in his dispatch of a month before). 
The barons, he wrote home, want a constitution modelled to some extent on 
the British, and they believe that the king’s opposition to it can be overcome 
only by Britain’s interference. Sicily, he stressed once again, was a country 
that was ripe for resistance, and the prospect of becoming a province of 
England was not unwelcome to most of its people. Tactful support by the 
British government of the kind of constitution the barons envisaged could 
have the effect of influencing the court. But in any case some change was 
needed. There must not be a repetition of what had recently occurred when 
the court, confronted by a French invasion, had clearly appeared to side with 
the enemy. 
}2 
For several months there had been no doubt that the enemy was seriously 
preparing an assault. Murat, who to the end of his career remained a victim of 
self-deception, had persuaded himself from intelligence reports that the bulk 
of the Sicilians were on his side. The British army and the court at Palermo 
were both, he was told, detested heartily and in any case were at odds with 
each other. He told Napoleon that he was sure he could count on the support 
of Prince Moliterno, who, together with his fellow officers, was awaiting his 
arrival with some impatience.!3 By the end of July Murat had concentrated 
500 boats in the little ports immediately facing Sicily, dismantled his forts to 
arm these boats, and erected batteries at points where transports could be 
assembled and troops embarked. Finally he arrived in person in the neigh- 
bourhood of Reggio, publicly pledging that in twenty-five days he would be 
dating his letters from Palermo. Yet in the face of all this the court had 
maintained an attitude of indifference, conduct that Amherst had stigmatized 
as “tardy, temporising and even suspicious.” After much delay, it did finally 
order the enrollment of 8,000 men, though not until the end of July. But in 
any case there was no real prospect of the order being carried out, since 
everyone knew the court had not the means to pay even the troops it already 
had. The court also ordered sixteen gunboats in Palermo harbor to join the 
British, but then reduced the pay of their crews, thus offering them, it 
seemed, an incentive to desert; and the crews were only prevented from 
doing so by the British agreeing to make good their pay.!* There is also 
evidence that agents of the queen issued secret instructions to the gunboats 

<<<PDFPAGE 89>>>
Growing Impatience with the Court at Palermo 
85 
not to resist an attack by the French and that the latter received from her 
assurances that their own vessels would not be fired on.15 
Meanwhile attempts to persuade the court to agree to a unified command 
continued to meet with a negative response.!© There was a suggestion that 
the Duke of Orleans, the Anglophile son-in-law of the king, should visit 
Messina to consult with Stuart, but the latter made it clear that he considered 
this pointless until the question of command was resolved. Finally Stuart did 
accept that Bourcard should be allowed to come and help coordinate the 
movements and positioning of the British and the Sicilian forces; yet almost 
on the eve of Murat’s invasion, the plans agreed upon were cancelled by the 
court.'” Efforts had been made to get the king to issue a public proclamation, 
denying that the interests of Sicily were in any way different from those of 
Britain, and to encourage the Sicilian people to join in resisting the invader, 
but no action had been forthcoming. Only in July could the king be induced 
to order his troops and his general in Messina to cooperate with the British 
forces, and to deny Murat’s claim that the people of Sicily looked to him 
(Murat) as their deliverer and savior. Even then the order in question did not 
carry the king’s signature nor those of any of his ministers, nor was it ever 
officially proclaimed or given widespread circulation. Mellish, the chargé 
daffaires in Palermo, had had to use the strongest language to get the order 
issued at all and to persuade the king to send to Milazzo a regiment of royal 
cavalry.18 
As the court had done nothing on its own to counter the stream of 
propaganda put out by the government in Naples, Stuart had organized his 
own with the newspaper Gazzetta Britannica, written and published in 
Messina and edited by a Sicilian priest. From March onward it described the 
horrors the people might expect once the French landed, and it led its readers 
to anticipate a Sicilian Vespers in reverse. Stuart relied on local support (as 
things turned out, with justification), and issued his own proclamation to the 
people, as the court had so signally failed to appeal to them.'? 
To resist the invasion, Admiral Martin disposed of a force of three sail-of- 
the-line, five frigates, nine brigs and nearly 120 gunboats, which harassed 
Murat’s invasion preparations and captured and destroyed a great many of his 
vessels. In one action alone on 21 July this force successfully intercepted a 
convoy carrying important stores, and out of a total of fifty boats captured 
thirty-seven and sank the rest. Even so Martin remained very conscious of the 
limitations within which he operated, and he emphasized to his superior, 
Cotton, the impossibility of interdicting the enemy’s coastal communica- 
tions.?° 
Stuart himself had under command a force of 16,000 troops, almost half of 
whom were foreign, which included a disposable reserve of between 8,000 
and 9,000 men. Since April he had resisted the orders that were sent to him 

<<<PDFPAGE 90>>>
86 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
by Castlereagh to send away four battalions to Spain, exercising, understand- 
ably, the ultimate discretion he had been given.*! He had much improved the 
coastal defenses in the area most exposed to attack, built a military road from 
Messina to Faro, and constructed batteries along the coast, suitably protected 
by fascines and sandbags. The local peasants had given their labor voluntarily 
to dig entrenchments, and the well-to-do had offered their horses, till forbid- 
den to do so by the governor of Messina.?* Whether this would have been 
sufficient to withstand an assault by 25,000 well-led, well-equipped and 
experienced troops (15,000 of whom were French), it is impossible to guess. 
Everything really hinged on the question of whether Murat would be able for 
long enough to maintain his line of communications in face of the threat 
from the British squadron, for his own tiny naval squadron had been largely 
destroyed on 3 May by a British frigate. And of course his ability to do so 
depended entirely on the weather. 
When the invasion eventually took place on the night of 17/18 September, 
it very soon developed into a fiasco. Thirty-five hundred Neapolitan troops 
under Brigadier General Cavaignac landed on the coast south of Messina, 
but, when attacked by a British force, retired swiftly and took to their boats, 
leaving behind 1,200 men (or according to French sources, just under 800), 
together with their brigade commander. In this encounter the British suf- 
fered the loss of only three or four men. The local peasants, most of whom 
had firearms, had also joined in the defense and surprised Stuart agreeably by 
their hostile attitude to the invaders. The major attack planned on the Faro 
mainly by French troops never took place—Murat giving as his excuse a 
sudden drop in the favorable wind. Whether their commander, General 
Grenier, who was in communication with Paris and directly responsible to 
the minister of war there, had been given an order not to embark cannot be 
proved by the documents. What is certain is he had been told by Napoleon to 
take no risks and to be sure of success. 
Jacques Rambaud, in an interesting article about this abortive enterprise, 
has shown that it was not only Napoleon and Grenier who were hesitant, but 
also the other French generals commanding the divisions of Murat’s assault 
force, especially Partonneaux, who knew the area, and Lamarque, who had 
earlier proved his enterprise in his bold assault on the cliffs of Anacapri. It 
was Murat’s awareness of his generals’ reservations that had almost certainly 
prompted him to launch Cavaignac across the straits, in order to convince the 
others that the operation was feasible.23 
Napoleon, we know, was primarily interested in making the British believe 
in the invasion in order to distract their attention from Portugal, Spain, and 
the Ionian islands. He was therefore furious with Murat when the latter, 
realizing his project had failed, withdrew most of his forces from Calabria, 
including all his gunboats and transports, Murat was unable to afford the 
expense of keeping his troops mobilized for invasion. He also feared running 

<<<PDFPAGE 91>>>
Growing Impatience with the Court at Palermo 
87 
the risk of sickness in an area known to be plagued by malaria during the 
months of July and August. But to save face, he announced publicly that the 
point of the exercise had been achieved. Napoleon told him he ought to have 
kept his army concentrated, to deceive the British, and that he was imme- 
diately to resume a threatening posture in regard to Sicily.24 When Stuart 
reacted as Napoleon desired and for fear of weakening Sicily’s defenses 
refused to send more troops to Spain, he was overruled by Liverpool and 
resigned his appointment in disgust.25.The court meanwhile declined to 
order any public celebration of the island’s deliverance.?° 
Sicily continued to remain vulnerable. The foreign troops under British 
command were of a very mixed quality, and two regiments were singled out 
as being especially unsatisfactory. The British forces covered only the area 
from Milazzo to Faro and then south to Cape Passaro and, apart from 
Trapani where they had a small garrison, the rest of the island was very 
exposed, guarded by an army of 9,000 virtually useless royal troops. As for 
protection by the British navy, this was entirely dependent on the weather. 
The ships could not operate off Calabria in the teeth of a strong northeasterly 
wind and might always be inhibited by currents or periodic calms (as Nelson 
had stressed earlier). On 18 September 1810 the fleet had not been able to 
prevent the bulk of Cavaignac’s invading force from returning safely to 
Reggio, nor to stop Murat’s fleet of 600 ships from making its retreat to the 
north, unscathed, save by volleys from coastal batteries. It is true that a 
fortnight afterward a flotilla of 110 of these boats was successfully inter- 
cepted off the coast of Calabria and that thirty-six of them were destroyed, 
but the bulk of Murat’s invasion armada contrived to return to Naples 
intact:27 
Nevertheless the ministry in London was not inclined to be pessimistic, no 
doubt influenced in their views by the conclusions of a military report 
submitted early in 1811. This report considered that the British garrison, 
amounting to 15,000 troops, could repel any attack from the east. And if the 
French were to land in the west, conveyed by a strong naval force from 
Toulon that might always evade the British fleet, it would still be months 
before they could drag their heavy siege guns across the mountains to reduce 
the fortresses of Messina and Milazzo. By which time, it was safely reckoned, 
the navy would haye come to evacuate the army.?® In refusing to strengthen 
Sicily further at the expense of Spain and Portugal, the ministers took a 
calculated risk, but one that proved in the end to be justified. 

<<<PDFPAGE 92>>>
7 
Lord William Bentinck and British Intervention, 
Stage One 
s the constitutional crisis worsened in the early months of 1811, so did 
A relations between the court and British officials, civil and military. The 
refusal of the Sicilian parliament to grant the king the money he had asked for 
led him to try to raise taxes by decree—a patent violation of the constitution. 
The royal edicts of 14 February imposed a tax of 1 percent on all sales, 
including those made by British merchants, and confiscated properties be- 
longing to the church and to various charitable communities, which were 
then ordered to be sold by public lottery. 
When the British merchants protested strongly that the sales tax was 
contrary to the treaty signed with Britain by Spain in 1667 and renewed by 
the Palermo court as recently as 1809, they received the cursory reply they 
should either comply or leave the island. The court was irritated not only by 
this protest but by the appeal being made to London by Prince Belmonte and 
other barons, calling for the repeal of the royal decrees and the summoning of 
a parliament. Protests on behalf of British subjects arrested and detained in 
Sicily and on behalf of the captain and owners of a ship flying the British flag, 
captured by three of Castrone’s privateers, were all rejected out of hand, and 
the British Consul General, Robert Fagan, when he tried to remonstrate with 
Circello, was accorded a less than polite reception. He was then suspended 
from all his functions on the grounds that he was interfering with the normal 
judicial processes.! Meanwhile five leaders of the dissident barons, including 
Belmonte and Castelnuovo, were arrested and deported to the smaller is- 
lands. 
All the tension this generated was made worse by the growing suspicion 
that the queen was involved in treachery. As early as March 1810, Amherst 
had drawn London’s attention to the queen’s change of attitude toward 
Napoleon, after she had learned of the latter’s intended marriage to her 
granddaughter, Princess Marie-Louise. There was evidence that the queen 
was corresponding with the government in Paris through Vienna, with the 
French consulate in Tunis, and with Lucien Bonaparte, a prisoner in Malta. 
In February 1811, reports reached Amherst that the queen had made a secret 
agreement by which the king was to get back Naples, while an Austrian 
88 

<<<PDFPAGE 93>>>
Bentinck and British Intervention, Stage One 
89 
prince was to rule in Sicily with the support of German troops. Though 
Circello, on behalf of the court, made a solemn denial of these rumors and 
assured Amherst that the king still remained staunchly loyal to the British 
alliance, Amherst professed himself dissatisfied, until the king conceded a 
unified command.” 
Meanwhile the government in London had at last followed Moore’s ad- 
vice, given to it by him four years earlier, namely to combine the offices in 
Sicily of diplomat and military commander as they had once been under 
General Fox. On 25 February 1811 Lord William Bentinck was appointed to 
this post—envoy extraordinary, minister plenipotentiary, and commander- 
in-chief of the British forces. Bentinck was a man of wide experience in both 
the military and administrative fields, having seen active service in Spain, 
Italy, and the Netherlands, and from 1803 to 1807 he had governed the 
province of Madras. Described by his most recent biographer as possessing 
something of the energy and forthrightness of a great Victorian public 
servant, cool in action and very determined, a Whig idealist but not doc- 
trinaire, he was to fill for the next four years the unenviable and exacting role 
of a British proconsul in Sicily,* which 1s what the ministry had decided that 
it wanted. His plain speaking as a straightforward soldier, as well as his 
somewhat charmless manner disguising what was a strong natural reserve, 
did not endear him to the court, and the queen who resented particularly his 
dictatorial attitude and actions described him as being a wild beast, a boorish 
sergeant, someone who having been sent to Sicily merely “in order to bow 
and scrape” had the temerity to give laws to the country.° While the king, 
completely unaccustomed to hear such blunt language used to a sovereign, 
thought of Bentinck as an instrument of God, sent to punish him for all his 
sins.© 
The British government had long been under pressure to intervene more 
in Sicilian affairs—pressures exerted by members of Parliament, a section of 
the popular press, the publications of Francis Leckie (the Scotsman who had 
farmed near Syracuse), British merchants who did business in Sicily, and also 
by a very influential book by the army captain Charles W. Pasley. Indeed the 
ministers had had some difficulty in persuading the irate Castelcicala that they 
were anxious to give such protection “to the honour and dignity of an ally” as 
the law allowed against offensive publications. A letter published in the 
Morning Post on 14 August 1810, which suggested that Sicily could only be 
saved by Britain annexing it and pensioning the king, caused the institution 
of legal proceedings, though a month later these were dropped. And a report 
in the Morning Chronicle on 2 May 1811 
reporting the speech of a member of 
Parliament, in which he had said of Sicily that “a more profligate government 
or a more oppressed people did not exist or had not existed,” called forth 
further protests from Castelcicala and renewed demands that the editor of the 
newspaper be prosecuted.” Captain Pasley’s “Military Policy and Institutions 

<<<PDFPAGE 94>>>
Lord William Cavendish Bentinck, painted by Sir T. Lawrence and engraved by 
R. J. Lane. (Courtesy of the British Museum, London.) 

<<<PDFPAGE 95>>>
Bentinck and British Intervention, Stage One 
ot 
of the British Empire” was published in November 1810 and ran through 
four editions in two years. It was read and admired by many army officers, 
including Generals Wellington and Beresford, and it was favorably com- 
mented on in the Quarterly Review of May 1811.8 
Pasley’s thesis was that Sicily had not fulfilled the terms of the treaty under 
which it received a substantial subsidy, and instead of providing an efficient 
army, it absorbed 15,000 of the best British troops. “As long as we hold 
Sicily . 
. 
. without applying its revenues and resources to our own purposes, 
in return for the protection we afford it, it can only be compared to a 
millstone round our necks.” He argued that Sicily was potentially an incom- 
parable asset to Great Britain, and militarily of far more value than all the 
West Indies put together, yet the British government seemed to regard it as 
nothing more than an appendage of Malta. Britain should have conquered it 
in 1805, instead of earning unpopularity by propping up an incompetent 
monarchy. Pasley therefore proposed that a Britain general should be put in 
command of all troops in Sicily, and that commissariat and postal services 
should be placed forthwith under British direction.? 
This may have made uncomfortable reading for the Perceval ministry in 
London, but in the end it was the resignations of both Amherst and Stuart 
coming together, that forced the government to rethink its policy.' 
Bentinck was commissioned by Wellesley to produce a memorandum of 
his views, based on a reading of the relevant dispatches, before being given 
his instructions. His conclusions, which did not please all the cabinet, were 
that if Britain were to keep Sicily, she could not delay her intervention. 
Britain, he submitted, must control the subsidy, take command of all troops 
in the island, appoint Sicilians to the government, and ensure the reform of 
outstanding abuses. These measures would appeal to the people and might 
have a great effect on Italy. If the Sicilian court refused to accept them, then 
the minister should withdraw to Messina. The great and pressing evil in Sicily 
demanded a “speedy and decisive remedy,” and unless he was given extraordi- 
nary powers he felt he could not perform the task or cope with all the “deceit, 
low cunning and intrigues of that miserable court.”?* 
The Prime Minister, Perceval, was not impressed by this reasoning. He 
pointed out that for the past five years every military authority had said the 
same thing, namely that the security of Sicily depended on effecting these 
reforms. Yet Sicily was still defended. The revolution envisaged by Bentinck 
would overthrow the royal prerogative and also noble and corporate priv- 
ileges, and he was not convinced that the changes proposed would necessarily 
result in the promised gains. He did not believe in the queen’s treachery and 
thought her jealousy of Britain understandable, and he did not feel that the 
use of violence was justified in the circumstances. Bentinck must be urged to 
accept the post, provided the views he submitted to the court were the British 
government’s and not his own.'? 

<<<PDFPAGE 96>>>
92 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
It was for this reason that Bentinck’s instructions specified that he must 
reassure the court. Britain, he must tell it, had no intention of interfering in 
its internal affairs or of trying to effect a revolution in the Sicilian constitu- 
tion. Nevertheless the king and queen must be informed that Britain, being 
as anxious as they were to see them restored to the kingdom of Naples and 
equally anxious to use Sicily’s resources in order to achieve this end, felt that 
the people must first be appeased. This could be done only by granting a 
share in the government to Sicilians, by paying heed to Sicily’s parliament, 
and by redressing popular grievances. If the court was not willing to do these 
things and so was faced by a revolution, it could not expect from its British 
ally any military or other assistance. 
Bentinck’s arrival in Sicily was awaited with both anxiety and hope, 
according to Douglas, the secretary of legation, who told Wellesley that most 
Sicilians would now “be satisfied with nothing less than the British flag flying 
all over Sicily,” and a “total eradication of the abominable system.”!4 When 
he did arrive on 18 July, only two days had elapsed after the arrest of the five 
barons—something he thought not coincidental, for having learnt of his 
reputation the court was afraid he might enlist the support of these noble 
dissidents. He stayed in Sicily five weeks only, at the end of which he 
returned to England to report and to ask for wider powers. He had, during 
his very brief stay, encountered a completely negative attitude on the part of 
the king and queen and their ministers, who denied there was any sort of 
crisis and therefore the need for any measures to meet one. He was told that 
the levy of 1 percent had been entirely constitutional, the barons’ protest had 
been illegal and disapproved of by parliament, the stories of popular discon- 
tent were entirely without foundation, and the barons who had lodged the 
protest were in no way representative. Although he considered it unlikely 
there would be a serious revolt with the opposition leaders removed, spies 
and informers everywhere, and the royal army disliking the people, Bentinck 
was convinced of the need for change—a change that could only be effected 
by controlling the British subsidy and by employing forcible language.!5 
Before he left Sicily for England, Bentinck had appointed General Freder- 
ick Maitland to act in his absence as his deputy, and Maitland soon convinced 
himself that the Sicilian government had joined hands with the enemy. 
Maitland wrote to Wellesley in some anxiety: 
We are in such a state as our enemy would wish—we believe the Sicilian 
government to be plotting and combining for our ruin, and whether from 
consciousness of their intrigues with Bonaparte or from apprehension that 
we will bring about a change of government, from a despotic one, which it 
now is, to a mixed one—certain it is, they distrust and fear us as much as 
we do them.!° 
And when Bentinck returned to the island in the late autumn of 1811, 
Maitland had just unearthed a plot that seemed to involve the court in 

<<<PDFPAGE 97>>>
Bentinck and British Intervention, Stage One 
93 
treachery. It entailed the suborning of German troops (part of the garrison of 
Messina), the seizure of the Faro fort, and an invitation to the French in 
Calabria to cross over without opposition. Sixteen people in all were arrested, 
including the colonel of the Sicilian flotilla, the principal judge, and an army 
captain. And a soldier of the Calabrian corps (who was arrested by a British 
patrol) was tried summarily by court martial and executed without further 
ado. The bishop of Oppido was also arrested, although by royal command 
and at Maitland’s request. 
In regard to the earlier “conspiracies,” revealed in the letters sent from 
Calabria that had been seized by the British authorities and had seemed to 
implicate the queen, the king had been anxious to demonstrate his con- 
tinuing loyalty to the alliance. The incriminating letters, so Circello pointed 
out, were clearly intended by whomever sent them (the agents of Murat in 
Calabria) to fall into the hands of the British, and so were designed to sow 
suspicion between Britain and Sicily. In November therefore the king had 
ordered the printing of a special dispatch to be given the widest distribution 
in which he condemned all such attempts to stir up discord between the 
allies. And in order to discourage future messengers, armed with such letters 
and carrying false papers, he decreed that any such persons caught were to be 
hanged within twenty-four hours of their having been found guilty of the 
offence. The British commander, Sir Frederick Maitland, was asked to assist 
in smuggling copies of this dispatch to Calabria, which Maitland of course 
agreed to do.!7 
But then had come the revelations of a “real” conspiracy at Messina. The 
king took violent offense at what seemed to be the high handed manner in 
which Maitland had handled the business. He could not, so Circello pro- 
tested, allow his subjects to be put on trial by the representative of another 
power, and he therefore ordered a special court, to include two British 
officers, to be set up to try the accused. He also took very great exception to a 
proclamation that Maitland had issued appealing for the support of Sicilians 
for the British forces protecting Sicily—a proclamation that had been made 
without the permission of the king, and one in which no mention was made 
of any alliance between Britain and Sicily. And the king took further grave 
exception to the promise, publicly made by Maitland, to pardon all those 
who might turn “king’s evidence.” Pardon was, it was pointed out, the 
prerogative of the king alone.!8 
By this time Bentinck had returned to Sicily and he had to apologize to the 
king for the tactlessness of his deputy. The need for speed in taking action in 
what seemed desperate circumstances had led Maitland, he feared, to forget 
due forms. But what Bentinck refused to accept was the kind of tribunal the 
king envisaged for trying the men accused of treason. The persons arrested 
had been detected in the very center of the British camp and at the headquar- 
ters of the commander-in-chief, and he therefore insisted that the court to try 
them must be appointed by him alone. It should be composed in equal parts 
>) 

<<<PDFPAGE 98>>>
94 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
of British and Sicilian officers and he himself must appoint its president. He 
also asked for authority to arrest and punish summarily anyone found inside 
the British defenses who either aided the enemy or was in correspondence 
with him. To the latter request the king demurred on the grounds that the 
matter was no longer urgent, but he did accede to other demands about the 
appointment of a “council of war” and the way in which it was to be 
composed. 19 
The most important figure in the plot, Captain Rossaroll, an infantry 
officer and chief of the royal police in Messina, was tried by an Anglo-Sicilian 
court, sentenced to death and subsequently hanged.?° Though Rossaroll did 
not reveal at his trial he had been acting on behalf of the queen, Bentinck had 
evidence enough to prove that her advisers were deeply involved. Another of 
her agents, Captain Cassetti, had earlier been arrested in Palermo and the 
letters he carried seemed to show on whose behalf he must have been acting 
in the contacts he had established with Murat.?! The threat to publish the 
relevant documents was one Bentinck used against the court, and the fact that 
the king refused to examine them showed he knew that they were not 
forgeries. But he told Orleans that although the queen had committed many 
indiscretions, he could not believe she would go so far as to correspond with 
the enemy.?7 
These plots were only the culmination of intrigues that had been detected a 
year before, revealing the queen to be rather more of an inveterate schemer 
than a serious danger. Castlereagh told Castelcicala that although he was 
convinced of her treachery, which meant that she could not stay in Sicily, he 
thought she had acted in a moment of passion and not with any premedita- 
tion. Bentinck himself never believed that the queen really meant to give the 
French Sicily, and he thought that only her love of intrigue, her love for 
Naples, and her Anglophobia had led her to compromise herself.23 
It seems that the initial reactions in both London and Messina to events in 
Sicily during 1811 had been overdone. One shrewd observer in Messina, the 
wife of a senior British officer and clearly a woman of some intelligence, 
wrote home of the alarmist reports then being circulated in England about 
the state of unrest in Sicily and the spy scare that for several months had 
caused a flurry in military circles: 
Each party speaks boastfully and threatens much—but in each there is so 
little spirit and real energy and so little genuine patriotism or 
power of 
pursuing consistently for any length of time one steady undeviating 
course—that small indeed is the reliance to be placed in anything said or 
projected by either party—a Sicilian talks loud and long, sometimes even 
well—but he has rarely the resolution or courage necessary for action.24 
That was in August 1811. Five months later, she wrote on the same theme: 
So far from the people of the island being in a state of insurrection, as has 
been asserted in many of your newspapers—the total apathy with which 

<<<PDFPAGE 99>>>
Bentinck and British Intervention, Stage One 
95 
they regard the changes now making in their 
government is astonishing 
even to us, who have had for so long an acquaintance with their want of 
sense and good feeling in any matters savouring of patriotism.?5 
In regard to the trials in Messina to investigate allegations of treachery, she 
described how the mountain of the council of war, of which her husband had 
been a member, had labored long to produce its mouse. (It had sat for seven 
weeks and examined an enormous volume of written evidence.) The pris- 
oners accused of selling the island had, the court found, merely offered to 
trade some trifling details to the French in Calabria about British strengths 
and dispositions, only in order to obtain facilities for promoting a trade in 
contraband.7° 
Bentinck had returned from England armed with the power to withhold 
the subsidy if the court refused to accept his demands. These were: the 
immediate appointment of Sicilians to positions of influence, the conceding 
of a unified command, the recall of the five exiled barons, and the removal of 
anti-British advisers.2” He proceeded at once to use this power, though it did 
not yield instantaneous results. His interviews with the king and queen and 
Marquis Circello all proved fruitless, so he sent for a regiment from Malta 
and ordered his troops at Milazzo and Trapani to embark on transports and 
sail for Palermo. As he wrote to General Oakes, the civil commissioner in 
Malta, “this uncertainty will never be determined until force, or the ap- 
pearance of force, shall produce the necessity of a decision.” It was a policy 
that Robert Fagan had urged on him the previous September (“every point 
will be obtained in four hours . 
. 
. Good God, what ridiculous and ill-judged 
delicacy has directed our conduct till the present”).?8 And indeed there were 
many others, generals, admirals and diplomats, who had acquaintance with 
Sicily and the political position there, who had written him letters of encour- 
agement, urging him to take a very firm line. 
In a stormy interview with the queen, Bentinck found himself accused of 
trying to dethrone the king, whose only course, so the queen declared, was to 
lead his army into Italy and die in the breach, like Tippoo Sultan.?? She 
ridiculed the whole idea of choosing Sicilians as ministers—people “who 
could neither read nor write”—yet there was little she could do.?° She was 
however almost certainly responsible for encouraging an officer called Melito 
to try to stir up the mob in the capital, and Bentinck now decided to make 
that city the future headquarters of the British garrison." 
The king offered to abdicate (a course that Bentinck himself favored) and 
was only dissuaded by the queen. But in the face of the military threat, he did 
agree to appoint his eldest son, the hereditary prince, as vicar general (a 
course that the queen had also opposed), although he refused to delegate any 
control over foreign affairs. He also agreed to a unified command, with 
Bentinck henceforth as captain general, and to recalling the five barons 
without any conditions attached.? 
Yet despite his apparent victory, Bentinck discovered that little was hap- 

<<<PDFPAGE 100>>>
96 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
pening, and he began to regret his precipitancy in resuming the payment of 
the British subsidy. The court was expert at procrastination, and the heredi- 
tary prince was no exception, being far more under the influence of his 
parents than Bentinck had either supposed or hoped. The prince tried to have 
the former ministers, including Circello, retained as councillors, a course to 
which Bentinck strongly objected, given their unprogressive views and gen- 
eral unpopularity. And the prince also took the view that to summon 
parliament at this time, when popular opinion was so inflamed, would merely 
be courting revolution, despite Bentinck’s attempt to impress on him that 
nothing less than a parliament could solve the country’s financial problems 
and introduce much-needed reforms. The king, who together with the queen 
really governed the hereditary prince, was convinced that Bentinck had 
exceeded his powers, and he therefore counted on Bentinck’s recall before a 
parliament could be summoned and a new constitution introduced. For that 
reason he advised the prince to delay in effecting any change.#4 
A new ministry was eventually formed of four members of the Sicilian 
nobility: Belmonte, Castelnuovo, Cassaro, and Aci, though not until March 
1812, and even then it failed to gain the hereditary prince’s confidence. 
Parliament did not meet until May and no progress whatsoever was made 
toward adopting a new constitution. There were still too many anti-British 
advisers who surrounded the king and queen, even if the worst had been 
removed (Ascoli exiled to Sardinia,3+ Castrone shut up in Castellamare, and 
Medici allowed to go to England in order to plead the cause of the queen 
there). Bentinck was certain that the queen was the real cause of all the delay. 
She was responsible, according to Belmonte, for spreading rumors that the 
latter was trying to get rid of the hereditary prince and set up a regency under 
Orleans. Orleans was the intimate of Belmonte and also the trusted friend of 
Bentinck, whom, through his link with the royal family, he was able to keep 
very well informed of what was being thought and done at court. Orleans 
told the hereditary prince that he was wrong to suspect him of wanting his 
place; that if he wanted that he would not try so hard to reconcile him to the 
British command. But the rumor, once started, was hard to scotch, and 
Bentinck thought it was one of the reasons for the unpopularity of the 
British.35 
Bentinck tried without any success to persuade the queen to leave Sicily, 
letting her know he was well acquainted with her agents dealings with the 
enemy. She grew by turns angry and tearful at the harshness of Bentinck’s 
inflexibility, his attempt to get rid of her “as if she were nothing but a 
common prostitute,” and his refusal to pay her debts, and she resolutely 
declined to move.#° The court had been banking on Bentinck’s support for 
an expedition to Italy and had therefore professed to approve of the idea of 
Sicily having a new constitution, having been convinced by Moliterno of the 
magical importance of that word as the key that would unlock the kingdom 

<<<PDFPAGE 101>>>
Bentinck and British Intervention, Stage One 
97 
of Naples. Moliterno himself liked to pose as the leader of an Italian resist- 
ance movement, obtaining the queen’s support by suggesting she herself 
might eventually become the ruler of all Italy. But Bentinck made clear he 
would not allow any Italian expedition until Sicily was given the free consti- 
tution he wished her to have. And when British troops in Sicily were ordered 
to Spain and not Italy, the court lost interest in the constitution.37 
Bentinck’s frustration reached breaking point when he found himself 
obstructed in measures for reforming the royal army in Sicily, whose con- 
dition he considered such that “no officer could lead it to the field with any 
confidence of victory.” The hereditary prince refused to allow him to issue 
orders except through the minister, on the grounds that he had no authority 
from the king and that Bentinck had already agreed to Sicily being governed 
by Sicilian ministers. The minister in question was Aci, a man who, Bentinck 
came to think, had “more vanity than patriotism,” was certainly incompetent, 
and deeply resented having to share the credit for army reform with anyone. 
Bentinck therefore decided he had once again to step in and withhold the 
subsidy.38 The Sicilian reaction was immediate. Parliament, though it had 
been elected in the spring, had not been summoned till 8 July, and since then 
it had done nothing at all. It now met on 20 July and passed all the 
fundamental articles (or Bases) of the constitution in one all-night sitting.9? 
The constitution approved by the parliament provided for a bicameral 
legislature with the exclusive power of making laws, and one to which 
government ministers and public officials were now responsible. Parliament 
was to meet at least once a year, though the king had the right to prorogue 
and dissolve it, as well as the right to veto laws. The king was given a civil list, 
while the Commons controlled all public expenditure. The judiciary were 
independent; there was trial by jury and the right of habeas corpus, and 
relative freedom of the press. But only the literate had the right to vote and 
the right to sit in parliament (which meant about 15,000 men out of a 
population of a million-and-a-half). The guilds and all religious bodies were 
to lose all their privileges, while feudal rights and entail were abolished, with 
certain provisions made for compensation. Sicily was declared independent, 
the king was forbidden to leave the island without the consent of parliament, 
and a provision was made, if he went back to Naples, for his eldest son to 
remain as viceroy.*° 
The modelling of'the constitution on the English prototype was deliberate, 
because in Sicily (outside Catania) there was more sympathy for English 
ideas of the politically enlightened kind than for those of Rousseau and the 
French Revolution, and Balsamo, who drafted the constitution, was well 
versed in English common law as well as in the writings of Blackstone. It was 
not originally Bentinck’s intention that the constitution should be so mod- 
elled, although he had been given complete discretion by Castlereagh to 
make recommendations. And he had at first opposed the idea of copying the 

<<<PDFPAGE 102>>>
98 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
English constitution, since he rightly thought it unsuited to Sicily. He had 
told Belmonte that because the English constitution was such that no one 
understood it or was able even to define it, to adopt it as a model was inviting 
ridicule. Later however he changed his mind, but only because no alternative 
plan seemed to present fewer difficulties.41 The king believed that the English 
constitution safeguarded the royal prerogative; the queen, who derived her 
knowledge from Medici, concluded that it was largely nonexistent, while 
what did exist was largely negative.4? But if Bentinck did not beget the 
constitution, he was certainly an active midwife at its birth. The Italian 
historian Capograssi thinks that without Bentinck’s enthusiasm it could 
never have seen the light of day. But clearly this enthusiasm got the better of 
his sober judgment and led him to imagine that Sicily, because it had for so 
many centuries boasted of having a constitution, must have had the necessary 
experience to assimilate a new one on the English model.#3 
The king was at first most unwilling to sanction the resolutions of the 
parliament, and adopted his usual delaying tactics. When pressed by Ben- 
tinck, he became very angry, exclaiming “Never, never, never” would he 
assent to the constitution. An English constitution, yes, but this was neither 
English nor Sicilian. But the threat of withholding the subsidy proved in the 
end to be decisive, because it was obvious that without it the army would not 
be paid or fed. The king therefore signified his agreement to the prince, 
although he insisted that he approved only of those articles that conformed 
with the English constitution.4+ He even talked of sending it to England, to 
be scrutinized and reported on, but in his heart he could never bring himself 
to approve of any new constitution. He later told Bentinck that he was afraid 
that the changes it brought could only end in bloodshed, revolution, and 
anarchy. “We are living with cannibals,” he said, and fearing for his life and 
property and for those of his family, he wanted a warship standing by to carry 
them off to some safe haven.45 
The hereditary prince was also, at heart, no enthusiast for the constitution, 
and he filled the role allotted to him out of fear rather than genuine convic- 
tion. He was fond of repeating the Latin tag Nescit regnare qui nescit simulare 
(He knows not how to reign who knows not how to feign), and Bentinck 
was amazed that such a “blockhead” should be so adept at dissimulation. In 
his private correspondence with Castelcicala, the prince complained of being 
bullied by Bentinck (of whom he was obviously afraid), as well as reviled by 
his father and mother, the queen calling him a villain and the king accusing 
him of ambition. Belmonte thought his character was “like that of a woman, 
subject very much to change,” while Bentinck told the Duke of Orleans that 
it was only fear or force that would ever induce the prince to act. Bentinck 
thought he was terrified of being damned for disobliging his parents, “always 
seeing Hell on one side and the reproach of posterity on the other.” Not 
possessing a strong character, the prince tried merely to keep afloat, refrain- 

<<<PDFPAGE 103>>>
Bentinck and British Intervention, Stage One 
99 
ing from compromising himself in the hope of preserving the dynasty. When 
he gave his approval to the constitution, he gave it in a qualified form, and he 
excepted from his approval articles relating to the civil list, the right of 
parliament to control expenditures, and compensation for the loss of civil 
rights.*° 
Much (too much) of Bentinck’s time was given up to getting rid of the 
queen, to whose presence in Sicily, it can now be seen, he attached an 
exaggerated importance. The king liked to shelter behind the queen, making 
out that it was she, not he, who was really the cause of any obstructions. The 
king, in his attitude to the queen’s removal, was, according to Belmonte, a 
cunning “old Fox,” pretending he could not get his wife to leave in order to 
make the British use force and thereby enhance his own prestige.47 In 
October 1812 Bentinck at last after considerable difficulty persuaded the king 
to induce the queen to go away to Santa Margherita (a town in the southwest 
of the island and far enough from the king and Prince), the king meanwhile 
living at La Ficuzza, a shooting lodge thirty miles from Palermo. The queen 
arranged that the following spring she would leave the island altogether, 
having been promised by the Habsburg emperor that she would be welcome 
to come to Vienna (although in fact the emperor did not want her there but 
found it impossible to refuse her). Yet two months later she was pleading her 
debts and her poverty as reasons for staying.*8 Castlereagh supported Ben- 
tinck in his efforts to exclude the queen from government and engineer her 
departure from Sicily, having been considerably impressed by the documents 
that Bentinck had sent him. He made clear to Castelcicala that Britain could 
no longer tolerate someone who stood in the way of change, misled foreign 
powers like Austria and Russia about Britain’s future intentions for Sicily, 
and engaged in treasonable correspondences. He did however in writing to 
Bentinck stress his desire to save the queen’s face by avoiding the use of any 
force, and to make her leaving Sicily appear to have been done by arrange- 
ment only.4? But when in April 1813 this dispatch arrived in Sicily, events 
had overtaken it and induced Bentinck to use forcible methods. 
Bentinck was angry when he found that the queen, in breach of the 
previous agreement, had returned to Ficuzza to be once more with the king; 
and still more angry when the king denied he had made any promise in regard 
to the queen, asserting that the written undertaking, signed on his behalf by 
Cassaro and Circellé, had merely been an expression of his wishes.°° Bentinck 
was already most dissatisfied with the influence that the duke of Ascoli 
(though in exile) continued to have on the king through his aide-de-camp 
Colonel Frilli, who acted as the king’s secretary, and through Lonzelot, 
comptroller of the royal household. And to compound matters, the king (he 
found) was consulting Cassaro and other barons who, disliking the idea of 
abolishing entail, had split the ministry and turned to the crown. Although 
parliament had since November completed its work on the constitution, the 

<<<PDFPAGE 104>>>
