king had, to Bentinck’s annoyance, so far made no move to give it his 
sanction.>1 
Bentinck was also increasingly frustrated at what he regarded as the weak- 
ness of the ministry—the lack of mutual confidence shown between ministers 
and the hereditary prince, disunion among ministers themselves, and the lack 
of cabinet government as that was understood in England. Bentinck said that 
all he could give was support to the administration—it was for ministers to 
lead the House. Belmonte in whom hitherto Bentinck had reposed such 
confidence now seemed “blinded by his own vanity,” could not apply himself 
to business, and was quite hopeless as a prime minister. Without assuming 
the government himself, Bentinck found it difficult to take a firmer line, as 
Orleans was urging him to do. “We appeared to have everything our own 
way and to have all the power . 
. 
. while in fact we have nothing. 
. 
. .°52 
Bentinck through Belmonte now approached the king, requesting that he 
sanction the constitution, call a new parliament as soon as possible, and order 
the queen away from Ficuzza. Although the queen was induced to go to 
Castelvetrano in the south of the island, the king on 9 March 1813 arrived 
unexpectedly in Palermo and announcd that he was resuming power, without 
informing either Bentinck or the prince. Acclaimed vociferously in the 
streets, he seemed to be returning in triumph, an event that was celebrated 

<<<PDFPAGE 105>>>
Bentinck and British Intervention, Stage One 
101 
shortly after by a solemn Te Deum being sung in the cathedral. But two days 
later he had to endure a stormy interview with Bentinck during which the 
latter told him plainly that he would not permit him to destroy the constitu- 
tion. The king was furious at what he regarded as Bentinck’s arrogant and 
threatening tone. He kept repeating the word “permit” with ever increasing 
irritation, arguing that Britain had no right to meddle in Sicily’s internal 
affairs.53 
For the next fortnight Bentinck endeavored to treat with the king through 
intermediaries (as the king refused to see him personally)—the king’s con- 
fessor, Prince Belmonte, the hereditary prince, and the duke of Orleans. 
Relations became increasingly strained, with Belmonte and all the ministers 
resigning, the king still adamant in his refusal to let Bentinck treat him (in his 
own words) as if Bentinck was his schoolmaster, and Bentinck widely re- 
ported as saying he would have the king and queen taken to London while he 
set up a regency in the island.5* Both the king and queen still clung to the 
hope that Bentinck was unsupported in London. Castelcicala had recently 
assured them that the ministers in Britain left their officials overseas without 
any definite instructions—if they succeeded, the ministers took the credit, 
while if they failed, they broke their neck. Since the mail between England 
and Sicily could take from two to three months to arrive, it was easy enough 
to insinuate that Britain’s representative had exceeded his instructions. It was 
a ploy that Paoli had used eighteen years earlier in Corsica when he had 
successfully undermined the morale of the British viceroy. But Bentinck was 
made of sterner stuff than the strangely self-doubting Gilbert Elliot, and 
Castlereagh was more punctual in answering dispatches than the ever-dila- 
tory Duke of Portland. 
After a week Bentinck’s patience was exhausted and he finally decided on 
the use of force. When he heard that the king was about to attend another 
thanksgiving service in the capital, and fearing a popular disturbance would 
follow, he ordered his troops and guns onto the streets, and threatened the 
king with the end of the alliance. At one stage he even contemplated forcing 
the king to abdicate in favor of the hereditary prince and then making the 
king his prisoner, but he realized his instructions did not allow for this.>° 
There was of course no attempt at resistance. The king agreed to delegate all 
his powers to the hereditary prince and accepted that the queen must be 
ordered to leave. Ih return, his civil list was confirmed and the queen was 
assured financial solvency. Even then, in order to get the king to put this 
agreement in writing, Bentinck had to post fifty dragoons on the road out of 
the palace at Colli in case the king should try to escape and raise a civil war in 
the countryside. What the king now put his signature to was a promise not 
only to restore the Vicariate but also not to resume his powers until permit- 
ted by Bentinck to do so.57 Before finally signing the agreement, the king had 
made yet one final effort to slip out of the political noose by offering to make 

<<<PDFPAGE 106>>>
102 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
Bentinck his prime minister or “Grand Chancellor.” Not unnaturally this 
offer was refused, Bentinck replying that he could not see how any foreigner 
could hold such a post.°8 
The king thus put firmly on one side, it only remained to deal with the 
queen and those advisers of the court who were known to be unfriendly to 
the constitution. The hereditary prince, at Bentinck’s request, relieved of 
their posts the king’s recent supporters, the barons Cassaro, Cuto, and Aci, 
agreed to deprive of all influence Tommasi, Hesse, and Circello, and ordered 
out of Sicily foreigners like Frilli and Moliterno.5? To the queen, who was 
still at Castelvetrano, Bentinck sent Macfarlane, a tactful general, with orders 
to ensure she left Sicily and to make arrangements for her departure. To make 
certain that the queen complied and did not attempt to raise the people, 
Bentinck dispatched three battalions of infantry, together with a squadron of 
cavalry. There was no physical resistance, but a great many protests and 
delays (some of them caused by the queen’s ill-health) until she finally 
departed from Mazzara on 14 June 1813. She sailed aboard a British warship, 
escorted by two more, travelled to Vienna via Constantinople, and died there 
in September 1814 shortly after her arrival. Financially she had been gener- 
ously treated, the British government paying her debts, redeeming her per- 
sonal jewels from pawn, and granting her a respectable allowance. Whether 
she had really been worth so much trouble and all this expense is perhaps 
debatable. What is certain is that her removal was not a solution to the 
island’s problems or to those of the British in Sicily.6° 
Bentinck now appeared to have triumphed, though he regretted that the 
relevant dispatches had arrived too late to make his coup needless. For what 
the dispatches did was make clear what the queen had been trying to cast a 
doubt on (thus bringing on a political crisis), namely that Bentinck had the 
support of the British ministers in London. But now he felt certain that the 
matter was resolved, the queen’s influence was finally extinguished (“the 
cause of all our past embarrassments”), the king was neutralized, the prince 
had full powers, and the ministry enjoyed his confidence. It was one in whose 
work he now played a full part, attending the council regularly and meeting 
ministers at his house or theirs to discuss tactics and policies, and the shaping 
of the new constitution. So involved had Bentinck become in bestowing a 
constitution on Sicily that he gave the matter priority over all other consid- 
erations, including the question urged by the British ministers of his taking 
over the command in Spain. It seemed to the egregious Sir Robert Wilson 
(disliked by Bentinck for his interference in the politics of the court at 
Palermo), who was staying in Sicily at this time, that the British were 
“preparing for a crusade, with the British code of laws on the points of our 
bayonets.”61 
Bentinck felt that he could now boast that the old system against which the 
British had been contending for the past six years had at last been “com- 

<<<PDFPAGE 107>>>
Bentinck and British Intervention, Stage One 
103 
pletely demolished.”©? But there is a certain irony about this boast. Only 
eight months before he had told Bunbury, “We are now masters and kings if 
you please,”°? while seven months later he was to find himself forced to 
assume the role of dictator. 
Castlereagh was not entirely pleased when he learnt from Bentinck what 
had happened in March. “We must,” he wrote back, “so try to arrange as to 
obviate the necessity of frequent acts of undisguised power.” Withholding 
the subsidy, he thought, should be a sufficient curb on the king, and stronger 
measures ought not to be necessary.°* It was certainly true that financial 
embarrassment continued to force the king and the prince to lean heavily on 
the British. The king complained of being burdened by debt, which he would 
not allow parliament to shoulder, fearing that the latter might at some time 
call him publicly to account. Bentinck, who the previous November had 
intervened to prevent parliament voting as much as a fifth of its revenue to 
meet the needs of the royal family, now had to lend the king and the prince 
enough money to tide them over.©> But he thought that the situation in 
Sicily was sufficiently stable for him to leave it to take command in eastern 
Spain, where his army had in the past few months suffered from unsatisfac- 
tory leadership. Accordingly he sailed from the island on 27 May 1813, 
delegating to Lord Montgomerie his plenary powers as minister. Neither 
Belmonte nor Orleans had wanted Bentinck to go away, but the latter felt it 
his only chance for what he termed “recovering his troops,” by which he 
meant, presumably, being able to retain a lien on them for a future Italian 
expedition.©® Even so, he took the precaution of taking with him a private 
letter in which Belmonte had given him warning of democratic perils ahead, 
so that he could, if the occasion demanded, justify a sudden return to Sicily.°” 

<<<PDFPAGE 108>>>
8 
Lord William Bentinck and British Intervention, 
Stage Two 
uring the absence of Bentinck in Spain, things went badly wrong in 
D Sicily, indeed so wrong that government, through lack of funds, was 
brought completely to a standstill, and parliament refused to grant money 
until its radical demands were met. Why things went wrong is a matter for 
debate. The Abbate Balsamo took the view that the troubles were really 
entirely due to the absence of Bentinck’s guiding hand in a political scene that 
was highly volatile.! Bentinck’s biographer, Dr. Rosselli, does not go as far as 
this, but he does consider that Bentinck’s presence would have prevented 
much of the friction—friction between Sicilians and British and between the 
various political factions.? Another historian, Dr. Lackland, thinks that much 
of the trouble stemmed from the recent departure of the queen, fear and 
dislike for whom had for so long bound together the parliamentarians. But 
she does concede that with Bentinck there things would not have reached the 
pitch they did, even though he could not have prevented a radical movement 
from emerging.* 
There is one factor that stands out in the turbulent scene of the summer 
months and that is the degree to which popular feeling was now directed 
against the British, something that struck Bentinck forcibly when he even- 
tually returned to the island.* It was they who had backed the constitution, 
and its enemies were not slow to associate all current troubles with the British 
presence. The British were really an obvious scapegoat for every fresh ill that 
affected the island, whether it was the threat of plague, the high prices of 
food in the towns, or the use of troops and martial law to restore order in the 
capital. A very serious outbreak of plague that affected Malta in 1813 had 
resulted in the Sicilian government imposing strict quarantine regulations 
and interpreting them in such a way that trade with the outside world was 
stopped. The British merchants were furious that licenses were being refused 
them to allow the landing of goods and passengers even from plague- 
free England and Spain, while attempts to get a relaxation of the total 
prohibition against ships from Malta calling at any Sicilian port gave rise to 
public accusations of officials’ disregarding public safety. The British army 
too was incensed at the government’s lack of cooperation in even the simplest 
104 

<<<PDFPAGE 109>>>
Bentinck and British Intervention, Stage Two 
105 
matters of routine, its refusal to allow troops to land and even to permit the 
landing of some cavalry horses dispatched from Egypt. The question of the 
horses became so sensitive that it gave rise to a debate in parliament. The 
horses were landed in defiance of the rules, on express orders from General 
Macfarlane, but eventually they were sent off to Spain, after Lord 
Montgomerie intervened. Marching the horses across the island would, he 
realized, have meant using force.® 
Force did have to be used in Palermo when on 18 and 19 July 1813 riots 
and disorders occurred in the capital. Provoked initially by the shortages of 
food and the profiteering accompanying them, they were naturally exploited 
by all those people who were eager to discredit the ministry. Indeed the food 
crisis had been induced by the removal of price controls—a policy adopted by 
Castelnuovo, minister of finance and a disciple of free trade—and Bentinck 
was forced a few months later to reintroduce a “law of the maximum.” British 
troops and artillery had to be used to restore order and the ringleaders of the 
revolt were executed.°® 
But all this was merely the background to a major constitutional crisis. A 
split had occurred in the ranks of the ministry and its supporters in parlia- 
ment, brought about by differences over abolishing feudal rights and entail. 
It was an ideological argument that divided Belmonte from his uncle Cas- 
telnuovo, though strong personal and material considerations also entered 
into it, causing Belmonte to turn his coat in order to side with the demo- 
crats.” There had emerged in the parliament a radical democratic party led by 
men influenced by France and the ideas of the French Revolution. Chief 
among these was a lawyer Rossi (the so-called “Mirabeau of Sicily”) and an 
ex-shopkeeper called Gaspare Vaccaro, president of the Board of Health (a 
body the British found highly obstructive). The latter was so plausible that he 
made quite an impression on Bentinck, who later described him favorably as 
being both “able and educated.”§ Both Vaccaro and Rossi, had spent some 
time living in revolutionary France, and Rossi had no love for the English, 
having once been imprisoned by Nelson. These two and their followers (men 
from the Sicily outside Palermo who were jealous of the capital and all that it 
represented) stood for the destruction of noble privileges and for a fairer 
distribution of wealth—cheaper food, agrarian reform, the breaking up of 
great estates, more church property to be sold, and an equitable system of 
taxation. They were certainly not the red revolutionaries of the extreme 
Jacobin kind, though their radicalism so alarmed the British that Macfarlane 
did not consider it safe to dispatch any reinforcements to Spain. Their tactic 
in the parliament was the classic one of steadily refusing to vote any taxes, and 
referring everything to committees on which they were always well repre- 
sented, until their first demand was met—the amalgamation of the houses of 
parliament.? 
At the end of July 1813 the ministry resigned, demoralized by Belmonte’s 

<<<PDFPAGE 110>>>
106 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
opportunism and frightened of a probable vote of censure for having intro- 
duced martial law. Montgomerie managed to get the prince to appoint a 
phantom ministry in order to maintain the semblance of government, and he 
toyed with the idea of a dissolution, which Orleans favored but the prince did 
not.!° Bentinck had written to dissuade Montgomerie not only from dissoly- 
ing parliament, but also from using violence against men like Rossi. Neither 
the press nor parliament in Britain would tolerate arresting men merely on 
account of their opinions, and in any event he did not think a revolution in 
Sicily likely, the people in general being far too timid. If there was to be a 
revolution, it was better that it should come from the actions of the parlia- 
ment than from any unconstitutional acts of the crown.1!! 
Montgomerie accepted Bentinck’s advice. Nevertheless he persuaded the 
prince to veto two resolutions of parliament that he regarded as dangerous: 
an attempt to get control of the army by using it to enforce the quarantine, 
and sending a deputation to London to complain of the conduct of British 
generals. At one stage he went so far as to threaten the parliament with 
instant dissolution if it still refused to vote supplies, it now having adopted 
the ruse of voting enough for a few months only, with the aim (or so 
Montgomerie thought) of perpetuating its existence and becoming a kind of 
Sicilian Long Parliament. When in the face of Montgomerie’s threat it did 
vote taxes for a year ahead, it still did not vote the ways and means by which 
those taxes could be raised, so Montgomerie got the prince to prorogue it 
until Bentinck returned at the start of October. 
+2 
Bentinck left Spain reluctantly and to Wellington’s extreme displeasure, 
and only because of the urgent pleas of the hereditary prince, the duke of 
Orleans, Generals Montgomerie and Macfarlane, and a number of Sicilian 
constitutionalists.!2 He was, it was clear, the only man who could resolve the 
crisis in Sicily. Montgomerie and his secretary Joseph Smith had been 
suffering from the effects of ill-health (they both died before the year was 
out), and neither had the experience to deal with the complex political 
situation that had developed since Bentinck left, Montgomerie being no 
diplomatist and Smith being new to Sicily.14 
Bentinck landed once more in Sicily on 3 October 1813. At once he set 
about trying to find a solution through the parliament, addressing its mem- 
bers personally and lobbying them in a final endeavor to get the financial 
measures adopted. When this failed, he had to resort to getting the prince to 
dissolve the parliament having appointed a new ministry whose members 
Bentinck picked himself.!5 But this ministry, for all its compliance, did not have 
the required courage to proclaim a state of emergency, which Bentinck now 
reckoned to be essential, if only because attacks on property had brought in a 
new and alarming element. So Bentinck himself proclaimed the emergency, 
assuming full responsibility for maintaining order in the island. He ordered 
his troops to march into the interior, where apparently they were 

<<<PDFPAGE 111>>>
Bentinck and British Intervention, Stage Two 
107 
well received, and he threatened with trial by a military court anyone who 
disturbed the peace. He had become, like Oliver Cromwell, a military dic- 
tator malgré lui.1© 
This patent admission of failure to find a political solution of a liberal kind, 
toward which his principles propelled him, underlined his now deep disillu- 
sion with Sicilians as political animals. The hereditary prince he now re- 
garded as weaker than the weakest of his subjects; Belmonte as more 
dangerous as a friend than an enemy; the nobles as lacking in education, 
courage, and the knowledge of public affairs; and the people as wanting the 
advantages of liberty without being prepared to serve or to pay for them.!7 
Bentinck seemed at last to have been converted to the views of the consul 
general Fagan. The latter for months had been counselling him to use the 
methods of a dictator with this people who failed to show any respect for 
familiarity or kindly treatment—“too long accustomed to the rod of iron not 
to get wild when you give them a certain liberty.”!8 Yet really Bentinck had 
known it all along. Writing in his journal in June 1812 he had referred to the 
Sicilians as “bullies, only to be managed by fear; they take advantage of the 
least concessions and always construe conciliation into weakness.”!? “Vanity 
was the great defect of the country” he had again confided to his journal, and 
he “doubted whether [they] were made for freedom.” It was a pity, he came 
to think, that the court had not paid attention to him when he first arrived in 
Sicily, for it would have been easier for the British to act through one single 
authority (the king) than through a Sicilian parliament.?° 
The nobles who at the beginning led the movement for the constitution 
were now for the most part content to destroy it, frightened of the prospect 
of universal suffrage and of the nationalization of private land. They had 
imagined they were going to return to their positions in the Middle Ages, 
free of the kind of royal restrictions imposed at the end of the eighteenth 
century by the king’s reforming viceroys. When they realized that what they 
had opened was in fact a Pandora’s box of change, they saw the time had 
come to backtrack. As Joseph Smith succinctly expressed it, “Rather than 
yield to the people they would yield to the crown again.”?? In these circum- 
stances Bentinck had felt he had no alternative but to intervene. “Sicily can 
only be saved from political convulsions by our interference 
. 
. 
. if we don’t 
direct, the enemy will.” 
As Luisa Giardina has pointed out in her article on Bentinck and his 
Sicilian journal, Bentinck had not initially been someone intent on the use of 
force or convinced that he must direct the Sicilians. Rather it had been 
Belmonte who had pressed him continually to be more ruthless and chided 
him for being too moderate and goodnatured. But Belmonte, as Bentinck 
came to realize, was largely interested in using the British to get rid of his 
enemies for him, and he had even tried in Bentinck’s absence to induce Smith 
and Montgomerie to arrest 560 of his opponents. Little wonder that Ben- 

<<<PDFPAGE 112>>>
108 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
tinck became so disillusioned with his Sicilians who reacted only, as he 
expressed it, to the strong man who was content to offer them “bonbons in 
the one hand and the stick in the other.”?% 
Yet blanket condemnations of a whole people seldom succeed in carrying 
conviction. Here they seem rather to be a reflection on Bentinck himself and 
on his attempt to impose a constitution on Sicily (“in part a creation of your 
own” as Castlereagh had written to him)?* through a small section of the 
aristocracy, in which he had placed an unjustified faith. The people by whom 
he had been “let down” were not the mass of Sicilians nor the majority of the 
literate, for they had not even been consulted. It had been a mistake to allow 
the old parliament to act as a constituent assembly, because by its traditional 
composition it could represent only minority interests. As Enzo Sciacca has 
pointed out,?° the radicals of the Demaniale and later those in the House of 
Commons were absent from the constitutional debates and the making of the 
constitution of 1812. He suggests (as Giovanni Aceto did earlier writing in 
1827) that had the Sicilian radicals taken part in a proper constituent assem- 
bly, possibly much of the disequilibrium of the new order would have been 
avoided, and perhaps the constitutional experiment initiated by Bentinck 
might not have failed. This is certainly a large supposition. But when Ben- 
tinck got the prince to dissolve the parliament in October, the democrats in it 
whom he accused of endeavoring to usurp the functions of both executive 
and judiciary were not the extremists they have been painted by the constitu- 
tionalist historians. Not even the most extreme of them ever tried to force the 
hand of the prince over the selection of his ministers. Sciacca thinks it a pity 
that some were not chosen to hold ministerial posts (an idea Castelnuovo had 
earlier toyed with), for if they had been they could have done a good deal to 
calm the troubled waters.?© Of course the radicals were anti-British, because 
they saw in British intervention the propping up of the dominant aristocracy 
and a captive monarchy, maintained through British subsidies. To them the 
continued British presence was the greatest obstacle existing to the kind of 
constitution they wished for Sicily. 

<<<PDFPAGE 113>>>
2 
Sicily as a Base for Offensive Operations, 
1812-1814 
Qe the threat of French invasion seemed for the moment to have 
receded from Sicily, the ministry in London was naturally anxious to 
use its garrison for offensive operations. It was simply playing Napoleon’s 
game to leave so large a force immobile while the war was being fought in 
Spain and Portugal. Liverpool expressed the hope to Bentinck, writing in 
June 1811, that it should be possible to reduce the garrison to the minimum 
figure of the 10,000 men stipulated in the treaty of alliance. Could not the 
Sicilians themselves contribute more to their own defense? For this purpose 
the British government were dispatching immediately to Sicily 14,000 stands 
of arms to help equip the Sicilian forces.! 
The deficiencies of the royal army have been referred to more than once. 
Bentinck told Circello in August 1811] that he had grave doubts about its 
effectiveness, consisting as it did of Neapolitans, anxious only to return to 
their homes, and foreigners and deserters from the French, many of whom 
were extremely disaffected. This army was not only ineffectual, a grave 
burden on the royal finances, but also a serious political danger. In the past 
year it had grown by as many as 5,000 men, augmented by a stream of 
French deserters and prisoners of war from Catalonia, until it now numbered 
19,000. At the instigation of the queen, it might, Bentinck thought, ally itself 
with the French. The remedy he recommended and caused the hereditary 
prince to adopt was to cut its numbers by about 5,000. As he told the prince, 
a few thousand less would make no difference to recovering Naples. The 
whole of its non-Neapolitan element was accordingly discharged in May 
1812, the Germans being recruited by the British army, the Italians formed 
into a corps of their own augmented by 2,000 prisoners of war that the 
British government shipped out from England. And later in the year the king 
agreed to reduce the number of his royal guards from 1,600 to 700 (half of 
whom were to be Sicilian)? 
Yet, despite all these economies, Bentinck still had to lend the prince 
money in June 1812 and in the following January to keep the royal army in 
existence; otherwise it would have dissolved for lack of clothing, pay, and 
food. The appalling state of the royal army was brought home forcibly to 
109 

<<<PDFPAGE 114>>>
110 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
Bentinck when, prior to their embarkation for Spain, Sicilian regiments were 
marched into Palermo to be reviewed by him and Macfarlane. “Many were 
without shoes, none had great-coats, and the whole looked as if they had 
been half-starved.”? The cloth needed to’ provide them with uniforms Ben- 
tinck had to have sent out from England. It was this same financial exigency 
that had forced the British to assume the responsibility for the royal flotilla. 
In return for thus propping up the monarchy and guaranteeing once again 
the sovereignty of Sicily to the royal family, Britain obtained an important 
asset in the shape of a sizeable Sicilian contingent, available for service 
outside Sicily, although within the bounds of the Mediterranean. By a treaty 
agreed to in September 1812, the signing of which the king was constrained 
to delegate to the hereditary prince, a division of 7,300 Sicilians was put at 
the service of the British commander. Its cost was deducted from the British 
subsidy and its recruitment, clothing, and equipment were to be Britain’s 
responsibility. The troops that during the next eighteen months were to be 
sent to fight in Spain consisted in part of these Sicilians and the recently 
formed Italian Levy. This levy had had its origins in what had been called the 
“Calabrian Free Corps,” formed by Stuart with the king’s permission in the 
early months of 1809, when 1,500 Calabrians, fleeing from Masséna’s extir- 
patory drive to eliminate the last traces of the masse, crossed over to Sicily. 
Rather than allow them to be recruited into the ranks of the royal army (seen 
now only as an instrument for forwarding the intrigues of the queen), Stuart 
took them into British pay, and appointed a British officer, Colonel Sir John 
Dalrymple, to inspect and train them. Its strength had soon risen to 500, 
divided into seven companies, and it was well reported on by Stuart. Initially 
bound only to serve within the territory of the kingdom of Naples, it was 
agreed in 1810 it could serve throughout the Mediterranean, and 300 of this 
corps were then dispatched as reinforcements to the island of Zante.5 But the 
aim of Bentinck was to form a force that would one day land on the mainland 
of Italy, the vanguard of a great national army to liberate that country from 
its foreign rulers. 
The objects for which the Sicilian garrison could be used for offensive 
operations was the subject of discussion both in England and Spain. Since the 
outbreak of war in the latter country there had been Spanish and English 
voices urging an expedition from Sicily to help the Spanish in Catalonia, and 
this was a policy that found favor with Wellington, the British commander- 
in-chief in Spain, and also with Lord Liverpool, first when he was secretary 
for war and subsequently when he was prime minister. Italy was the other 
possible objective. Wellesley, the foreign secretary until he was replaced by 
Castlereagh in March 1812, had shown an interest in this theater, as did 
Bunbury, now under secretary for war and William Hamilton, foreign under 
secretary. But in holding these views, they were largely alone, and Bentinck, 
who was an enthusiast for the idea of Italian liberation, was very hesitant at 

<<<PDFPAGE 115>>>
Sicily as a Base for Offensive Operations, 1812-1814 
Pe 
first about the timing of such an operation. Castlereagh, like most of his 
ministerial colleagues, shared the views enunciated in a memorandum edited 
by Huskisson in 1809, namely, not to excite any country to take up arms 
against the enemy, but to give it every possible help once it had been aroused 
to resistance. By April 1813 most ministers in London had lost faith in a 
rising in Italy.° 
In a dispatch of June 1811 Liverpool had outlined the uses to which 
Bentinck might put his troops and mentioned certain desirable objectives, 
while leaving Bentinck the ultimate discretion over the choice he made of 
these. If the occasion presented itself, Corfu should be seized, though at 
limited cost; a force should be sent to protect Minorca, if it looked like being 
threatened from Toulon; troops could be sent to Catalonia, provided Sicily 
was not in danger. What Bentinck was instructed vot to do was to waste his 
efforts recovering Naples. Its capture depended on French fortunes in the- 
aters remote from southern Italy and, as Moore had written earlier, it was not 
likely to “run away.”” 
Four months later Bentinck was told to coordinate his plans for forming an 
army in Italy with the Archduke Francis, a member of the house of Austria- 
Este with claims to the throne of Modena. But this was merely contingency 
planning, and Wellesley in writing to Bentinck emphasized that there was no 
intention of encouraging the Italians to revolt or of becoming involved in a 
project that might be risky or premature. The island of Sardinia, it was 
envisaged, might form the base for future operations, and the sum of 
£50,000 was made available to equip the Sardinians, while a further £50,000 
was to be given to the Archduke Francis.* This was the fruit of a project of 
Bentinck, suggested by him to Wellesley in June and put into his head by 
Metternich’s agent, Colonel Nugent, an Austrian officer (though in fact an 
Irishman by birth). Bentinck had thought the projected uprising was well 
worth backing at so little cost, and on his return from five weeks in Sicily had 
waxed even more enthusiastic. He had even proposed that he should send to 
Sardinia two or three British regiments from Sicily to serve for the immediate 
defense of that island and encourage that kingdom to raise and pay a new 
army for Italian operations. Failing the acceptance of this proposal, he 
suggested taking into British pay as many troops of the king of Sardinia as 
the sum of £10,000 would furnish. The king of Sardinia however demurred, 
fearing a loss of his independence and possibly also French reprisals.” 
The lack of any reliable evidence of a national Italian resistance movement 
had forced Bentinck to agree reluctantly that the time was not ripe for 
intervention. The claims made by Prince Moliterno to be the leader of such a 
resistance struck him as being completely absurd. Looking however to plans 
for the future, Bentinck submitted that Corsica and Elba would form suitable 
bases for invading northern Italy, and only if these were ruled out as objec- 
tives would he think of sending his troops to Spain.!° The mention of 

<<<PDFPAGE 116>>>
WI 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
Corsica and Elba was harking back more than fifteen years to the days of the 
Anglo-Corsican kingdom, memories of which ill-fated episode were still fresh 
in the minds of ministers; and as Admiral Pellew pointed out, both islands 
would need a permanent garrison. So Bentinck was very firmly told that any 
attempt to take these two islands would be a total waste of resources, and as 
there was so little evidence of any popular movement in Italy, surplus troops 
could best be employed in support of the Spanish in Catalonia. They could 
always be recalled to Sicily and made available for Italy, if prospects there 
became more inviting. 
!1 
A month before this dispatch was penned, Bentinck at the request of the 
navy, which had been pressing for it since the previous summer, had sent a 
small force of 600 men to occupy Lissa in the Adriatic, an island of greater 
strategic importance than any of the southern Ionian islands. It was to serve 
as a depot for trade, as a port for the Adriatic squadron, and as a means of 
maintaining links with both the Italian and Balkan shores.!? But any attempt 
to seize Corfu, as mentioned by Liverpool in his earlier dispatch, was never 
seriously considered by Bentinck, its very powerful fortifications being de- 
fended by a garrison of 13,000. Britain did not possess in Sicily the resources 
for military operations, at least on any extended scale, in both eastern and 
western Mediterranean. 
One way however in which some of Bentinck’s forces, once part of the 
Sicilian garrison and now stationed in the Adriatic, could he thought be 
more usefully employed than sitting in the Ionian islands was in helping a 
Montenegrin revolt. On information received from Lissa (information that 
later proved false) that there was a rising in Montenegro against the occupy- 
ing French, Bentinck on 8 June 1812 authorized Admiral Fremantle, the 
naval commander in the Adriatic, to go to the help of these “freedom- 
fighters,” taking whatever force he thought fit from the garrison on the 
Ionian islands. He told Fremantle that it was his policy (indeed it was that of 
the British cabinet) not to encourage any partial rising in Italy or the 
Adriatic, but to wait until “a general effort can be made by all nations 
engaged in the same cause.” But nevertheless, since it now appeared that a 
revolt had in fact broken out and that war between France and Russia was 
certain—a war that might very well touch off a major rising in Italy—he 
thought it right to assist the Montenegrins. 13 
Fremantle collected at once 1,200 troops from the Zante garrison, em- 
barked 400 on his own warships and took them to the island of Lissa, and left 
the remainder to follow on in troop transports as soon as they could. By the 
middle of July he was getting ready to escort his little force across to 
Montenegro, when he learnt that no rising had taken place. The French were, 
it seemed, in complete control. Naturally, when Bentinck learned of this, he 
cancelled his orders for the expedition and the troops were returned to their 
billets in Zante, and his political agents brought back to Palermo.14 

<<<PDFPAGE 117>>>
Sicily as a Base for Offensive Operations, 1812-1814 
113 
Over the question of Catalonia, Bentinck forestalled Liverpool by sending 
his brother to Portugal to coordinate plans with Wellington, and it was 
agreed that Bentinck should find a force of 10,000 from Sicily to arrive in 
Spain by the end of June. Even so, the final decision to choose Spain and not 
Italy had been left to Bentinck’s sole discretion, though the tone of Liver- 
pool’s dispatch made it clear that he hoped he would opt for Spain. In the 
event, Bentinck hesitated, thought he would go to Italy after all, and infuri- 
ated Wellington, who was counting on the force arriving from Sicily as part 
of his overall plan of campaign. Liverpool tried to blame Bentinck for 
disobeying “clear” instructions (“as positive as any which can be given to a 
commander so circumstanced”).!5 Bathurst however supported Bentinck. 
He told Wellington that Bentinck’s instructions were in the last resort con- 
ditional, and that though he thought his decision wrong, there was no doubt 
that Bentinck had acted properly.!© By that time Bentinck had changed his 
plans, and his troops were sent to Spain after all, though not the 10,000 
Wellington had been promised.+7 
What had led to all these changes during the month of June 1812 had been 
reports from Nugent and from allied agents in Italy that its people (especially 
in the Roman states) were on the point of rising up. This was clearly wishful 
thinking and depended, as Bentinck himself admitted, on whether the Rus- 
sians entered the war. He justified his decision to switch from the Spanish to 
the Italian plan by arguing that the force he could spare for Spain was too 
small on its own to make any impact, while his 14—18,000 troops would 
outnumber any army the French could bring against him in Italy, now that so 
many of their men had been sent north for the Russian campaign. And any 
success achieved in Italy would benefit the cause of the allies in Spain. His 
force moreover would include 5,000 Neapolitan troops, thus removing a 
danger from Sicily while easing the path of royal acceptance of Sicily’s recent 
constitution.!8 But this was no sensible military reason for undertaking the 
operation. What appeared uppermost in his mind was the risk of losing an 
opportunity (perhaps one that might never recur) of leading a war of national 
liberation. Bentinck’s arguments were not convincing, and Wellington, who 
had his own good reasons for wanting a force in eastern Spain, was certainly 
right when he wrote to Bathurst that the French would never allow an army 
of 15,000 Anglo-Neapolitans to remain for long on Italian soil.’ 
Within a matter ‘of less than three weeks, Bentinck had changed his mind 
again. The news of a Franco-Austrian treaty and of a letter to the prince 
regent sent by the Habsburg emperor that urged him to send his army to 
Spain rather than into Italy (where Austrian troops at Napoleon’s behest 
might well be called on to fight the British) had caused Bentinck to have 
second thoughts. Maitland with 7,000 men would go to eastern Spain after 
all, although Bentinck confessed he had little faith in their being able to 
achieve very much there.?° As matters turned out, the late arrival of the force 

<<<PDFPAGE 118>>>
114 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
from Sicily on 31 July and numbering 3,000 less than promised was not the 
setback it might have been for the fortunes of Wellington’s campaign. Suchet, 
the general commanding the French in Valencia and Catalonia, had been so 
alarmed at the rumored arrival at any time in June or July of a great army 
from Sicily that he did exactly what Wellington wanted and sent no help to 
Soult or Joseph, with whose forces Wellington was engaged.?! 
The force from Sicily was never one that Wellington could depend on 
unreservedly, so its usefulness to him was limited. Bathurst made plain that 
the Sicilian situation must take priority over all others so far as Maitland’s 
troops were concerned, and that Wellington must let them go if Sicily was 
ever threatened.?? This is precisely the situation that occurred in the spring of 
1813 when Sicily’s political situation forced Bentinck to act against the court. 
The 44th Regiment, destined for Spain, had to be diverted to Sicily from 
Malta, and General Murray, then the commander of the Sicilian forces in 
Spain, was required to send back 2,000 troops.?? All this not unnaturally was 
unwelcome news to the ministers in London, who had recently been pressing 
Bentinck to send more of his men in the opposite direction. Spain in their 
view was the only theater in which Britain could afford to exert herself?4 (a 
point that had to be made clear to the Russians, who tried to involve 
Bentinck and his government in financing a force of 40,000 men to be sent 
from Moldavia to the Adriatic to cooperate in an Italian campaign).?5 If 
Sicily was not a liability it was scarcely the asset the cabinet hoped for. 
Nevertheless during 1813, as Bentinck was later at pains to point out in 
trying to justify constitutional change as the means by which Sicily had been 
made stable, there were dispatched from Sicily to Spain 13,500 troops, 2,500 
of whom were Sicilian soldiers, the remainder British, Italian, and German.2¢ 
Bentinck himself had left Sicily for Spain on 27 May 1813, only to be forced 
to return in October to restore the political situation. With the remainder of 
his army he now planned to invade Italy. 
Already in January 1813 Bentinck was outlining to Bathurst his scheme to 
use the Italian Levy as the cadre for constructing an Italian army. He wrote in 
almost glowing terms of the military qualities of its two corps—something 
perhaps difficult to understand, considering the poor record for desertion 
one Italian regiment earned in Spain,27 though doubtless it would have 
shown more spirit had it been fighting in Italy. Ponza, the island in the Bay of 
Naples his forces had captured in February, was to become, he hoped, the 
springboard and funnel for an Italian invasion. Twelve-hundred troops were 
earmarked for it, leaving behind in Sicily just sufficient men to garrison 
Palermo and the fortresses of Milazzo and Messina. The situation, he told 
Bathurst, was more favorable than it had been in 1812, and though he 
realized that Spain was important, Spain alone could not defeat Napoleon. 
To do this, the greater part of the peoples of Europe must be brought into 
the field and that meant involving Italy (“Italy is that instrument—Italy is 

<<<PDFPAGE 119>>>
Sicily as a Base for Offensive Operations, 1812-1814 
115 
ripe for it”). There the British could act as principals, not as auxiliaries, as 
they were in Spain; and he painted himself a rosy picture of his army from 
Sicily taking Naples and then recruiting as it went along, heading for the 
north of Italy with its numbers swollen to 40,000.28 
Again he mentioned the political reasons for getting his army across to 
Italy. The Neapolitan troops in Sicily were a continuing source of anxiety, the 
Sicilian parliament was unwilling to pay them, and their officers were ready 
to desert. Had the passage to Naples been less difficult, many he was sure 
would long since have joined Murat. They were such a threat to security that 
while they remained in Sicily British troops would have to be left behind to 
ensure the safety of that island. It was for this reason that he had to tell 
Bathurst, writing in May 1813, he could not comply with the latter’s request 
to send back two regiments to Spain.?? To further influence the British 
cabinet in favor of his Italian strategy, Bentinck sent home his secretary, 
Lamb, to present his views in more detail to Castlereagh. In a long and 
persuasive memorandum Lamb presented at the end of May, designed for the 
eyes of Lord Castlereagh, it was argued that only by the opening up of a third 
front, this time in Italy, could Napoleon be decisively defeated. Napoleon 
had drained Italy of troops in order to support his campaigns elsewhere, and 
all the intelligence reports received showed that Italians would rise to a man 
before a French army could cross the Alps. To be forced to fight a campaign 
in Italy would put an enormous, perhaps an intolerable, strain on Napoleon’s 
available resources. The British corps now at Alicante could never hope to 
achieve very much, because it was tied to its transports for supplies. “Every 
article, even the straw for the whole of the horses for the army during the 
entire winter has been sent from Sicily at a great expense and with a very 
considerable quantity of shipping.” Italy, on the other hand, could supply all 
an army might require. And if revolts broke out in Italy as a result of allied 
successes and a British army was not then sent there to take advantage of the 
situation, Austria and Russia would benefit; yet it was important to the 
future of the continent and to England that Italian affairs should be taken out 
of the reach of these powers and placed firmly under English protection. 
Bentinck, so the memorandum went on, thought that the hereditary prince 
would not offer very much opposition to undertaking this enterprise, based 
as it was “upon liberal principles which appear to be necessary for its 
suiccess.1 90 
It does not seem to have occurred to Bentinck that the introduction of 
political arguments (and ones that might not appeal to Castlereagh) into 
what was primarily a military proposition could do any thing other than to 
weaken his case. 
When Bentinck wrote to Bathurst in February he sent Wellington a copy 
of his letter. Sensing the danger to his plans of any withdrawal of Bentinck’s 
army, then deployed in eastern Spain, Wellington wrote forcefully to 

<<<PDFPAGE 120>>>
116 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
Bathurst of the hopelessness of an undertaking involving sending the army to 
Italy. Unless the government were prepared to arm, clothe, and equip an 
Italian army on the scale it had done in Spain and Portugal, and in addition 
put into the field a force of 30-40,000, the whole scheme was foredoomed to 
failure. His anxiety was unnecessary since there was no chance of the ministry 
ever agreeing to such a scheme. As Bathurst wrote privately to Bentinck, 
“among our rulers . 
. 
. there is no belief of any character or energy among the 
Italians.”3! The bulk of the army of Sicily therefore continued to serve in 
Spain. 
What Bentinck was now contemplating was a much more limited enter- 
prise, to keep Murat’s army guessing, but also to rid Sicily of the disaffected 
Neapolitan troops. General Macfarlane, understandably, was not happy 
about the project of launching an immediate attack on Calabria, viewing the 
matter, as Bentinck did not, from a purely military standpoint. Landing 
troops in the kingdom of Naples, unless the operation succeeded, would do 
nothing to solve Sicilian problems and Macfarlane had little hope of success 
with only two British battalions available out of a total strength of 12,000. 
Bentinck was not deflected from his plan so easily. He argued that the loss of 
the Neapolitan troops would cause the Sicilians to make greater efforts in 
providing for their own defense and would also save them a lot of money. He 
had long deplored the attitude of the native Sicilians toward military service 
and their refusal to accept conscription, agreeing with what Belmonte had 
said to him about their desiring contradictions—no foreigners in their army, 
yet no Sicilian to be forced to serve in it; a large army to defend their island, 
yet no taxes to pay for it. It was quite sufficient to his mind that there was a 
chance of the plan succeeding.3? Fortunately for the reputation of both the 
British forces and Bentinck, the expedition did not take place, at least not for 
another eight months, and then it was sent to another part of Italy and in 
quite altered circumstances. The renewed fear of a French invasion (the 
combined threat from the Toulon fleet and 20,000 of Murat’s troops), 
followed by the political crisis that resulted in Bentinck’s return from Spain, 
meant that no expedition to Naples from Sicily would take place that year. As 
for the invasion threat, it proved to be nothing but a piece of bluff. When 
Bentinck cited it as a reason for his not returning two battalions to Spain, 
Wellington commented somewhat tartly that in his opinion the island of 
Sicily was “in no danger whatsoever,” an opinion that Admiral Pellew 
endorsed. Wellington and Pellew proved to be right, but it did not alter 
Bentinck’s decision.33 
What was attempted in December, however, on a very much less ambitious 
scale, was the dispatch of an Italian regiment to seize Leghorn by a coup de 
main. It was made on the initiative of Colonel Catinelli, the commander of 
the Third Italian Regiment, and Bentinck initially had misgivings about its 
chances of success, preferring it to go only to Ponza and there to make a more 

<<<PDFPAGE 121>>>
