SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
detailed reconnaissance. However he soon convinced himself that he must 
not let slip any occasion for rallying the Italian people and giving them the 
opportunity “to assert their rights, liberty and independence.” He hoped that 
another Catalonia would be created in Tuscany and that its population would 
hasten to join the standard of a national revolt. It all very quickly came to 
nothing. After capturing Viareggio and Lucca and beating off an attack from 
Pisa, the expedition failed in its aim of forcing the port of Leghorn to 
surrender. Bentinck blamed the lack of Congreve rockets, though bad 
weather also played a part in curtailing the operation.*4 In any case too much 
was expected of a force consisting of 1,000 men only. It is true that forty- 
seven years later Garibaldi with a more famous “Thousand” achieved spec- 
tacular success in Sicily, but the circumstances in Tuscany in 1813 were rather 
different. It seems that where his political principles and Italian liberation 
were concerned, Bentinck remained an incurable romantic. 
Shortly after this minor fiasco, another expedition was planned—this time 
for a force of 5,000 men to take possession of Bastia, Bentinck having learnt 
from the navy of an Italian regiment there asking the British to take it away. 
Were this enterprise to succeed, Bastia, it was planned, was to act as a base for 
the invasion of the north of Italy and the rest of the army of Sicily was to be 
sent there as soon as possible.*5 The scheme was never carried out for, with 
the news of Murat’s defection and his undertaking to join the allies, Bentinck 
was told to sign an armistice. This he did on 3 February 1813 on the basis of 
military cooperation to drive the French out of Italy. 
The armistice was the culmination of negotiations going back nine 
months. Murat, once he had given up command of the Grand Army in 
Poland, had fallen from favor with Napoleon with whom his relations had 
long been strained. Murat was now anxious to secure his throne, to enlarge 
his kingdom at the Pope’s expense, to aim perhaps for the throne of Italy and 
to displace Britain in the role of liberator. Once the British had captured 
Ponza, their contacts with Naples became quite easy, and these resulted in 
Bentinck calling there en route for Spain in June 1813 in an endeavor to 
persuade Murat to relinquish his claim to the throne of Naples. Murat had no 
intention of doing so, as became clear once Bentinck reached Ponza, though 
Bentinck hoped for a temporary accord by which both Murat and Ferdinand 
would recognize each others’ claims and pledge themselves that they would 
not contest them until the question of compensation was settled. Though he 
did get the hereditary prince to accept “with considerable disinclination” the 
principle of compensation, Bentinck could not carry Murat with him. The 
latter continued to pin his hopes on the allies offering him the crown, and he 
was buoyed up by the recent news of Napoleon’s victories at Liitzen and 
Bautzen. Bentinck remained completely opposed to offering the crown to 
Murat, convinced (as was Castlereagh) that once he was sure of it, he would 

<<<PDFPAGE 123>>>
Sicily as a Base for Offensive Operations, 1812-1814 
119 
cease to exert himself for the allies. Seeing that the cause of Napoleon was 
lost, it would not be very long (Bentinck thought) before Murat renewed the 
negotiations.?° 
Six months later this proved to be the case. Approaches were made on 
Murat’s behalf through General Manhés, commanding in Calabria, and the 
Neapolitan secretary of legation at the court of Vienna, the Chevalier 
Schinina; but Bentinck refused to make any concessions. Napoleon’s decisive 
defeat at Leipzig made Murat’s position uncomfortable, and he could not 
take his army northward without exposing his kingdom in Naples. Bentinck 
could therefore see no reason to prejudice the claims of Ferdinand, and he so 
informed the hereditary prince.3”7 Meanwhile he was being pressed by Aus- 
tria and by Lord Aberdeen, British envoy at Vienna, to sign an armistice with 
Murat in order to get the latter’s help in driving the French from Italy. 
Bentinck tried his best to delay conforming with this desire for a ceasefire 
with Murat. He sent Colonel Graham, his aide-de-camp, to make contact 
with allied headquarters and to try to convince them that the army in Sicily 
remained a valuable bargaining counter in getting Murat to give up his 
throne.38 His attempt failed, for the Austrians were to cut the ground from 
under his feet by the treaty of military alliance that they signed with Murat in 
January. Bentinck complained in vain to Castlereagh, condemning the treaty 
as “impolitic, inexpedient and unnecessary.” It meant, he wrote, the aban- 
donment of a great opportunity for Britain to champion the cause of Italian 
nationalism, and the showering of undeserved rewards on a man whose life 
had been one of crime. He tried to postpone the evil day by telling the 
Austrian representatives that he had no instructions on the matter,?? but this 
would not answer for very long. On 18 January Bentinck received a dispatch 
sent to him from Lord Aberdeen and forwarded to him by Count Neipperg, 
the Austrian envoy at the court of Naples. This made clear that Aberdeen had 
agreed to Austria’s approach to Murat and was anxious that Bentinck should 
also conclude a similar convention as soon as possible. While Castlereagh 
himself on 22 January sent clear instructions to Bentinck from Bale that 
authorized him to conclude an armistice. Bentinck therefore left Palermo on 
30 January 1814, and four days later he signed the armistice with the man he 
both despised and detested. Even so, he did not sign on behalf of the king in 
Sicily, who refused fo acknowledge Murat’s claims, and Bentinck himself also 
declined to acknowledge those claims in any way.*° 
On 28 February 1814 Bentinck sailed with an army from Sicily made up of 
6,000 British troops and the remainder Italian soldiers, leaving behind to 
guard the island only 4,500 men. A further contingent of 3,000 troops under 
the command of General Macfarlane (almost all of them Sicilian) joined him 
at Leghorn in early April.41 Bentinck disembarked at Leghorn on 7 March 
1814 and a week later issued a proclamation in the name of the hereditary 

<<<PDFPAGE 124>>>
120 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
prince calling on the people of Italy to rise and assert their rights and 
liberties. He was not alone in doing this, for Murat had already done so and 
Nugent and the Austrians were to do the same.*? 
Bentinck had been ordered by Castlereagh to cooperate closely with the 
Neapolitans who were now in occupation of Rome, Emilia, and Tuscany, 
and he had been told that their “active assistance” was “indispensable” to 
allied success. But Bentinck could not tolerate Murat, nor was he willing to 
share with him the honor and glory of freeing Italy. He therefore demanded 
that Murat’s army should forthwith evacuate Tuscany, which Bentinck made 
out he required as a base for his planned assault on Genoa. And when he 
found he could not get his way, he adopted a threatening attitude, talked of 
expelling the Neapolitans, marching on Naples, and then proclaiming Ferdi- 
nand IV as its rightful sovereign. Reprimanded by Castlereagh and told he 
was under Austrian orders and must in future work beside Murat, he ac- 
cepted Austrian mediation and agreed to withdraw from Tuscany.*3 
Genoa surrendered to Bentinck’s army on 18 April 1814 and Bentinck, 
again disobeying orders, proclaimed the Republic of Genoa restored. But by 
this time Napoleon had already abdicated—his decision to do so having been 
caused not by allied successes in Italy but by other events to the north of the 
Alps. A few weeks later Bentinck again gave encouragement to the council of 
Milan in their endeavor to get accepted the concept of a free and united Italy. 
Again Castlereagh intervened, telling him that he must abstain from inter- 
ference in Italian affairs, since the future of Italy (and of course Sicily) must 
be decided in another place.44 
It is somewhat ironical to reflect that when at last the occasion arrived for 
using the army of Sicily that Britain had paid and maintained so long for a 
major campaign in Italy, the effort proved to be largely wasted. Bentinck was 
far more interested in championing the cause of Italian nationalism than in 
working closely with his country’s allies, one of whom was his béte noire, 
Murat. Reprimanded by Castlereagh for acting against the government's 
wishes (“It is not an insurrection we want in Italy . 
. 
. we want disciplined 
force under sovereigns we can trust”),45 he was sent on leave and gradually 
replaced as British minister at Palermo, returning first briefly to the island, 
which he finally left on 16 July. The military command in the Mediterranean 
he kept until April of the following year. 

<<<PDFPAGE 125>>>
10 
Speculation on the Future of Sicily and Naples 
A’ the end of the great war with Napoleon finally began to draw in sight, 
the whole question of Sicily’s future and its possible annexation by 
Britain was raised again (somewhat unexpectedly) in the shape of what was 
called “Bentinck’s Dream.” This solution to Sicily’s problems was put for- 
ward quite sincerely, in the interests of the island as much as of Britain, by the 
man who was now the dictator of Sicily. Its disclosure caused a temporary 
sensation both at the court and in Downing Street, and this reaction led 
Bentinck’s enemies to hope it might lead to his immediate recall. 
The whole question of Sicily’s future and its relationship to Britain—the 
country that for the past eight years had protected it from the grasp of 
Napoleon—was certainly not a novel one when it was raised in December 
1813. Howick and Grenville in 1806/7 had contemplated making Sicily a 
British protectorate in peacetime. Moore had certainly believed it necessary 
for the duration of the war to assume the government of Sicily, though there 
is no evidence that he ever thought it would be wise to retain it in peacetime. ! 
And the persuasive propagandist Charles Pasley in his Essay on Military Policy 
had deplored the fact that Britain had failed to annex Sicily from the begin- 
ning. Leckie, another active propagandist, the writer of articles in the Morn- 
ing Chronicle and a series of open letters and pamphlets published in 1806/7, 
as well as (almost certainly) the paper Hints for the improvement of Sicily, which 
was addressed to Lord Castlereagh, wrote in this last: 
The master of Sicily regenerated holds Egypt under his foot; Tunis is his 
tributary; and the depot of the commerce of belligerents, the ee 
Powers, cease to have ships of war. He watches the fall of the Turkis 
empire, and stands with expanded wings to aes upon Greece. He 
closes the Dardanelles with Lemnos, and forbids the Russians to enter the 
archipelago. 
This visionary talk of British dominance in the Mediterranean, in war and 
peace, had been used earlier by apologists for making Corsica a British 
possession, and the same sort of arguments were to be used a year later by 
Richard Church and General Oswald to try to persuade British ministers to 
take over the Ionian islands. It says much for the hardheadedness and 
pragmatic approach of British statesmen, notably Pitt and Castlereagh, that 
121 

<<<PDFPAGE 126>>>
122) 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
such arguments carried little weight. Pitt cared nothing for Corsica, save as a 
useful base for the fleet, and he later offered it to the Russians. Castlereagh 
saw in Sicily a military station and nothing more, serving a purely wartime 
purpose; and if he accepted an Ionian protectorate it was only to stop the 
allies from quarrelling and to help to safeguard the Turkish Empire. He was 
concerned as was William Pitt, and rightly so, with the winning of the war, 
and then with constructing a durable peace—a peace based on the hard, 
inescapable, and (to liberals) often most unpalatable facts of international 
politics, the need to retain reliable allies and the continued support of the 
other Great Powers. 
Bentinck’s dream of annexing Sicily with the willing compliance of the 
Sicilians sprang from a tour he had made of the island during November 
1813, the purpose of which had been to find out what reforms in the 
administration of justice the mass of the people really wanted, but also to 
impress on local councillors the need to elect sensible men when the next 
parliament was summoned. During the tour he had experienced the typical 
warm welcome that southern peoples, many of whom were simple peasants, 
liked to extend to the man in power, from whom they hoped to obtain 
concessions—freedom from burdensome taxes and tolls, injustices righted, 
and corruption purged.? It was just the sort of spontaneous reception that 
had warmed the heart of Sir Gilbert Elliot, when, as the newly appointed 
Viceroy, he had made his progress through Corsica. It put the idea into 
Bentinck’s head (he had never before shown signs of favoring it) that the 
people of Sicily would surely welcome being annexed to the British Empire. 
The Constitution of 1812 provided for an independent Sicily. If the kingdom 
of Sicily was to be united once again with the kingdom of Naples, it would, 
he felt sure, mean the spilling of blood. Yet Sicily, it was painfully clear, could 
not exist independently, any more than Corsica could, and, as had been 
shown by recent events, it was quite unable to govern itself. Sicily in the 
hands of Naples would remain as it had been in the past a source of 
embarrassment to the latter. Under the benevolent rule of Britain it could 
become both content and prosperous, and pose no threat to its neighbor 
Naples. The king of course would be compensated, probably in the form of 
money, and Britain would provide 10,000 troops to guarantee his possession 
of Naples.4 
All this was outlined in a letter from Bentinck, sent to the hereditary prince 
on 3 December 1813, though a few days later he wrote again to explain his 
proposal was not really serious, but a “dreamchild of his whimsical brain.”® It 
was not however seen as such by a worried and apprehensive prince, who had 
for years heard his mother declaiming against the sinister designs of the 
British. Having taken possession unilaterally of Malta and six Ionian islands, 
it now seemed that they wanted Sicily too. The suggestions that Bentinck had 
put forward seemed to his Neapolitan mind, accustomed to the indirect 

<<<PDFPAGE 127>>>
Speculation on the Future of Sicily and Naples 
123 
approach, to be only a form of kite-flying, coming as they did from Britain’s 
minister and the commander-in-chief of her army. He therefore replied in a 
guarded manner. Sicily, he wrote, was sacred property that the king his father 
was unable to renounce, and he could not think that Britain would wish to 
deprive her ally of the kind of refuge that Sicily had provided in times of 
adversity. In any case, the compensation Bentinck had mentioned seemed 
quite inadequate—thereby hinting that if more were offered, the scheme 
envisaged might yet be considered.® 
Bentinck replied that the plan he had outlined was something that was 
entirely his own—the government in London knew nothing about it. But he 
did add that Sicily must always require some maritime protection, and 
Britain being likely for many years to remain the world’s preponderant 
seapower, she was the obvious country to provide it.” 
The prince consulted Medici, who in turn took counsel with Castelcicala, 
and the two in conjunction with the king (though apparently without the 
prince’s knowledge) plotted to get rid of Bentinck by raising the matter with 
Downing Street. It seemed an excellent method by which, if Bentinck had 
acted without instructions, the minister could be disgraced and his immedi- 
ate recall procured. But both Bathurst and Castlereagh, though not un- 
naturally surprised at learning of the relevant correspondence, were not 
prepared to be used in this manner. Castelcicala, despite being assured that 
the ministers had never heard of the scheme put forward in Bentinck’s letter 
to the prince, a copy of which the ambassador had sent them, asked for 
Bentinck to be removed. It appeared, he wrote, that Bentinck’s “dream” was 
no dream but rather an “avowed intention”—not to give Sicily liberty but to 
annex it to Great Britain. And furthermore, if Bentinck was not recalled, the 
case of his master the king of Sicily must be submitted to the king of Spain. If 
the king of Sicily was to be reduced to the status of a mere pensioner, he 
preferred that he should receive his pension from the court of his brother 
rather than from Britain. Castelcicala therefore asked for a passport to be 
issued to his messenger who was to take this request to Madrid.® 
The reply he received was chilly in tone. The British government, Bathurst 
wrote, had already made clear it had been no party to an idea “thrown out” 
by Bentinck in what had been clearly a confidential letter. If Castelcicala had 
received authority to request the recall of Lord William Bentinck, he was 
asked to supply thé date on which the hereditary prince had sent such 
instructions. Castelcicala’s bluff was called and his earlier boast that he had 
given to Bentinck “a blow that will have broken his neck for good” was 
shown up for the bombast it was.? 
All this took place in the first half of March. In early February (after 
signing the armistice) Bentinck had written a long letter to Castlereagh, 
setting out his case for the “dream.” At the coming peace, so he told 
Castlereagh, Sicily would be faced with two alternatives. Either it would be 

<<<PDFPAGE 128>>>
124 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
rejoined to Naples and see its liberty crushed by the crown (“it is not easy to 
conceive the terror universally felt at the unforgiving nature of their Ma- 
jesties”), or its independence would be upheld—in which case it would fall 
the victim of a combination of self-seeking barons, the king, and a licentious 
people quite unable to govern themselves. If on the other hand Sicily were to 
be placed under British protection, the island could become “after Ireland, 
the brightest jewel in the British crown.” With Murat secure on the throne of 
Naples, Sicily would need more than ever the protection afforded by British 
troops. A garrison of 5,000 men (the figure mentioned in 1806 when a draft 
treaty had been proposed by Howick) could be kept in Sicily in peacetime, 
while Sicily could as at present provide a contingent at the disposal of Britain. 
He thought that both the king and the prince would be glad to be rid of 
Sicily, provided they were offered attractive terms, and the queen had fre- 
quently said the same. He was only sorry he had made the suggestion 
without first obtaining cabinet approval, but the moment had seemed to him 
propitious and there had been no time to refer the matter to Castlereagh and 
the ministry.1° In addition to writing, he had also dispatched his secretary 
James Graham to Chatillon to interview Castlereagh personally, and this 
James Graham did on 21 February 1814. Graham, being both “young and 
romantic” (to use the words of Dr. Rosselli), convinced himself that Castle- 
reagh sympathized with Bentinck’s “dream,” and reported him saying that if 
Murat proved treacherous and the Bourbons were to be restored to Naples, 
the British might keep Sicily; even if Murat stayed in Naples, the British 
would still need their army in Sicily after the signing of a peace. This however 
was not the impression Castlereagh retained of the interview, or perhaps did 
not wish to retain of it, once the matter was raised by Castelcicala. He entirely 
disowned the views on the subject attributed to him by James Graham. As 
Rosselli charitably comments, his mind was then occupied by greater issues, 
and he may not have given to what Graham said the close attention Graham 
thought he had.!! 
It was not until 3 April that Castlereagh finally wrote to Bentinck, sending 
him a mild reprimand. Dissociating the ministry entirely from the proposals 
of the “dream,” Castlereagh emphasized the danger of making such un- 
authorized speculations, especially when one had to deal with “characters so 
suspicious and intriguing.”!? The last word upon the matter had really rested 
with Liverpool, the prime minister, who had written to Castlereagh in 
March: 
Because Bentinck finds his constitution and the Sicilian court never likely 
to work together, he sees no way out save the annexing of Sicily. If there 
were no other objections, it would be very difficult to manage the island 
with such a constitution, but you know other Powers are more jealous of 
our obtaining Bovis in the Mediterranean than in any other quarter. 
Circumstances 
have made it necessary for them to accept our retaining 

<<<PDFPAGE 129>>>
Speculation on the Future of Sicily and Naples 
125 
Malta, but the idea of our possessing Sicily by an act which in its most 
favourable hight must be considered douce violence could not fail greatly to 
revolt them. 14 
It was just such jealousy that the British had encountered twenty years 
previously in relation to Corsica and that was, in regard to the Ionian islands, 
to concede them a protectorate only, instead of the sovereignty that Bathurst 
had wanted. 
Meanwhile events in Sicily were moving toward an unhappy conclusion so 
far as Bentinck and his friends were concerned. When he had toured the 
island in December, he had told local councillors that it was their duty to 
elect to parliament honest and enlightened representatives, and when the 
elections were held in June 1814, a lower House had been elected that was 
predominantly “constitutional”’—a result obtained, almost certainly, by in- 
timidation and corruption. But although the ministry now enjoyed the 
support of one House, it lost that of the other, because the nobles now 
turned to the king rather than submit to more losses of privilege. A political 
impasse had been reached, and although Bentinck counselled a coalition, the 
nobles made it clear that they would not accept one, the Belmontists being 
quite determined not to work together with the Castelnuovists.!* Finally the 
hereditary prince, at the request of Belmonte and others, asked the king to 
resume his powers as the only way of resolving the crisis. Belmonte’s remark- 
able volte face in thus turning to his enemy the king was caused by his 
thinking he could only benefit personally from such a move. He thought the 
king would be duly flattered but did not believe he would ever accept or that 
the British would ever leave—in both of which respects he proved wrong. 
Bentinck refused to allow his name to be used by the prince in approaching 
the king and he refused to sign a written memorandum requesting the king 
to take over the reins, since he doubted the king’s sincerity. But the king 
eventually expressed publicly his determination to forget the past, to reunite 
the political parties, and to complete the Sicilian constitution, on condition 
that he be released from the pledge he had given to Bentinck in March 1813 
not to resume his powers again without the permission of the British minis- 
ter. Bentinck released him from this bond, as there seemed to be little 
alternative, and the king therefore reassumed power on 5 July 1814."° 
The king’s sincerity was rightly in doubt. His attitude was currently being 
influenced by a wish to please the allied sovereigns, in whose hands it lay, at 
the Congress of Vienna, to return him to his kingdom in Naples. Both the 
Czar and the Austrian emperor, through the channel of the Duke of Orleans, 
had impressed on Ferdinand the need to adopt a more liberal attitude toward 
his subjects, if his claims were to be considered favorably. The restored 
Bourbon king of France, Louis XVIII, who had also found it necessary to 
accept a constitution in order to recover his throne, strongly advised him to 
do the same. There was however an additional reason for Ferdinand to show 

<<<PDFPAGE 130>>>
126 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
a liberal face, and that was the fear of what Murat might do, the latter having 
proclaimed his intention of granting a constitution to Naples.1° 
British public opinion favored the return of Ferdinand to Naples, as 
Castlereagh told the Austrian ambassador, if only because he had lost his 
kingdom through having allied himself with Britain; it was a matter of 
national honor and dignity to see him restored. Metternich shared the view 
of Castlereagh about putting Ferdinand back on his throne. He told the 
Sicilian minister in Vienna in confidence that he did not intend that Murat 
should keep the throne of Naples and that Ferdinand must have his kingdom 
back, with Sicily, but not with a constitution. The Austrian emperor's 
invitation to Ferdinand to behave more liberally in future clearly therefore 
did not extend to his governing his kingdom with a constitution.!7 
Bentinck’s reaction to Ferdinand’s new posture may at first appear puz- 
zling. Before leaving Sicily for good on 18 July 1814 (his position there as 
minister having been assumed by William A’Court), he penned a dispatch to 
Castlereagh in which he seemed to show satisfaction at the way events had 
turned out recently—surely the exact opposite of what he for so long had 
hoped and worked for, and of sentiments expressed in his journal as recently 
as a week before (“I never could think it desirable that the king should 
conduct the government” and “How could one suppose that a man who had 
governed ill for fifty years could all of a sudden change?”).!8 But now, 
writing on 5 July, he congratulated Castlereagh on what he described as a 
happy ending to a long and embarrassing relationship between two allied 
governments, and he dwelt on the king’s “sincere desire” to support the 
Sicilian constitution and to be reconciled to his political foes.!9 Yet clearly 
Bentinck did not believe this; he was either seeking to deceive Castlereagh or 
was putting the best face he could on the matter. He had tried in vain to get 
the barons to work together for a compromise and now was disgusted at the 
outcome. He told his friends he would be glad to go away—“I owed nothing 
to the country which I despised.”2° As A’Court (whom Bentinck thought 
“clever” and “honorable,” though he held very different political views) 
wrote privately to William Hamilton, “Having brought the comedy to the 
fifth act, he [Bentinck] seems to be by no means sorry to get away before the 
dénouement actually takes place.” He [A’Court] was grateful that the king had 
resumed power before he took over and that “it was Lord William and not I 
who did it, otherwise I should have been reproached.”?1! 
Not surprisingly, the king now chose to employ as his ministers men who 
had been no friends to the party Bentinck had supported, indeed the very 
ones he had got rid of in the purge of March 1813. The British government 
had given assurances that there was to be no victimization, but not feeling at 
all confident about that promise being fulfilled, the duke of Orleans and 
Prince Belmonte both decided to seek safety in exile—in Paris, where they 
hoped, though vainly, to persuade Castlereagh to adopt a more liberal stance 

<<<PDFPAGE 131>>>
Speculation on the Future of Sicily and Naples 
127 
toward Sicily.?* Yet the king was still unable to get a new parliament to vote 
him taxes (elected in August, its composition was much more democratic 
than the previous one), so for twelve months he had to subsist on taxes raised 
unconstitutionally and what remained of the British subsidy. Bentinck, when 
he had returned from Italy, had threatened to stop the subsidy altogether 
until the sums he had lent the prince were repaid, but realizing the embarrass- 
ment this would cause, decided the payments might continue if parliament 
made some reasonable arrangement.?? It was here that Castlereagh inter- 
vened. According to the treaty of alliance, the subsidy was supposed to stop 
with the war, but withholding it for even six months would not liquidate the 
debts owed to Britain. Such was the state of the country’s finances that the 
king might have to dismiss his army, and if this happened the bulk of the 
troops would almost certainly desert to Murat. A’Court was accordingly 
instructed that the British government would continue to pay one half of the 
subsidy agreed upon, until the Congress of Vienna ended or 1 March next, 
whichever was soonest.?4 
This windfall of the half subsidy put new confidence into the ministry in 
their confrontation with parliament, but it disheartened the supporters of the 
constitution who regarded its payment as tacit approval of the king’s uncon- 
stitutional measures. Even so, the Sicilian government was not preserved 
from bankruptcy. By the middle of March 1815 (the Congress of Vienna 
being still in session), the Sicilian army was about to dissolve. Without pay 
for the past six months, its officers were reduced to begging and even to 
robbery by night. The Sicilian government had tried for too long to keep up 
a style it could not afford, maintaining not only an expensive court, expensive 
embassies abroad, a host of civil servants, not to mention pensioners, but also 
an army far larger than it needed.?5 It had remained solvent only because 
Bentinck had succeeded in slimming the army and in lending the government 
money. Now that the subsidy was cut by half and further loans were no 
longer forthcoming (and were indeed due to be repaid), the harshness of 
fiscal reality must be faced. 
All this time the British garrison still remained in Sicily. Orders had been 
issued in August for its immediate withdrawal, but the king had asked for it 
to stay. He was careful to avoid giving any hint that he needed it for internal 
security, but made out that it might be required for a possible future 
offensive against Murat. Castlereagh agreed to this request because the 
Congress was still deliberating and no decision had yet been reached about 
the future of Naples or Murat; but he used the occasion to point out to the 
king how unfounded all past suspicions had been of the British wanting to 
take over Sicily.?° 
The escape of Napoleon from Elba in February and the beginning of the 
Hundred Days provided Sicily with a temporary respite in weathering its 
financial crisis. It also relieved Metternich of the problem posed by Murat’s 

<<<PDFPAGE 132>>>
128 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
claims to be recognized on the throne of Naples. On 15 March Murat once 
again took up arms against the Austrians, and a month later Bentinck 
declared that the armistice with Murat was at an end. On 17 May the king, 
having failed to get the parliament to grant him money to take his army 
across to Naples, finally dissolved it and named a commission for the task of 
reforming the constitution. Britain now stepped in to fill the gap, Castlereagh 
agreeing to renew the subsidy, provided it was used exclusively for offensive 
operations in Italy and that command of the expedition was entrusted to a 
British officer.?7 
Accordingly, a mixed force (half of it Sicilian) set sail from Sicily in the 
middle of May under the command of General Macfarlane. When it arrived 
in the Bay of Naples it found its presence largely superfluous, as an Austrian 
army occupied the town and the British fleet lay anchored in the bay.?® A 
fortnight later the king himself arrived back in his capital, having left behind 
the hereditary prince to act as lieutenant general of the island. The death of 
the queen the previous September had made his return considerably easier, 
since memories of her vindictiveness in 1799 were still very much alive. 
Needless to say, the king had not sought permission from parliament to leave 
Sicily, as by the terms of the constitution he was in strictness bound to do. 
The last chapter of Britain’s occupation and protection of Sicily was about to 
begin. 

<<<PDFPAGE 133>>>
11 
The Dénouement 
Wwe the conclusion of the Hundred Days, the award by the Congress of 
Vienna of the kingdom of Naples to king Ferdinand, and the igno- 
minious end of Murat, there was no longer any need for the presence of 
British troops in Sicily, and the last contingent left its shores on 9 October 
1815. But there still remained the vexing question of how Britain was to 
extract herself with honor from Sicily’s internal situation. She had for a 
period of almost three years committed herself to the support of the constitu- 
tional party in Sicily (it had come to be called “the British party”) and had 
shouldered a moral obligation that could not lightly be cast off. Indeed any 
attempt to do so would stain Britain’s name internationally, as well as cause 
an uproar at Westminster. Yet the king, who was now restored to Naples, was 
notoriously opposed to the constitution, had found it quite unworkable, and 
since June 1815 had tied his hands by a new treaty with Austria, a treaty that 
enabled him at last to be free of his long dependence on Britain. In return for 
a guarantee by Austria of military support for his dominions, he had pledged 
himself politically not to tolerate any institutions that were at variance with 
those permitted in Austria’s Italian possessions. In other words, no responsi- 
ble government. Now Austria was an ally of Britain; her support in uphold- 
ing the Peace of Vienna was paramount in Castlereagh’s eyes, and no scruples 
about the constitution could be allowed to stand in the way of establishing 
his principal goal, an enduring international settlement. 
During the war, Castlereagh had been willing to go along with Bentinck’s 
policy and permit him to work his political experiment. As he frankly 
admitted in May 1814, “I found the whole line of politics with respect of 
Sicily so completely chalked out that nothing was left for me to do but to let 
things take their cburse and to remain a spectator of the experiment which 
was to be made and to give it a fair trial.”! But once the end of the war was in 
sight, he decided that Bentinck must be replaced in his civilian capacity by the 
more conservative William A’Court, then envoy extraordinary to the Barbary 
States and a man known to have little sympathy with popular assemblies in 
foreign countries. A’Court had presented for Castlereagh’s eyes a memoran- 
dum of his views on the Sicilian situation based on his reading of the 
correspondence, and this had clearly impressed the foreign secretary. He was, 
A’Court wrote, quite convinced that, despite Bentinck’s hopes for a new 
129

The Dénouement 
131 
parliament due to be summoned in the spring, matters could not go on as 
they were. “It is possible that the presence of his Lordship with the sword in 
one hand and the purse in the other, may influence and control the factious 
spirit of the assembly”; but once the British army was removed from Sicily, 
either there would be a revolution carried out by the democrats, or the nobles 
and those who supported the king would combine with the mob and the 
royal troops to put the king back in his old position and do away with the 
constitution. Bentinck himself, A’Court pointed out, had in a private letter to 
Liverpool admitted that only British interference could save Sicily from 
internal convulsion. Yet the jealousy of Britain’s allies would never permit her 
army to stay there, while intervention privately by the British minister in 
Palermo would merely compete with private intrigues by the ministers of 
other powers, and France for instance could scarcely desire a better field for 
activity than “that provided by an ignorant, profligate and . 
. 
. venal House 
of Parliament.” A’Court had already made up his mind that a free constitu- 
tion was not something in any way suited to the people of Sicily.” 
A month later he was duly appointed to act as deputy for Bentinck as 
British minister at Palermo, during the latter’s absence in Italy, in charge of 
military operations. And in his instructions from Castlereagh he was told that 
his most immediate task was to get the constitution modified. The original 
error, as Castlereagh saw it, had been making the king dependent on parlia- 
ment in a country where the spirit of jealousy and faction was unfortunately 
all too prevalent. What A’Court had to attempt to do was to ensure that only 
men of property were represented in parliament, and that the crown was no 
longer dependent on parliament for its normal expenses.? 
In a revealing interview Castlereagh gave to A’Court in May before the 
latter left for Sicily, Castlereagh told him, in reply to his question about what 
action he should take should the king resume power and the queen return, 
that the time for direct interference was past and that there could now be no 
warrant for behaving as Britain had done in the course of the war. As to 
Sicily’s constitution, Castlereagh disclaimed all responsibility for it. Its suc- 
cess had been as great as might have been expected, but he thought nothing 
could save it now. “God knows,” he went on, “it was never any principle of 
mine that we should meddle with the constitutions of other nations,” but 
even had he wanted to, he would have been completely cured of any inclina- 
tion to do so by what he had seen to happen in Sicily. He thought it would 
meet the same fate as the constitutions then forming in Europe, and the best 
thing that could happen was for Sicily’s government to revert to its original 
form.* 
A’Court’s appointment had been canvassed for months by Castelcicala and 
the king, some time before the affair of the “dream.” They had even tried to 
get A’Court accredited solely to the king, until informed by Castlereagh that 
the hereditary prince was the head of the state.° But there was no question 

<<<PDFPAGE 136>>>
eZ 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
but that A’Court would be far more acceptable to the king and prince than 
the constitutional dictator Bentinck. 
A’Court did not take very long after arriving in Sicily to reaffirm his earlier 
view that the Constitution of 1812 had been a profound mistake for the 
island. The Sicilian people, he told Castlereagh, were quite unfitted to be 
entrusted with any sort of political power, and a strong monarchical govern- 
ment was the only one that was suitable for them. This view, in fact, is not all 
that different from the one Bentinck had been forced to adopt in his mo- 
ments of greatest disillusion.© “If I had known,” the latter had written, “as 
much of the general character of the people as I did at present, I do not think 
I should ever have undertaken the business. I doubted whether these people 
were made for freedom.”” All would have been well, A’Court thought, if 
those who had drafted the constitution had contented themselves with lim- 
ited aims—the improving of the administration, the abolition of feudal 
rights, establishing equality before the law, and the introduction of fairer 
taxation. But the great mistake, to his mind, had been to create deliberative 
assemblies endowed with legislative power; for this meant Britain had been 
forced to take sides and now found it difficult suddenly to withdraw. He felt 
it acutely embarrassing to have, in the name of British honor as well as of 
sheer humanity, to give support to individuals with whose principles he so 
disagreed. He painted a very gloomy picture of a bankrupt island whose old 
institutions had now become discredited, and whose new ones were not yet 
functioning. The best friends Britain possessed in Sicily were making haste to 
abandon the island, and they were not willing to return unless it became 
controlled by Britain. Thus it was that the British cabinet would have to 
make a crucial decision, either to support what Britain had created or to 
renounce British influence there altogether. It was extremely unfortunate, he 
thought, that Britain had ever interfered over the matter of a constitution: 
“We have done either too much or too little. 
. 
. 
. we satisfied ourselves with 
the shadow, when we should have seized the substance,” and by this he meant 
forcing the king to abdicate (something that Bentinck had always wanted). 
For the king was now the principal difficulty, determined to ditch the 
constitution yet so untrustworthy and vindictive that he would, if permitted 
a free hand, soon establish a tyranny. Were it not for him, it would be quite 
easy for Britain to withdraw her support and “let the constitution sink into a 
form more adapted to the manners, vices and prejudices of this ignorant and 
profligate people.” The only course that now seemed open was to get the 
king to agree to accept a constitution of some sort, before he eventually 
returned to Naples.? 
But the whole affair of the constitution took on a completely new complex- 
ion with the terms of the treaty the king signed in June 1815 with Austria. 
Metternich proposed to A’Court that Britain should join with Austria to 
disown the Sicilian constitution, as they had already jointly disowned the 

<<<PDFPAGE 137>>>
The Dénouement 
133 
Republic of Genoa, revived by Bentinck. This however was considered 
impracticable, since the two cases were quite unlike. Castlereagh made clear 
to A’Court, writing in September 1816, that any concert between Austria 
and Britain would embarrass rather than help the king, might be wrongly 
construed in Europe, and would moreover make the two countries responsi- 
ble for whatever was done. While it was right to advise the king, A’Court 
must not commit the British government. The best course would be to let the 
king carry out his projected scheme (or subterfuge) for modifying the consti- 
tution, while letting him know, privately, that Britain would tolerate neither 
the extinction of any of Sicily’s ancient rights nor the persecution of any of 
those who had lent their support to British authority.!° This course was 
accordingly adopted, and A’Court wrote triumphantly on 5 November that 
the new, modified constitution on which his private advice had been taken 
(without committing his government) would ensure that nothing was done 
in Sicily that could be considered reactionary. He therefore congratulated 
Castlereagh on being now totally released “from all responsibility arising 
from the past.”!! The king was delighted and had awarded A’Court the 
highest decoration he had the power to confer (the Grand Cross of the Order 
of St Ferdinand), even though British protocol forbade A’Court from accept- 
ing it.!? But from the Sicilian constitutionalists the latter earned the nick- 
name of “William the Bad,” in contradistinction to Lord William Bentinck, 
who of course was referred to as “William the Good.”!$ 
On 8 December 1816 the king declared the kingdom of Naples and the 
kingdom of Sicily to be one. The new constitution he promulgated effectively 
robbed the Sicilian parliament of any power to legislate or prevent the king 
from raising revenue, save for extraordinary purposes. Though the privileges 
granted by the crown were confirmed, freedom of the press was now abol- 
ished and a measure of conscription introduced. 
!* There was nothing left of 
the constitution over whose birth Bentinck had presided. 
The policy in regard to Sicily that he had pursued during these two years 
Castlereagh defended vigorously in the House of Commons in 1821, when 
rejecting a motion put forward by Bentinck for a fresh intervention in Sicilian 
affairs. Castlereagh said that although the government felt strong affection 
and esteem for Sicily, it was not on that account or to secure its people’s 
happiness that British troops had been stationed there—in fact it had been a 
military occupation. Britain’s guarantee had not been to form a constitution, 
but to protect the dominions of its king. True, it had been necessary to 
intervene to impress on the mind of the Sicilian government the need for a 
modern constitution. Moreover if this had not been done, the government of 
the island could not have’ continued and the place would have been quite 
unfitted for its use as a British military base. It was never felt that Lord 
William Bentinck was entering into any arrangements with respect to the 
Sicilian constitution. 
!® 

<<<PDFPAGE 138>>>
134 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
Yet Bentinck clearly had believed that he was entering into an arrange- 
ment. How far then was he justified in suggesting that Britain’s name and 
honor had been smirched by Castlereagh’s realpolitik? As Professor Mack 
Smith has pointed out, the king, though he was later blamed for destroying a 
liberal experiment, had been supported in so doing by a large proportion of 
the people of Sicily and had only dissolved the Sicilian parliament for the last 
time in 1815, after the failure of four parliaments to vote the necessary 
taxes.1© Charles Macfarlane, who was in Sicily only six months after the 
British troops left, heard many complaints of pecuniary loss now that the 
freespending forces had gone, but heard no regrets for a constitution that 
people described as a kind of “madness.”!7 Earlier the king had told Ben- 
tinck, “The Sicilians are not Englishmen, the habits of this country are not 
the habits of yours. 
. 
. 
. You fancy that Sicilians can govern themselves like 
Englishmen. They cannot do it. Give them this constitution and you yourself 
will soon have cause to repent it.”!8 Ferdinand’s words had proved pro- 
phetic. 
Since, then, the maintenance of the constitution (the creation of an en- 
lightened few) rested quiet clearly on British bayonets, to describe the 
withdrawal of such questionable support as being in some way “dis- 
honorable” seems to stretch the meaning of the word too far. Nor could any 
such withdrawal be described as unreasonable. As Charles Macfarlane em- 
phasized, writing a generation after the event, “Fond as they are of liberty, 
and prone to believe . 
. 
. that constitutions like our own are a panacea and fit 
for all countries and all peoples, the English would not have relished paying, 
for an indefinite number of years, an army of 10,000 men for the sole 
purpose of protecting a political experiment in Sicily.”!9 And an experiment, 
he could have added had he not made the point already, to which most 
Sicilians had proved indifferent. In any case, to keep troops on the island for 
that avowed purpose was bound to incur not only the anger of Austria but 
the risk of an open breach with that country.2° No considerations of “honor” 
could have warranted taking such a risk. 
Certainly it would have been dishonorable to permit the king to victimize 
those who had loyally supported the British, but Ferdinand here remained 
true to his pledge where higher officers were concerned. Settimo, who had 
been the minister for war, was even offered membership of the chancellery 
responsible for Sicilian affairs, though perhaps understandably he declined it. 
Belmonte had died earlier in Paris. Castelnuovo and other nobles associated 
with the 1812 Constitution, chose to retire into private life, a sort of 
immigration intérieure, in which they were allowed to remain undisturbed. 
Nor was it any longer true that native Sicilians were kept out of office in favor 
of Neapolitans. Indeed the latter complained of being swamped by a host of 
job-hungry Sicilians who had followed the king when he returned from 
Palermo.*! Giovanni Aceto in his history refers to the number of individuals 

<<<PDFPAGE 139>>>
The Dénouement 
135 
(none of whom he identifies by name), magistrates, and other officials who 
through their political affiliations were deprived of their posts after 1815, 
denied promotion, or persecuted.?? It could unfortunately well have been so, 
more especially in a country like Sicily where mutual jealousies could be so 
intense, but it would have been impracticable, indeed impossible, for the 
British government to have intervened in any such cases unless its official 
attention had been drawn to some specific cause of complaint. 
Castlereagh had acquiesced in the constitution so long as it served its 
immediate purpose—keeping the island of Sicily quiet, so as to let the allied 
troops there be made available for service elsewhere. But it could have no 
relevance in peacetime to the foreign policy of Lord Liverpool’s government. 
Yet even in wartime it had not been the entire success various ministries had 
hoped for. As has been shown in earlier chapters, Bentinck was twice com- 
pelled to recall a proportion of his army from Spain in order to cope with 
internal unrest, was forced to delay his own departure there, and once in 
Spain had had to return because of Sicily’s turbulence. 
If the question finally has to be asked whether, viewing the war as a whole, 
the military and financial investment Britain made in Sicily had been worth- 
while, the answer must be a qualified yes; though undoubtedly the highest 
hopes entertained for it were never fulfilled. As a forward base from which to 
launch effective attacks on the enemy, whether in Egypt, the Dardanelles, 
Italy, the Adriatic, or Spain, there were few successes of which it could boast, 
the dramatic anticlimax of Maida being the one that deserves to be best 
remembered. At times there had been maintained in the island upward of 
15,000 troops, yet never enough to intervene effectively or for very long in a 
continental enterprise that in any case if it were to succeed needed the help of 
Austria or Russia. In Spain there were some results to show, but internal 
troubles in Sicily and Bentinck’s preoccupation with Italy had prevented their 
being more convincing, just as the danger from the court and its followers 
had earlier prevented more than a portion of the troops available from being 
used in Italy or being dispatched to the Dardanelles. At all times there had 
remained present the threat of invasion by the French, even if after 1810 that 
threat was never a very real one. And if the British cabinet did not take that 
threat very seriously after Murat’s halfhearted, aborted thrust, the court and 
the generals and admirals did, and the king insisted that the minimum force 
of 10,000 effective British troops, guaranteed by treaty, be maintained on the 
island as long as the war with Napoleon lasted. 
Yet looked at purely defensively, the occupation had been well worth the 
trouble. Without it, Malta would have been at risk—indeed Sir Alexander 
Ball once wrote to Castlereagh of “the disaster which must necessarily accrue 
to the inhabitants of Malta, should the French possess themselves of Si- 
cily.”23 And with Malta at risk, so also would be British command of the 
Mediterranean as well as Britain’s commercial interests in the Levant and 

<<<PDFPAGE 140>>>
136 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
North Africa. The British navy would not only have lost its harbors at 
Augusta and Syracuse, but also the fresh food and water that the island was 
able to provide. Without Sicily, its arduous role in the Mediterranean would 
have been hard to fill. Sicily was never a firm base, but all the same it was one 
Britain needed to sustain her effort in the war with Napoleon. 
Of the legacy of British rule in Sicily, Louis Simond, a Frenchman, wrote 
in 1828: 
The English, it must be acknowledged, have left here no honourable 
monument of a 
power paramount to sovereignty . 
. 
. the roads, the pris- 
ons, the ECan the corrupt and barbarous administration of justice, 
remained in just as wretched a state as before they interfered. It is true they 
saved the island from French dominion; from the violence and plunder 
which attended it at De and in Calabria; they kept strict discipline; 
they paid honestly and liberally for all they had; but they did not mix 
cordially with the people; they continued meddling, teaching, ruling, with 
a ee hand and a supercilious pride, till all classes were tired out; and yet 
all classes regret them, simply because they saved them from Naples for a 
while.?4 
Apart from the fact that the British did do something to improve the 
appalling roads in Sicily, certainly in the northeast tip of the island, this rather 
superficial judgment really misses the important point. As has been stressed 
so often in this book, the ministry in London was extremely loath to interfere 
in Sicily’s internal affairs, and when eventually it was constrained to do so, its 
representative, Lord Wiliam Bentinck, spent his time trying to get the 
Sicilians to effect the needed reforms themselves. His lack of success in 
persuading them to work a system of parliamentary government induced 
them in the end to turn back to their king, who personified Neapolitan 
domination. 

<<<PDFPAGE 141>>>
Appendix A 
The Economic Implications of the British 
Occupation of Sicily 
less presence of British troops in Sicily together with the payment of a 
British subsidy brought to the island an influx of capital and a vast 
increase of its purchasing power. The demand of the armed forces for meat 
meant that the price of cattle almost doubled, as did that of wool and corn. 
The wages of agricultural laborers did not keep pace with this inflation, and it 
was therefore the merchants and farmers and the landowners with their 
increased rents who benefited from this situation. The attempt by the Sicilian 
government to introduce a free trade in corn led in the summer of 1813 to 
such a huge increase in its price that serious riots broke out in Palermo, and 
not long after Lord William Bentinck was forced to reimpose price controls. 
When however the war ended and British troops were finally withdrawn, 
there was inevitably a collapse of prices and an end to the period of pros- 
perity for those classes who had benefited by the British presence. ! 
The interdiction of trade to Europe caused a wartime flowering of local 
industry—silk manufacture in Palermo, the manufacture of wool, hemp, and 
linen in new factories at Girgenti, and of linen at Caltanisetta. Hats, fur- 
niture, even harpsichords to rival the best products of France and Spain (if 
one witness is to be believed) were all being produced in Sicily during the 
wartime period, while new sulphur and asphalt mines were being successfully 
exploited.” 
The contraband trade with the continent was very largely in the hands of 
the British, for whom Sicily became a vast entrepét for British manufactures 
and for colonial goods. Britain’s minister at Palermo was authorized by the 
British government to issue licenses for this trade to any vessels other than 
French, though in regard to the trade to Calabria there were difficulties with 
British cruisers who tended to stop any vessels crossing, purely on grounds of 
security; and on one occasion in 1808 the merchants of Leeds complained to 
the ministry that legitimate trade was being hampered through orders issued 
by the Admiralty. 
When during the outbreak of plague in Malta in the summer and autumn 
of 1813 the Sicilians imposed a rigorous quarantine, British merchants in 
Sicily found that their trade was badly hit. In consequence, Bentinck felt 
compelled to authorize a full-scale trade between Sicily and the Neapolitan 
137 

<<<PDFPAGE 142>>>
138 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
ports. Permission was given at the end of December for the export to Naples 
of Sicilian produce as well as British and colonial produce and of course 
British manufactures, while imports were no longer confined to iron, flour, 
and rice as hitherto, but could now include all the produce of Naples, only 
excluding French manufactures.* 
By the treaty of alliance of 1808 the ports of Sicily were thrown open to 
the merchant ships of British subjects, and merchants were not required to 
pay duties on food or military stores imported for the use of British troops. 
Nevertheless some of these duties did continue to be levied, on the pretext 
that the royal right to collect them had been farmed out to private bodies. 
These commercial privileges granted the British were deeply resented by 
Sicilians, and once the war ended in 1814, although British troops still 
remained on the island until October 1815, there were complaints from 
British merchants that their privileges were being ignored and that delays 
continued to be imposed on the import of British goods. As A’Court wrote 
indignantly to one official: “It would seem that nothing could be more 
acceptable at the present moment than anything which may wear the ap- 
pearance of hostility to the British interests.”> 
There were many British merchants in Sicily, most of them living in 
Palermo and Messina, though the firms of Woodhouse and Ingham were 
most closely linked with the port of Marsala where they virtually controlled 
the trade in wine and had kept Nelson’s fleet supplied with it.© How many 
English merchants there were, how much capital they had, in what sectors of 
trade they operated, and what was the extent of their profits are questions 
about which it is not yet possible to be precise. The petition of the merchants 
resident in Sicily made to the Council of Trade and Plantations on 27 May 
1811 was signed by twenty-eight merchant firms or individuals trading on 
their own.” These British merchants had protested against the tax on mone- 
tary transactions imposed by the king in 1811, though this was removed after 
Bentinck’s arrival. They also protested that there existed no Anglo-Sicilian 
commercial treaty. It will be recalled that in 1806 the Ministry of All the 
Talents initially suggested that a clause be added to the subsidy treaty then 
being negotiated by which British merchants in Sicily should be given the 
position of most-favored nationals, but that fierce Sicilian opposition had 
caused the suggestion to be withdrawn. Two years later, Mellish impressed 
on Canning the need for some such commercial agreement, since British 
merchants in Sicily were in the position of those least favored, their goods 
being required to pay 36 percent more than the goods of other nations.8 
Canning apparently took no action, because three years later Wellesley at the 
Foreign Office raised the matter again with the Board of Trade. Bathurst, 
then in charge of that department, replied that there were difficulties. Britain 
could not ask for commercial advantages simply because she was paying a 
subsidy (one she was bound by treaty to do). Sicily would seek reciprocity 

<<<PDFPAGE 143>>>
Appendix A 
139 
and that meant reducing the duty on wine—not just Sicilian wine, but the 
wines of all wine-producing countries (other, of course, than the enemy 
France)—and this the Treasury could not afford.? Only a month later, 
however, the Board of Trade did produce a projet for an Anglo-Sicilian 
commercial treaty that was designed to be included in the wider treaty of 
1812.!° This however proved unnecessary since by the end of 1811 
the main 
merchants’ grievances had been removed. New instructions had been issued 
by the court in regard to the seizure of British ships and to improving the 
conduct of the prize court; steps had been taken to adjust the assessment of 
the value of British goods entering the country to keep pace with the 
lowering of prices, and an assurance had been given to London that in future 
British goods might be freely transshipped without the payment of any 
duty.!! There is no doubt that British manufactures, despite the court’s 
grudging attitude, enjoyed a wartime boom in Sicily, and one witness who 
was resident in Palermo through 1811 and 1812 writes of English cloth 
being universally worn, and every kind of household necessity being not only 
“on the English model” but “generally procured in England.”!? And certainly 
the British community could not complain of underrepresentation where 
consular services were concerned, since by 1812 there were functioning in 
Sicily no less than thirty vice-consuls and consuls.1* Not however until 1816 
was a trade treaty finally signed between Britain and the Two Sicilies. This 
gave the subjects of both countries the status of most-favored nationals and 
remitted 10 percent of the duty paid on British imported goods in return for 
Britain abandoning the right of exemption from customs search. 
14 
Sicilian merchants for their part benefited from British naval protection 
against French privateers and Barbary pirates. When the British navy arrived 
off Sicily at the end of the eighteenth century, a constant state of war 
obtained between the island and the Barbary states, and Sicilian trade was 
either confined to vessels plying a coastal traffic or to those of some other 
country whose flag the Barbary states respected. It was the British who, 
through the good offices of Admiral Fremantle in 1812 (acting on behalf of 
Lord William Beninck), concluded a treaty with the bey of Tunis. Four 
hundred-odd Sicilian subjects being held in slavery were cheaply ransomed, a 
truce was made between Sicily and Tunis to last as long as Britain was Sicily’s 
ally and permission was given for boats from Sicily to fish coral along the 
North African coast.!5 The economic benefits accruing from nearly ten years 
of close relations between Britain and Sicily were not the perquisite of one 
side only. And one major contribution to the Sicilian economy that the 
British occupation of Sicily made was to the island’s merchant marine. Before 
1806 Sicilian exports were carried to Europe in the foreign craft of France, 
Genoa, and Ragusa. The Sicilian merchant marine consisted largely of a few 
small vessels, principally engaged in a coastal trade, creeping from port to 
port in the kingdom, terrified of Barbary pirates but also ignorant of naviga- 

<<<PDFPAGE 144>>>
140 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
tion. The masters of Sicilian craft seldom ventured out to sea or went beyond 
the Straits of Gibraltar. But during the British occupation, the ship repairs to 
the British squadron were carried out by Sicilian shipwrights and as a result 
there was created a considerable body of these skilled workmen. After the 
occupation ended, they began to build much larger vessels for the trade 
between Sicily and Naples than had been in use hitherto, and so laid the 
foundation for what became a very flourishing merchant fleet.!© 

<<<PDFPAGE 145>>>
Appendix B 
Attempts Made from Sicily to Rescue 
Pope Pius VII 
Nee February 1808, when French troops were quartered in Rome be- 
cause the Pope had refused to join an offensive alliance against Great 
Britain, Pius VII was a virtual prisoner there—forced to dismiss his secre- 
taries and to dissolve his personal guard. It was in these circumstances that 
Cardinal Gabrielli, pro-papal secretary of state until he was expelled by the 
French, concocted a plot with a Scottish prelate to get the British to rescue 
the pope and convey him safely to Sicily.! The Scottish prelate was Cardinal 
Erskine, the same cardinal who thirteen years earlier had endeavored to get 
the British government to recognize the papal court and to resume diplo- 
matic relations with it in return for the then pope, Pius VI, accepting the 
Anglo-Corsican kingdom and being willing to sign a concordat with it. This 
time Erskine was more successful in obtaining a hearing at Westminster, and 
the government there sent instructions to Drummond, their minister at the 
court at Palermo, and to Admiral Collingwood, then commanding the Brit- 
ish fleet in the Mediterranean, to set on foot a rescue operation.” 
In mid-July 1808 Collingwood was ordered by the Admiralty to station a 
frigate off the Roman coast in order to receive on board the pope and any 
cardinals who might wish to accompany him, after first getting in touch with 
Drummond to coordinate plans for the operation.* Meanwhile Drummond 
had heard from Canning that Collingwood had been so instructed. But 
Drummond had already discovered that the queen, entirely without his 
knowledge or indeed that of the king and Circello, had been involved in a 
plot of her own to rescue the pope and bring him to Sicily—perhaps at the 
prompting of that strange character the Irish-born painter Rogert Fagan. 
The latter had lived for a long time in Rome, where he had acted as the 
British agent. He knew the pope personally and had only recently been 
expelled from Rome on the express order of Napoleon. On arrival in Palermo 
he quickly succeeded in becoming a confidant of the queen. In June 1809 he 
was to become the British consul-general in Sicily. The queen had dispatched 
a Sicilian frigate with Fagan on board to the Roman coast with an invitation 
to the pope to come and seek refuge in Palermo. Needless to say, the 
operation, like everything else the queen undertook, had not been thought 
out sufficiently well. Fagan was told the pope would not leave unless he 
141 

<<<PDFPAGE 146>>>
142 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
received an invitation, written out in the king’s own hand that, it was said, 
the pope would recognize. After three weeks Fagan returned, only to find 
himself out of favor both with the queen for having failed and with Drum- 
mond, who was extremely incensed that Fagan had not consulted him. And 
what was particularly galling was that surprise had now been lost. All Italy, in 
Drummond’s view, must now know of the attempt, and this had made it 
doubly difficult to make another with hope of success.# 
Collingwood had ordered the frigate Alceste under the command of Cap- 
tain Maxwell to undertake the rescue task, but he wanted Drummond to find 
for him at least two reliable men to act as agents in contacting the pope. 
Drummond decided that no Sicilian would ever agree to accept such a role 
unless he knew that the king was behind him, and so he decided he must 
confide in the king and in Circello, whom he swore to secrecy, for on no 
account was the queen to be told. It was they who eventually procured four 
Catholic priests to sail on the frigate. Drummond writes of only three—a 
Jesuit and two monks or friars—while Maxwell, who must here be relied on, 
mentions four, though he called them all Jesuits (a term that he, as a good 
Protestant, was ready no doubt to apply indiscriminately). In addition there 
travelled on board the Alceste a Roman guide and a young nobleman, Count 
Canizzaro, a friend of Drummond and thought by him a suitable person to 
keep His Holiness company.° 
The Alceste set sail on 26 August and after first calling at the island of Ponza 
to pick up a pilot who knew the area made for the coast at Fiumicino where 
one of the friars and the guide disembarked. The friar managed to reach 
Rome safely, had an interview with Cardinal Pacca, the papal secretary of 
state, and found the latter surprised and pleased to learn that an English ship 
had arrived. Although he looked like a Sicilian brigand, the friar’s credentials 
were believed, even though he failed to carry on him the important letter 
written (as requested) in the king’s own hand, unsigned and unaddressed, 
lamenting “the gradual decay of his friend’s health” and inviting him “to try a 
change of air at his villa which was accounted very healthy.”¢ 
No direct answer was apparently forthcoming, but the friar was told to go 
back to the coast and wait there for the next three nights for a messenger with 
the pope’s reply. 
Maxwell then arranged to have waiting on shore every night his first 
lieutenant, to receive the expected messenger. No one arrived on the first two 
nights and the third night it blew a gale, so that no boat could approach the 
shore. Whether the messenger did arrive on the crucial third night is not 
known, but once the weather again permitted, a Party was sent ashore each 
night to await the envoy who never came. Finally Captain Maxwell decided 
he must send someone again to Rome to find out what was happening. But 
here he found himself frustrated, since all the priests were too frightened to 
go and asked only to return to Palermo. Maxwell sent them back on a small 

<<<PDFPAGE 147>>>
Appendix B 
143 
ship (the Acorn) together with the young Count Canizzaro—the only one to 
show any courage—with a message to Drummond to inform him of events. 
Within a week Canizzaro was back, carrying with him on board the Acorn a 
Roman colonel by the name of Vanni who was nominated to go to Rome. 
Vanni set off but never returned, and Drummond learnt later from news- 
papers that he had been arrested and shot as a spy. Maxwell of course knew 
nothing of this and waited a further month off the Tiber, hoping to see the 
colonel return. The weather continued to worsen steadily and on only 
thirteen nights was it possible to put a boatload of men ashore. One night 
one of the launches was swamped and four of the sailors taken prisoner. 
Maxwell arranged an exchange of prisoners with the officer commanding 
Fiumicino fort, but when the time came to carry out this exchange the 
Neapolitan broke his word, and the first lieutenant of the Alceste and the party 
of men accompanying him ashore under flag of truce were marched off to the 
north. 
How long Maxwell continued to remain at his fruitless task we do not 
know. Collingwood, on receiving his dispatch, must have decided to order 
him back. Maxwell had written on 15 October of rapid currents and frequent 
gales, rendering “this service rather arduous.” One cannot envisage Colling- 
wood being prepared to run the risk of losing a frigate by continuing what 
was clearly a failed operation.” 
The version of the story as recounted in his memoirs by Cardinal Pacca, the 
pope’s pro-secretary of state, tells us a slightly different tale. According to 
him, the Franciscan friar whose disguise made him look like a vagabond or 
bandit did not carry any letter to authenticate either him or his mission. He 
had, he admitted, been too frightened to do so—thinking (correctly) that 
were he arrested and the king’s letter found on his person, he would certainly 
be shot as a spy—the fate that awaited Colonel Vanni (who did carry a letter) 
a few weeks later. The friar referred Pacca to Cardinal Erskine, whom the 
friar had apparently seen already. Erskine’s reaction, when tackled by Pacca 
about the plan as revealed by the friar was to say the least equivocal. He 
disclaimed all personal responsibility, said he had been told of it by Gabrielli, 
but, having then heard no more of it, had assumed the idea to have been 
abandoned. As Pacca notes, it would appear that Erskine had now become 
alarmed and was trying to dissociate himself entirely from a plot initiated by 
himself. 
When, the following day, Pacca saw the pope, the latter agreed that he 
knew of the plot, formulated by Gabrielli, but that he had never had the least 
intention of leaving Rome of his own free will. His reasons being, as Pacca 
adds, not merely the fear of provoking schism but his refusal to give Napo- 
leon a pretext for annexing the Roman states to France by allowing himself to 
become a guest in a country controlled by France’s enemies. Nor would he 
agree to contemplate flight merely in order to secure his person. 

<<<PDFPAGE 148>>>
144 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
In view of all this, why then did Pacca send the friar off with the idea that 
further contact would be made at the coast during the course of the next 
three nights, if he were already so sure that the pope had effectively vetoed 
the scheme from the start?® 
It must be very frankly stated that the clergy do not emerge from these 
incidents with any very great measure of credit. Why, it may be asked, did 
Gabrielli allow the scheme to go ahead, if he knew that the pope was 
completely opposed to it? Even to the extent of encouraging Fagan (it was 
presumably he who saw Fagan) to try again, but this time bearing a letter? 
And why did he not inform his successor as pro-secretary of state of what he 
had done? And if he were hurried out of the kingdom before he had had time 
to inform Pacca, why had Erskine said nothing to Pacca? And again, if Pacca 
was so convinced that the pope had made up his mind for good (and Pius was 
not a man to be shaken, once he had formed his own opinion), why did he 
allow the Franciscan friar to think there was still a chance of some change in 
the papal attitude toward escape? 
The result of what one can only call muddle, or perhaps even moral 
cowardice, in refusing to tell unpleasant truths, interwoven with clerical 
intrigue, was that the lives of all those on board the British frigate were 
needlessly risked, the life of the brave Colonel Vanni was forfeit, and the 
British were made for no good reason to hazard one of their valuable ships. 
The second attempt to rescue the pope was made in the summer of 1810 
when a ship was again dispatched from Sicily, though this time it was a 
Spanish operation, with the British ship acting as auxiliary only. There had 
been no orders sent out from England to instruct Amherst how he was to act, 
and he and Admiral Martin were content to rely on Canning’s dispatch of 
June 1808 as an indication of British policy. It is true that there was now a 
new administration, but Perceval’s ministry expressed no views, though it 
would doubtless have been delighted if the operation had been successful. 
This time the initiative had come from the pope’s almoner, acting (or so he 
wrote) on the pope’s instructions, and taking the form of letters to Sicily 
addressed to a Sicilian prelate and to the Spanish minister in Palermo. These 
letters, which arrived in Holy Week and whose contents at first appeared 
innocuous, were found to contain a hidden message. This message was 
written in invisible ink and asked the letter’s recipients to help rescue the 
pope and with the assistance of the British navy to arrange for his passage to 
and asylum in Malta. Given the known views of the pope, that he certainly 
had not become (as some think) Napoleon’s bitter enemy, but rather that he 
clung to the hope that the latter would become a latter-day Charlemagne and 
emerge in the end as the Church’s defender, it seems unlikely to say the least 
that he would have instructed his almoner to make the appeal set out in these 
letters.” In view of the independent behavior of Cardinals Erskine and 

<<<PDFPAGE 149>>>
Appendix B 
145 
Gabrielli during the summer of 1808 in formulating a plot to which they 
knew the pope was completely opposed, one may perhaps be allowed to 
doubt the truth of the papal almoner’s statement that his letters were written 
on the pope’s instructions. 
Padre Gil, the Spanish minister, told Amherst he would like His Holiness 
to be taken if possible to Alicante. The latter was a secure resort and the 
presence of the pope in Spain would have an incalculable effect on the 
national struggle the Spanish were waging. The Spanish had a corvette they 
could send, but they would welcome the presence of a British warship to 
accompany it to Genoese waters, since it had been learned that the coast near 
Savona was strictly guarded by French privateers. Amherst then approached 
Admiral Martin, who expressed his willingness to detach a brig (the Eclair) to 
perform the task. 
The two warships set sail in June, the Spanish warship having aboard 
someone who knew both the pope and Savona, and when they reached the 
Maddalena Islands, this person was sent on ahead of the rest to meet the pope 
and discover from him when the corvette had best appear. He was at the 
same time to offer the pope a choice of places to which he could go—first 
Alicante, second Minorca, and only Palermo as a last resort.!° 
Neither Amherst nor Admiral Cotton (now in command in the Mediterra- 
nean) ever had very much faith in the scheme, given the close watch main- 
tained on the coast. And Cotton later wrote of its failure as one that had been 
“foreseen and expected.”!! The person sent from the Maddalena Islands did 
not succeed in reaching the pope until it was too late to take any action, for 
the long delay had led the Spanish with British agreement to abandon the 
plan. They had thought that although they might succeed in making a 
landing near Savona, they would never be able to get away; and an unsuc- 
cessful attempt of this sort could only endanger the life of the pope. 
Amherst was convinced that lack of security had contributed largely to the 
failure of the mission. There was great reason to believe, he wrote, that the 
secret of the expedition had been divulged even before the two warships had 
set sail from Palermo roadstead.1? And the same had been true in 1808 
when, before the Alceste had sailed for the Tiber, its mission was being 
discussed in Malta.13 The court at Palermo was of course notorious for the 
presence of spies there and for careless talk. 
A further attempt in 1811 to involve the British government in an attempt 
to free the pope was made by Queen Maria-Carolina through a Livornese 
banker called Gaspar Cifenti, but that plan very rapidly came to nothing. 
Napoleon’s police got to know of the plot and Cifenti paid for it with his 
life +* 
Whether the British in 1812 had any intention of trying again to take the 
pope away from Savona it is not possible to say. There is no documentary 

<<<PDFPAGE 150>>>
146 
SICILY: THE INSECURE BASE 
evidence of it in the British Admiralty or Foreign Office files. But Napoleon 
certainly thought they had and that it was too dangerous to allow the pope to 
remain at Savona any longer, even though in the previous year its garrison 
had been reinforced. It was for this reason that Pius VII was removed to the 
safety of Fontainebleau.

8. Drummond had had the poorest opinion of Sicilian nobles as potential minis- 
ters. He told Fox in April 1807 that he had made some attempt to get Prince 
Belmonte included in the cabinet and went on, “But who is there in the country, with 
the exception of Belmonte (of whom I could not think favourably, from the many 
reports I have heard) who could have been brought into the Government? Pantelleria 
is Our avowed enemy, Cassaro is governed by Brissac, General Acton is hated by the 
whole country; Butera is shallow and without principles; Sambuca is too old; Trabea 
is a buffoon; Tommasi is a man of low birth, with great knowledge is capable of 
playing any game, and therefore, if he could be useful, he might be dangerous.” 
Drummond to Fox, 28 April 1807, Bunbury, Military Transactions, 117-18 and n.
